Lectures on entanglement, von Neumann algebras, and emergence of spacetime Preprint: MIT-CTP/5938
Hong Liu

Abstract
We review recent developments in the use of von Neumann algebras to analyze the entanglement structure of quantum gravity and the emergence of spacetime in the semi-classical limit. Von Neumann algebras provide a natural framework for describing quantum subsystems when standard tensor factorizations are unavailable, capturing both kinematic and dynamical aspects of entanglement. The first part of the review introduces the fundamentals of von Neumann algebras, including their classification, and explains how they can be applied to characterize entanglement. Topics covered include modular and half-sided modular flows and their role in the emergence of time, as well as the crossed-product construction of von Neumann algebras. The second part turns to applications in quantum gravity, including an algebraic formulation of AdS/CFT in the large- N N limit, the emergence of bulk spacetime structure through subregion-subalgebra duality, and an operator-algebraic perspective on gravitational entropy. It also discusses simple operator-algebraic models of quantum gravity, which provide concrete settings in which to explore these ideas. In addition, several original conceptual contributions are presented, including a diagnostic of firewalls and an algebraic formulation of entanglement islands. The review concludes with some speculative remarks on the mathematical structures underlying quantum gravity. Expanded from lectures at TASI 2023 (Boulder, June 2023), the New York University Workshop on Operator Algebras (August 2023), the Asian Winter School (Kyoto, December 2023), and the China-India-UK School in Mathematical Physics at ICMS (Edinburgh, June 2025).



August 10, 2026

I
Introduction and Motivations

Entanglement characterizes intrinsic quantum correlations, which have no classical counterparts.
While entanglement was already recognized in the early days of quantum mechanics by the EPR thought experiment
EinPod35
and Schrödinger
Sch35
, its importance in quantum many-body systems has only been understood since the 1990’s.
It now plays key roles in quantum information and quantum computations, condensed matter physics, and also in understanding quantum gravity. In the context of the AdS/CFT duality
Mal97
;
GubKle98
;
Wit98
, the Ryu-Takayanagi proposal
RyuTak06
;
RyuTak06-2
;
HubRan07
;
Wal11
;
EngWal14
;
LewMal13
;
FauLew13
strongly indicates that in a quantum gravitational system the spacetime structure is tied to the entanglement structure of the system.
While much has been learned through subsequent developments, a precise and complete understanding of the relationship between entanglement and spacetime structure remains elusive.

This lack of a precise relation reflects a deeper issue: we currently do not have a fully developed language for describing the quantum gravitational regime,
1
1

1

Throughout these lectures, we set
\hbar=1
. The quantum gravity regime is characterized by a finite Newton constant
G_{N}
, with the semiclassical limit corresponding to
G_{N}\to 0
.
where spacetime itself (e.g., the metric) is expected to undergo large fluctuations.

Geometric concepts fundamental to classical gravity and quantum field theory in curved spacetime—such as spacetime geometry, causal structure, notions of time, and local regions—are sharply defined only in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit. These notions typically serve as starting points in the search for a quantum theory of gravity—for example, in canonical quantization or gravitational path integral approaches—where the challenge is to understand how geometry becomes “fuzzy” as one moves away from the semiclassical regime.

Here, however, we adopt the opposite perspective: suppose we are given a complete theory of quantum gravity at finite
G_{N}
, in which conventional geometric structures are not defined a priori. Then how do such structures emerge in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit? What are the underlying physical and mathematical mechanisms responsible for the emergence of spacetime geometry, causal structure, and locality? In particular, what role does entanglement play in this emergence? Hopefully, answers to these questions will help guide us toward a formulation of the quantum gravitational regime.

This latter perspective is, in fact, the natural one in the context of the AdS/CFT duality, where quantum gravity in an asymptotically anti-de Sitter (AdS) spacetime is described by a conformal field theory (CFT) living on its boundary.
The boundary CFT contains a parameter
N
that characterizes the number of field degrees of freedom, and this parameter maps to a positive power of
1/G_{N}
on the gravity side. Consequently, the quantum gravity regime with a finite
G_{N}
corresponds to a CFT with a finite
N
, and the semi-classical
G_{N}\to 0
limit corresponds to the
N\to\infty
limit.
In this context, the questions posed in the previous paragraph become: How do the bulk spacetime and the associated geometric concepts (such as locality and causal structure) emerge from these CFT degrees of freedom in the
N\to\infty
limit?

It turns out that in the
N\to\infty
limit, the entanglement structures of a boundary CFT undergo profound changes, and these emergent entanglement features have been argued to give rise to bulk spacetime structures
LeuLiu21a
;
LeuLiu21b
;
LeuLiu22
.

The emergent entanglement structures in the
N\to\infty
limit cannot be captured by the standard formulation of entanglement, which defines subsystems through Hilbert space factorization.
In the standard formulation, we say that a subsystem
A
is entangled with its complement
{\overline{A}}
if the state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
of the combined system
L=A\cup{\overline{A}}
,

\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\sum_{n}c_{n}\lvert{\psi_{n}}\rangle_{A}\otimes\lvert{\chi_{n}}\rangle_{\overline{A}}

(1)

cannot be factorized into a simple product state of
A
and
\overline{A}
.
The tensor factorization,

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\overline{A}},

(2)

where
{{\mathcal{H}}}
,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{A}
, and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\overline{A}}
are the Hilbert spaces associated with
L
,
A
and
\overline{A}
respectively, is necessary for writing down (
1
). Without (
2
), entanglement cannot be defined.

However, as we will discuss in detail in Sec.
II.1
, the factorization of
\mathcal{H}
can break down when there is infinite entanglement between
A
and
\overline{A}
, making it impossible to define separate Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\overline{A}}
for
A
and
\overline{A}
.
As illustrated in Fig.
1
, in the
N\to\infty
limit, new forms of infinite entanglement emerge in the boundary CFT, giving rise to novel structural properties.

In situations involving infinite entanglement, the conventional approach has been to introduce regularizations to render entanglement measures (such as Rényi and entanglement entropies) finite. However, such regularizations can obscure entanglement structures that are intrinsically tied to the underlying physical system. To characterize entanglement in a more innate and universal manner, a generalized notion of subsystems is needed—one that goes beyond the traditional framework of Hilbert space factorization.
Fortunately, this is possible by drawing on another early development of quantum mechanics. It involves a shift in perspective: employing operator subalgebras to define and analyze subsystems.

Figure 1
:
Cartoon of entanglement structure of a CFT for a subregion
A
. There are two types of entanglement: short-range entanglement near the boundary
\partial A
(represented by short blue lines) due to local interactions there and long-range entanglement (represented by long red dashed lines). We assume that the CFT is put on a lattice, so that the former is regularized. In the
N\to\infty
limit, both types of entanglement become infinite as they are proportional to the number of field theoretical degrees of freedom, leading to new entanglement structures. In particular, as we will discuss in detail in this review, the divergence of the long-range entanglement is responsible for the emergence of local bulk physics and bulk causal structure
in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit.

In the late 1920s and early 1930s, von Neumann, while formulating the mathematical foundations of quantum mechanics, embarked on the task of classifying operator subalgebras for general quantum systems
Neu30
;
Neu36
. With Murray, he established the fundamental theory for what is now known as von Neumann algebras, including a classification scheme for them
MurNeu36
;
MurNeu37
. Over the years, von Neumann algebras have undergone extensive development and found numerous applications in both mathematics and physics. What has gone largely unappreciated for many years is that subsystems can, in fact, be defined in terms of von Neumann algebras, and that the structure of these algebras reflects the underlying entanglement structure of the system.
In particular, in these lectures we will elaborate on the following points:

1.

The classification of von Neumann algebras can be understood as a broad classification of entanglement types, i.e.,

\begin{bmatrix}\text{classification of}\cr\text{von Neumann algebras}\end{bmatrix}\quad{\leftrightarrow}\quad\begin{bmatrix}\text{broad classification of}\cr\text{types of entanglement}\end{bmatrix}\ .

(3)

2.

Beyond this, more sophisticated tools from von Neumann algebra theory provide access to significantly finer entanglement features than those captured by the broad classification (
3
).

We will utilize von Neumann algebras to describe the emergent entanglement structures of the large
N
limit of a boundary CFT and explain how such structures can be used to understand the emergence of bulk spacetime.
A key concept is called the subregion-subalgebra duality
LeuLiu22
, which equates an arbitrary causally complete bulk spacetime subregion with a specific type (type III
1
) of emergent von Neumann subalgebra of the boundary CFT.
See Fig.
2
(a) for illustrations.
The boundary subalgebra dual to a bulk subregion is emergent in the following sense:
(i) it can only be precisely defined in the
N\to\infty
limit; and (ii) its entanglement structure undergoes qualitative changes, such as a transition from a type I von Neumann algebra to type III
1
in the large-
N
limit.

The subregion-subalgebra duality opens new avenues for understanding the emergence of bulk locality, causal structure, and, more generally, spacetime itself
LeuLiu21b
;
LeuLiu22
.
It also provides novel approaches for defining bulk causal and event horizons in the stringy regime
GesLiu24
;
HerKud25
and fresh insights into spacetime connectivity
EngLiu23
.

The subregion-subalgebra duality reformulates entanglement wedge reconstruction
Van09
;
CzeKar12
;
CzeKar12b
;
Wal12
;
HeaHub14
;
AlmDon14
;
JafSuh14
;
PasYos15
;
JafLew15
;
DonHar16
;
Har16
;
FauLew17
;
CotHay17
and causal wedge reconstruction
BanDou98
;
Ben99
;
BalKraLaw98
;
HamKab05
;
HamKab06
;
KabLif11
;
Hee12
;
HeeMar12
;
PapRaj12
;
Mor14
;
Hub14
;
EngPen21a
;
Wit23
;
EngLiu25
in algebraic terms, extending them to arbitrary bulk subregions such as those shown in Fig.
2
(b). It also provides new insights
LeuLiu22
;
LeuLiu24
into features of entanglement wedge reconstruction, including its quantum error-correcting properties
AlmDon14
and the existence of islands
AlmMah19a
.

Figure 2
:
(a) Cartoon of subregion-subalgebra duality. A bulk subregion
O_{1}
is dual to a boundary subalgebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}
, which captures all bulk physical operations in
O_{1}
. Similarly bulk subregions
O_{2}
and
O_{3}

correspond to boundary algebras
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{2}
and
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{3}
, respectively. Bulk geometric and causal relations among these regions translate into algebraic relations among the corresponding algebras. For example,
O_{1}\subset O_{2}
becomes
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{1}\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}_{2}
. That
O_{1}
and
O_{3}
are spacelike separated is captured by
[{{\mathcal{A}}}_{1},{{\mathcal{A}}}_{3}]=0
, i.e., the bulk causal structure in one higher dimension is captured by the emergent boundary commutant structure in the large
N
limit. That these regions can be sharply defined in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit requires a specific bulk entanglement structure. This structure is captured by the type III
1
nature of the
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{i}
’s, which arises from the infinite long-range entanglement described in Fig.
1
.
(b) A causal diamond in the global AdS that does not touch the boundary is dual to an emergent subalgebra in the boundary system that does not have a geometric description.

It may sound surprising that a subalgebra of a certain type can serve as a dual description of a bulk spacetime region. Such relationships, however, have deep roots in modern mathematics. The Gelfand duality, for instance, asserts that the algebra of continuous functions on a topological space can be employed as a description of the space itself. This duality has been a starting point for defining noncommutative geometries using noncommutative algebras (see e.g.
Con95
).
The subregion-subalgebra duality shares a similar spirit and generalizes such relations. It posits that an abstract algebra in the boundary CFT (possibly without a boundary geometric interpretation) describes all the physical operations of a bulk gravity theory in a local spacetime region in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit. In other words, the bulk spacetime region may be considered as a geometrization of certain abstract algebra from the boundary theory.

Tools from the theory of von Neumann algebras have also opened up many other exciting explorations of AdS/CFT and more broadly quantum gravity, including the construction of quantum error correcting codes for embedding bulk physics into the boundary system

;
KanKol18

; understanding observables’ algebras

in low-dimensional quantum gravity theories such as Jackiw-Teitelboim gravity

;

;

;
constructing new models of quantum gravity and diffeomorphism invariant observables
Wit21b
;

;

; understanding the physical origin of generalized gravitational entropies, both for asymptotically AdS spacetimes and for spacetimes with other asymptotic structures
ChaPen22

The roles played by algebraic structures of the boundary theory in describing emergent bulk spacetime structures in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit, as well as generalized gravitational entropies, strongly suggest that operator algebras should play crucial roles at finite
G_{N}
as well, where conventional geometric notions are no longer applicable.
They offer hints about the mathematical structure of quantum gravity. We provide some perspectives on these aspects in the conclusion section.

Operator algebras have long been used to characterize relativistic quantum field theory, an approach known as algebraic QFT (see e.g.
Haa92
;
Ara99
;
Bor00
;
Yng04
;
HolWal08
;
HolWal14
;
Yng14
;
Wit18
;
Wit21a
for textbooks and reviews), to treat statistical systems (see e.g.
BraRobV2
;
Sew02
), and to extract quantum information (see e.g.
OhyPet
). More recently, they have also played important roles specifically in algebraic formulations and proofs of the Average Null Energy Condition (ANEC)
CasTes17
;
FauLei16
and the Quantum Null Energy Condition (QNEC)
BalFau17
;
CeyFau18
;
FauLi18
;
HolLon25
.
See also
Ges23b
;
FurLas23
;
OusFur23
;
GesSan24
for discussions of quantum chaotic hierarchies within the algebraic approach.

The use of von Neumann algebras to understand the AdS/CFT duality was pioneered by Rehren and collaborators in a series of early papers
Reh99a
;
Reh00
;
DueReh02a
;
DueReh02b
. Further pioneering contributions include the work of
PapRaj13b
, which connected black hole physics with Tomita-Takesaki modular theory, and of
Har16
;
KanKol18
;
Fau20
;
KanKol21
;
GesKan21
, which employed von Neumann algebras to construct toy models of holography as quantum error-correcting codes, and
Jef18
.

I.1
Plan of the article

This article contains two main parts:

1.

Sec.
II
—
V
: Entanglement and von Neumann algebras

This part introduces the relevant background on von Neumann algebras and elucidates how they can be used to capture entanglement.

Sec.
II
introduces the basics of von Neumann algebras, including their classification. Secs.
III
and
IV
then elaborate on how these algebras can be used to characterize entanglement. A central takeaway is that standard quantum information techniques are only applicable to type I von Neumann algebras, while many physically relevant situations require type II or type III algebras.
The tools for analyzing the entanglement structure of type III algebras are discussed in Sec.
IV
. There we introduce modular theory, a powerful framework for understanding entanglement in type III von Neumann algebras. We explain how it gives rise to an emergent notion of time and provides a way to further classify the entanglement structures of type III algebras. Among the various type III algebras, type III
1
is of particular importance, as local operator algebras in relativistic QFT are of this type. We also discuss half-sided modular flows, a powerful structure that leads to another notion of emergent time.
Finally, Sec.
V
introduces the crossed product construction for type III
1
algebras, which will play an important role in later discussions of quantum gravity models and in elucidating generalized entropies.

2.

Sec.
VI
—
IX
: Applications to AdS/CFT and quantum gravity

This part discusses recent applications of operator algebras to the emergence of spacetime structure and gravitational entropies.

Sec.
VI
develops the formulation of the AdS/CFT duality in the large-
N
limit using operator algebras. In Sec.
VII
, we introduce the notion of subregion-subalgebra duality, and reformulate entanglement wedge and causal wedge reconstruction in algebraic terms.
Sec.
VIII
is devoted to understanding how various geometric features of bulk spacetime—such as local regions, causal structure, notions of time, and spacetime connectivity—emerge from the boundary theory.

Sec.
IX
presents simple models of quantum gravity constructed from operator algebras. The first model, involving static observers, is used to clarify the physical origin of generalized entropies of black holes, static patch of de Sitter spacetime, and arbitrary bulk regions. The section then turns to a model of dynamical observers in de Sitter spacetime, which illustrates the emergence of cosmological horizons, and concludes with a discussion of the operator algebras of JT gravity.

Finally, Sec.
X
offers some speculations on the mathematical structure of quantum gravity.

I.2
Conventions and Notations

\bullet
The large-
N
limit (equivalently, the
G_{N}\to 0
limit) generally refers to the perturbative expansion in
1/N
, and does not necessarily mean only the leading-order term. By contrast, finite
N
(or finite
G_{N}
) refers to treating
N
(or
G_{N}
) non-perturbatively.

\bullet

{{\mathcal{H}}}
denotes a Hilbert space, and
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
the set of bounded operators acting on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
. In the context of field theory, we assume that any operator under consideration has been suitably regularized and is bounded.
{\mathbf{1}}
denotes the identity operator.

\bullet

\tilde{J}^{\pm}(Y)
denotes the causal future/past of a region
Y
in the conformal completion of the bulk spacetime. A prime on a region
Y
denotes its causal complement (with the bulk or boundary context understood), while a double prime denotes its causal completion.

\bullet
Suppose
R
is a subregion of a Cauchy slice. Its complement on the slice is denoted by
\bar{R}
, and its domain of dependence by
\hat{R}
. The same notation applies to both bulk and boundary subregions, with the context understood.

\bullet

\subset
always means proper subset.

II
Introduction to von Neumann algebras

In this section we discuss basic elements of von Neumann algebras and their classifications. In Sec.
III
and Sec.
IV
we discuss how they can be used to characterize entanglement structure of a quantum system. These sections are not meant as a comprehensive review of the theory of von Neumann algebras, which is a vast subject.
2
2

2

See
BraRobV1
;
TakV1
;
TakV2
;
TakV3
for textbooks. A recent very nice review for physicists is
Sor23a
.
It is our hope to convey the basic physical ideas with as little formal mathematical language as possible. In doing so, we may inevitably sacrifice some mathematical rigor. For example, we will often say “it can be shown” without giving a proof, and instead use heuristic arguments to convey the physical and mathematical intuitions behind a statement. Some of the proofs are simple, which can be filled in by readers without using significant extra mathematical machinery. In such cases we will often use the phrase “it can be
readily
shown” to encourage readers to give a try.

II.1
Systems with an infinite amount of entanglement

In this subsection, we motivate the operator algebraic approach to entanglement by discussing some simple examples where the standard quantum informational techniques prove inadequate.

In the standard treatment of entanglement of a quantum system (e.g. bi-partite entanglement), we
divide the system into two subsystems
R\cup L
and
assume that the full Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
can be factorized as
a tensor product of those of
R
and
L
, i.e.

{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}\ .

(4)

Suppose the system is in a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle

from which we can construct the reduced density operator for
R

\rho_{R}\equiv{\rm Tr}_{L}\left(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|\right)\ .

(5)

\rho_{R}
is the key object from which entanglement information of the system can be extracted through various entanglement measures.
Examples include the von Neumann entropy and Rényi entropies of
\rho_{R}
,

\displaystyle S_{R}=-{\rm Tr}_{R}\rho_{R}\log\rho_{R},\qquad S_{R}^{(n)}=-{1\over n-1}\log{\rm Tr}_{R}\rho_{R}^{n},\quad n=2,3,\cdots

(6)

and the relative entropy
between two such reduced density operators
\rho_{R}
and
\sigma_{R}
,

S(\rho_{R}||\sigma_{R})={\rm Tr}_{R}\rho_{R}(\log\rho_{R}-\log\sigma_{R})\ .

(7)

Figure 3
:
(a) A one-dimensional lattice gauge theory: dynamical variables
U_{ij}\in G

are assigned to each oriented link
ij
joining lattice points
i
and
j
, and gauge transformations are

U_{ij}\to V_{i}U_{ij}V_{j}^{\dagger}
with
V_{i},V_{j}\in G
, where
G
is the gauge group.
(b) Two copies of a system of
N
spins (blue dots) in an entangled state. Red dashed lines represent entanglement between pairs of spins.
(c) A spatial slice of a relativistic quantum field theory is separated into
R
and
L
regions by surface
x=0
. Dashed blue lines represent short-range entanglement near
x=0
.

There are, however, situations where the factorization of Hilbert space (
4
) does not exist, in which case (
5
)–(
7
) as well as many other quantum informational measures cannot be immediately defined.
Here are some examples:

Ex. 1:

Consider the lattice gauge system of Fig.
3
(a).
Variables
U_{ij}
are not gauge invariant. That physical states must be gauge invariant means that the Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
can cannot be factorized into
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
for
R
and
L
regions.

Ex. 2:

Consider two copies of a system of
N
spins, as indicated in Fig.
3
(b).
Suppose each pair of spins of
R
and
L
systems is in an entanglement state

\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle=\cos{\theta}\lvert{00}\rangle+\sin{\theta}\lvert{11}\rangle,

(8)

with the state for the full system given by

\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle=\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle_{1}\otimes\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle_{2}\otimes\cdots\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle_{N}\ .

(9)

For
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
the two spins in a pair are maximally entangled, so are the
R
and
L
systems.
3
3

3

As a result,
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
is the thermofield double (TFD) state of the
R
and
L
systems at an infinite temperature.

For a general
{\theta}\in(0,\pi/4)
, the two systems are entangled, but not maximally.

Now consider the “thermodynamic” limit
N\to\infty
. Since physical processes involve only finite energy, we restrict our attention to finite-energy excitations around the state (
9
), which can be completed into a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
.
For a generic Hamiltonian
4
4

4

Consider for example,
H_{R}=H_{L}=f\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z_{i}
where
Z_{i}
denotes Pauli matrix
\sigma_{z}
for
i
-th spin, where
H_{R},H_{L}
are respectively the Hamiltonians of
R
and
L
systems.
, any finite-energy excitation around the state (
9
) can flip only a finite number of spins. As a consequence:

(i) All states in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
possess an infinite amount of entanglement. Unentangling the

R
and
L
subsystems would require flipping an infinite number of spins—thus costing infinite energy.

(ii) The Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
cannot be factorized into separate Hilbert spaces for the

R
and
L
systems. Such a factorization would require the existence of product states with no entanglement between

R
and
L
, which are absent.

(iii) The states
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
corresponding to different values of
\theta

differ by an infinite amount of entanglement, and are therefore expected to belong to distinct Hilbert spaces.

In the
N\to\infty
limit, the
2^{2N}
-dimensional Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{N}
of the original system of
N
spin pairs is no longer relevant for the physics of interest, as it includes states with infinite energy. In fact, the resulting vector space has uncountable dimension and is not a separable Hilbert space.

Ex. 3:

Consider dividing a time slice of a
relativistic
QFT (RQFT), say a scalar field theory in a
d
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime, into
R
and
L
halves (see Fig.
3
(c)).
Across the surface
x=0
separating
R
and
L
regions, there are an infinite number of local degrees of freedom coupled together, which results
in an infinite amount of entanglement between
R
and
L
in
any finite energy state
5
5

5

Here, the energy corresponds to the Hamiltonian associated with Minkowski time, measured relative to the vacuum.
.
For example, when the short-distance cutoff
\epsilon
approaches zero (the continuum limit), the entanglement entropy of the
R
-region in a general state contains a universal divergent term given by

S_{R}=b\frac{\text{Area}(\partial R)}{\epsilon^{d-2}}+\cdots\ .

(10)

Similar to the entangled-spins example, the Hilbert space of the QFT cannot be factorized into those of
R
and
L
, as product states in such a factorized space would possess infinite energies.

Without factorization of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, the reduced density operator (
5
) for a subsystem cannot be defined, and the standard approach of extracting entanglement correlations of a system cannot be used.

We stress that the physics underlying the non-factorization of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
in the lattice gauge system of Ex. 1 is fundamentally different from that of Ex. 2 and 3. The former arises from gauge constraints. The latter is due to infinite entanglement from infinite number of degrees of freedom. This latter situation, which will be the main focus of these lectures, occurs generically in statistical systems in the thermodynamic limit and in QFTs.
6
6

6

Using the argument given earlier for the entangled spin example, it is expected that in generic situations, infinite entanglement prevents factorization of Hilbert space—states separated by infinite entanglement are expected to be separated by infinite energy barriers, and thus do not coexist in the same Hilbert space. Nevertheless, (fine-tuned) counterexamples exist, see Appendix
A
. Below in Sec.
II.3
, we will discuss a precise criterion for when factorization exists.

As we will see in later discussions, the treatments of these two situations differ significantly: the lattice gauge system requires only modest modifications to the standard procedure, while Ex. 2 and 3 demand fundamental changes in perspective and new theoretical tools.

The usual strategy for dealing with systems with an infinite amount of entanglement, such as those of Ex. 2 and Ex. 3, is to introduce a regulator
\epsilon
such that the amount of entanglement becomes finite, and as a result
{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\epsilon}={{\mathcal{H}}}^{\epsilon}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\epsilon}_{L}
, where
{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\epsilon}
denotes the Hilbert space in the presence of regulator
\epsilon
.
In the entangled spin examples, this can be done by first calculating quantum informational quantities for a finite
N
(whose Hilbert space clearly has a factorization structure), and then taking
N\to\infty
at the end of the calculation. In a QFT, the system can be put on a lattice, with the short-distance cutoff
\epsilon
in (
10
) being the lattice spacing.
While the entanglement entropies or other quantum informational measures become divergent when the regulator is removed, sometimes finite values that are independent of regularization procedure can be extracted. Powerful techniques, such as the replica trick, have been developed to calculate various regularized entanglement measures and to extract from them regularization-independent quantities.
Many important results and insights have been obtained this way.

However, introducing a regulator risks obscuring physics inherent to the continuum or thermodynamic limit. As we will see in later sections, this is indeed the case. Divergences often serve as reminders that the appropriate tools or language have not yet been employed. To intrinsically characterize entanglement in such situations, it is necessary to generalize the definition of a subsystem beyond Hilbert space factorization. Below we will demonstrate that von Neumann algebras provide both the language and the powerful tools needed to capture the structure of entanglement in these contexts.

II.2
What is a von Neumann algebra

II.2.1
Von Neumann algebras as subsystems

Consider a quantum system with Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, with
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
denoting the set of all bounded operators.
For technical simplicity, we will always restrict our discussion to bounded operators, whose actions on the Hilbert space are continuous.

Suppose we have only partial access to the system, i.e. we have access to only a subset of operators of the system, the collection of which will be denoted as
{{\mathcal{M}}}\subset{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
.
{{\mathcal{M}}}
contains all manipulations we can perform to either extract information from or to influence the system. For example, given a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
, we can obtain expectation values
{\left\langle{\Psi|A|\Psi}\right\rangle}
and perform operations
B\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, with
A,B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

We will assume that
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is closed
under operator products, additions, and hermitian conjugate (i.e. if
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
,
A^{\dagger}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
), which means that it is a
*
-subalgebra
of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
.
In addition to being closed, it is also useful to have a notion of completeness, i.e. the limit of a convergent sequence of operations in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
should lie in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

There are multiple ways to define the convergence of a sequence of operators. Bounded operators on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
have a well-defined norm
||\cdot||
, which satisfies

||O^{\dagger}O||=||O||^{2}\ .

(11)

We say a sequence of operators
\{A_{n}\}\equiv\{A_{1},A_{2},\cdots\}

is
norm convergent
if there exists an operator
A
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}||A_{n}-A||=0
.
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is complete with respect to norm convergence if
\{A_{n}\}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
implies
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and such an algebra is called a
C^{*}
algebra
.

A different notion of completeness can be introduced by using instead matrix elements.
We say a sequence of operators
\{A_{n}\}=\{A_{1},A_{2},\cdots\}
is
weakly convergent
if there exists an operator
A
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\left\langle{\xi|A_{n}|\eta}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\xi|A|\eta}\right\rangle}
for
all
\lvert{\xi}\rangle,\lvert{\eta}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
. If
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is complete under weak convergence, i.e. a weakly convergent sequence
\{A_{n}\}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
implies
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, it is called a
von Neumann algebra
.
Since norm convergence implies weak convergence, a von Neumann algebra is a
C^{*}
algebra, but the converse is not true.

C^{*}
algebras can in fact be defined abstractly without a Hilbert space as long as a norm satisfying (
11
) exists.
But the definition of von Neumann algebra requires a Hilbert space.

Since matrix elements are, in principle, physically observable, requiring weak convergence is often more appropriate in most physical situations. For this reason, we will primarily focus on von Neumann algebras.

Now, instead of using tensor factorization of the Hilbert space to define a subsystem, we define a subsystem using a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, i.e.,

\text{subsystem}\quad=\quad\text{von Neumann algebra}\ .

(12)

{{\mathcal{M}}}
represents the collection of all possible physical operations that can be performed on a subsystem, closed under Hermitian conjugation, operator products, and weak convergence, providing an operational and more physical definition of a subsystem.
We define the
complement
of a subsystem—specified by a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
—in terms of its commutant
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
, which is the set of operators that commute with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, i.e.,

{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}=\{B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}),BA=AB,\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\}\ .

(13)

We will see that the definition (
12
) includes the factorization definition (
4
) as a sub-case, but is also applicable to non-factorized situations.

For the rest of this section, we discuss basic properties of von Neumann algebras and their classification, establishing the necessary background for the examination of their entanglement properties in Sec.
III
and Sec.
IV
.

II.2.2
Basic properties

Despite seemingly small differences in the definitions of von Neumann and
C^{*}
algebras, their properties differ significantly.
A fundamental statement about von Neumann algebra is von Neumann’s double commutant theorem which says that a von Neumann algebra satisfies
7
7

7

For a general
*
-algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, it follows immediately from the definition of commutant that
{{\mathcal{M}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
.

{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime\prime},

(14)

where
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
means taking the commutant twice. Equation (
14
) is a highly remarkable and powerful result, as it converts the completeness under weak convergence, which is a topological statement, into an algebraic statement. Operationally, checking whether
{{\mathcal{M}}}
coincides with its double commutant is in general simpler than checking the completeness condition.

It can be readily shown that for a
*
-subalgebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}

{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime\prime\prime},

(15)

so the commutant of a
*
-subalgebra is always a von Neumann algebra. From
{{\mathcal{A}}}
we can also construct a von Neumann algebra by taking its double commutant

{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime\prime}\ .

(16)

It can be readily shown that
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is the smallest von Neumann algebra containing
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}
.

Consider two von Neumann algebras,
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1},{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
. Denote the smallest von Neumann algebra containing
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
as
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\vee{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
and the
largest von Neumann algebra contained in
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
and in
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
as
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\wedge{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
. We then have

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\vee{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}=({{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\cup{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2})^{\prime\prime},\quad{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\wedge{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}\ .

(17)

There is no need to have double commutant on the right hand side of the second equation as it can be readily seen that the intersection of two von Neumann algebras is a von Neumann algebra.
Also note that

({{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}\vee{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2})^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}^{\prime}\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}^{\prime}\ .

(18)

The center of
{\mathcal{Z}}({{\mathcal{M}}})
of a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is defined as the collection of elements of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
that commute with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, i.e.,

{\mathcal{Z}}({{\mathcal{M}}})={{\mathcal{M}}}\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}\ .

(19)

A von Neumann algebra is called
primary
or
a factor
if it has a trivial center, i.e.
{\mathcal{Z}}({{\mathcal{M}}})={\mathbb{{C}}}{\mathbf{1}}
, or equivalently

{{\mathcal{M}}}\vee{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
. A general von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be written as direct sums (integrals) of factors, so below we will mostly concentrate on factors.

II.2.3
Weights and states

In quantum mechanics, a key characterization of an operator is its expectation values in states of interest.
For
C^{*}
and von Neumann algebras, the definition of a state does not require introducing vectors or density operators in a Hilbert space and can be
formulated abstractly.

A
linear functional

\omega
on a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a continuous map:
\omega:{{\mathcal{M}}}\to{\mathbb{{C}}}
satisfying

\omega(aA+bB)=a\omega(A)+b\omega(B),\quad\omega(A^{\dagger})=(\omega(A))^{*},\quad a,b\in{\mathbb{{C}}},\;A,B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(20)

A
weight

\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a linear functional that satisfies in addition to (
20
),

\omega(A^{\dagger}A)\geq 0,\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(21)

Equations (
20
)–(
21
) imply that
a weight
\omega
satisfies the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

|\omega(A^{\dagger}B)|^{2}\leq\omega(A^{\dagger}A)\omega(B^{\dagger}B)\ .

(22)

A weight
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is called a
trace
if it further
satisfies

\omega(AB)=\omega(BA),\quad\forall A,B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(23)

The trace of an operator does not have to be finite.
A
positive linear functional
is a weight with
\omega({\mathbf{1}})
finite.
A
state
is a weight with

\omega({\mathbf{1}})=1\ .

(24)

That is, a state is properly normalized.
A
tracial state
is a trace with
\omega({\mathbf{1}})=1
. We can interpret
\omega(A)
as the expectation value of
A
in
\omega
.

A
normal
state is defined as a state
\omega
satisfying the condition
8
8

8

\sup
denotes the supremum.

\sup\omega(A_{i})=\omega(\sup A_{i})

(25)

where
\{A_{i}\}
is a bounded sequence of
positive
operators in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. There exists a partial ordering among positive operators, i.e.
A_{i_{1}}>A_{i_{2}}
if
A_{i_{1}}-A_{i_{2}}
is a positive operator. Equation (
25
) means that the partial orderings of positive operators are preserved by the expectation values in the state
\omega
.
It can be shown that a state is normal if and only if there exists a density operator
\rho
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
with

\omega(A)=\mathop{\rm Tr}(\rho A),\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(26)

From now on we will only consider normal states.

A state
\omega
is
faithful
, if
\omega(A^{\dagger}A)=0
implies that
A=0
.

For two states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
, and some
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

\omega=\lambda\omega_{1}+(1-\lambda)\omega_{2}

(27)

is again a state. If
\omega
can be written in the form (
27
), we say
\omega

dominates

\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
.
An
\omega
which cannot be written as (
27
) is called a
pure
state.

II.2.4
C^{*}
and von Neumann algebras generated by a single operator

To get some intuition for the norm and weak completeness used to define respectively a
C^{*}
algebra and a von Neumann algebra, here we consider the simplest example of such algebras.
Consider a bounded Hermitian operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}
. We denote the
C^{*}
and von Neumann algebras generated by
{{\mathcal{O}}}
(i.e. the algebras built from
{{\mathcal{O}}}
alone) respectively by
{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
.
Since
{{\mathcal{O}}}
commutes with itself, these are Abelian algebras.
Both
{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
should include polynomials of
{{\mathcal{O}}}
, i.e., operators of the form
\sum_{n=0}c_{n}{{\mathcal{O}}}^{n}
for
c_{n}\in{\mathbb{{C}}}
, but can contain additional elements
arising from the norm and weak completeness.

Denote the spectrum of
{{\mathcal{O}}}
as
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
, which is a compact subset of
{\mathbb{{R}}}
.
9
9

9

Recall that the spectrum
\sigma(A)
for an operator
A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
is the set of complex numbers
\kappa
for which
A-\kappa{\mathbf{1}}
is not invertible. For a bounded Hermitian operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}
,
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
is a closed compact subset of
{\mathbb{{R}}}
.

It can be shown
10
10

10

See e.g. Theorem A.8 in Appendix A of
Sor23a
.
that
{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
is isomorphic to the algebra of
continuous
functions on
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
.
That is, for any continuous function

f(\kappa):\kappa\in\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})\to{\mathbb{{C}}}
, there exists some operator
f({{\mathcal{O}}})\in{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
. In contrast, it can be shown
11
11

11

See e.g. Theorem A.11 in Appendix A of
Sor23a
.
that
{{\mathcal{M}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
is isomorphic to the algebra of
bounded
functions
12
12

12

More precisely, bounded measurable functions up to equivalence on sets of measure zero.
on
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
.
Since continuous functions on a compact set are bounded, the bounded functions include continuous functions as a subset. Thus,
{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
is a subset of
{{\mathcal{M}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
.

In particular, the von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
includes all the projection operators in the spectral decomposition of
{{\mathcal{O}}}
, while
{{\mathcal{A}}}({{\mathcal{O}}})
in general does
not
include them. Such projection operators
correspond to indicator functions on
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
, which are in general discontinuous.
For example, consider the projection operator
P_{I}
that projects to a
subset
I
of
\sigma({{\mathcal{O}}})
, the corresponding indicator function for
I
is

f_{I}(\kappa)=\begin{cases}1&\kappa\in I\cr 0&\kappa\not\in I\end{cases}\ ,

(28)

and
P_{I}=f_{I}({{\mathcal{O}}})
.

II.2.5
Projections

The discussion of Sec.
II.2.4
implies that a von Neumann algebra can be considered as being spanned by its
projection operators (or simply projections). More explicitly, for a Hermitian operator
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}
includes the von Neumann algebra generated by
A
, and thus contains all the spectral projections of
A
. That is,
A
can be expanded in terms of projections of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. A (non-Hermitian) operator
B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be written as
B=B_{R}+iB_{I}
, where
B_{R}={{\frac{1}{2}}}(B+B^{\dagger})
and
B_{I}={1\over 2i}(B-B^{\dagger})
are Hermitian and are in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
(as
B^{\dagger}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
by definition). Thus
B
can also be expanded in terms of projections of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

Projections are familiar objects in quantum mechanics. Recall that there is a one-to-one correspondence between projections and closed subspaces of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
:
a projection
P
projects to the subspace
P{{\mathcal{H}}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{H}}}
, and conversely, for any closed subspace of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
we can associate a projection. If
P\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, we say the subspace
P{{\mathcal{H}}}
belongs to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. Heuristically, this is a subspace that observers who control
{{\mathcal{M}}}
have access to.
The correspondence between projections and closed subspaces induces a partial ordering on the projections. We say
13
13

13

Alternatively it can be defined as
P\leq Q\Leftrightarrow Q-P\;\text{is a positive operator}
.

P\leq Q\quad\text{if}\quad PQ=P,

(29)

which means that
P{{\mathcal{H}}}\subseteq Q{{\mathcal{H}}}
. Clearly, the largest projection is the identity
{\mathbf{1}}
. For two projections
P
and
Q
,

P\land Q
denotes the projection onto
P{{\mathcal{H}}}\cap Q{{\mathcal{H}}}
, and
P\lor Q
is the projection for the closure of
the span of
P{{\mathcal{H}}}\cup Q{{\mathcal{H}}}
.
The orthocomplementation
\bot
is defined as
P^{\bot}={\mathbf{1}}-P
.
If
P,Q\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, then
P\land Q
,
P\lor Q
,
P_{\perp}
are also in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. That is, projections in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
form a complete
lattice with respect to the operations
\land,\lor
.
14
14

14

A lattice is a partially ordered set in which every pair of elements has a least upper bound and a greatest lower bound. A complete lattice extends this property to every subset, not just pairs of elements.

Given the fundamental role of projections, the properties of a von Neumann algebra—and indeed its classification—can be characterized in terms of the properties of its projections.
Two characterizations of projections will play important roles: minimal and finite projections.

The notion of minimality is straightforward to define: a projection

P
is said to be
minimal

in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
if it is nonzero and
{{\mathcal{M}}}
contains no projection
Q<P
.

The definition of finiteness is more subtle. Consider, for example, the projection

P=\lvert{\psi}\rangle\langle{\psi}\rvert\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}

(30)

on a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
, with
\lvert{\psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
the identity on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
. Suppose
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
is infinite dimensional. While
P
may be an infinite-rank projection in the full Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
,
it is a finite projection of rank one when considered within the subspace
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
—that is, within the subalgebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
. This example illustrates that the notion of finiteness depends not solely on its rank but on the algebraic context in which the projection is considered. We therefore require a notion of finiteness that reflects the intrinsic properties of a projection within an algebra itself.

For this purpose, we first introduce the notion of equivalence: two projections
P,Q\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
are called
equivalent

with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, denoted as
P\sim Q
, if there is an operator
V\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that

P=V^{\dagger}V,\quad Q=VV^{\dagger}\ .

(31)

This means that there exists a partial isometry
V
that
maps the subspace
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}=P{{\mathcal{H}}}
isometrically onto the subspace
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}=Q{{\mathcal{H}}}
.
A nonzero projection
P
is called
finite
if it is not equivalent with a smaller projection.
Conversely,

P
is called
infinite
if there exists a projector
Q\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that
Q\sim P
and

Q<P
.

Under this definition, (
30
) is a finite projection in the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
.

For a von Neumann factor
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, the identity operator
{\mathbf{1}}
is the maximal projection.
Depending on the nature of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, this projection can be either finite or infinite. If

{\mathbf{1}}
is finite, then every projection in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
must also be finite; in this case,

{{\mathcal{M}}}
is called a
finite von Neumann factor
. Conversely, if the identity is infinite, we say that

{{\mathcal{M}}}
is an
infinite von Neumann factor
. It can be shown that in such cases, any two infinite projections are equivalent, and in particular, every infinite projection is equivalent to the identity
{\mathbf{1}}
.

II.3
Classifications of von Neumann factors

We now turn to the classification of von Neumann factors, based on the properties of their projections.

The dimension of a projection
P

is usually defined as
\mathop{\rm Tr}P
, where
\mathop{\rm Tr}
is the trace of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
. The value of
\mathop{\rm Tr}P
gives the dimension
of the subspace
P{{\mathcal{H}}}
. For example,
P=\lvert{\chi}\rangle\langle{\chi}\rvert
has dimension
1
, while
P=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert{\chi_{i}}\rangle\langle{\chi_{i}}\rvert
with
{\left\langle{\chi_{i}|\chi_{j}}\right\rangle}={{\delta}}_{ij}
has dimension
n
. The dimension as defined is given by an
integer, which can be infinite. But this dimension is in general inadequate as it does not reflect intrinsic properties
of a projection in an algebra. In the example (
30
),
\mathop{\rm Tr}P={\rm dim}\,{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
, which is infinite. However,
from the perspective of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
—or equivalently the algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
—it is a rank-one projection.
Therefore, to characterize the dimension of a projection
P
in a von Neumann algebra, we need a definition that reflects intrinsically its nature in the algebra.

The equivalence relation (
31
) together with the partial ordering (
29
) of projections implies that we can introduce a dimension function
d(P)
in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that it is positive for any nonzero finite projection and satisfies

d(P)<d(Q)\quad\text{for}\quad P<Q,\quad\text{and}\quad d(P)=d(Q)\quad\text{for}\quad P\sim Q\ .

(32)

For an infinite projection
P
, then there exists a
Q\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that
d(Q)<d(P)
and
d(P)=d(Q)
at the same time, which means
d(P)
and
d(Q)
have to be infinite,

d(P)=\infty,\quad P:\;\text{infinite projection}\ .

(33)

Such a dimension function can be used to demonstrate the existence of a
normal and faithful
trace
\mathop{\rm tr}(\cdot)

on the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
,
with

\mathop{\rm tr}P=d(P),\quad\forall P\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(34)

and this trace is unique up to an overall normalization (as
d(P)
is only defined up to a scaling).
The trace
\mathop{\rm tr}(\cdot)
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
generally differs from the standard Hilbert space trace
\mathop{\rm Tr}
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, and, as we will see below, can be understood as a kind of “renormalized” trace.

In the example of (
30
) on
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
, since
P
is a rank-1 projection on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
, we can define
d(P)=1
and the corresponding trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
can be defined as

\mathop{\rm tr}(\cdot)={\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}}(\cdot)

(35)

by simply stripping off the factor
{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
in (
30
), as all operators in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
has such a trivial factor.

We are now ready to discuss the classification of a von Neumann factor
{{\mathcal{M}}}
:

1.

Type I:

{{\mathcal{M}}}
contains nonzero minimal projection(s).

Using the scaling freedom of
\mathop{\rm tr}
(or
d(P)
), we can normalize
\mathop{\rm tr}
such that
d(P_{0})=\mathop{\rm tr}P_{0}=1
for a minimal projection
P_{0}
. In this case, it can be further shown that all the projections can be built from superpositions of minimal projections, and

d(P)
is given by a positive integer.

Depending on the value of
n=d({\mathbf{1}})
, a type I factor can be further classified as

I
n
:

d(P)=\{0,1,...,n\}
, where
n
is a natural number.

(36)

I
∞
:

d(P)=\{0,1,\cdots,n,\cdots,\infty\}
.

(37)

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
for a separable Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
is trivially type I: it is type
I_{n}
if
{{\mathcal{H}}}
is
n
-dimensional, and is type
I_{\infty}
for an infinite-dimensional
{{\mathcal{H}}}
. In this case,
\mathop{\rm tr}
is just the standard trace
\mathop{\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}
of
{{\mathcal{H}}}
.

Since the trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
normalized to unity for minimal projections and all dimensions in (
36
)–(
37
) are integers, we expect that
\mathop{\rm tr}
should have the usual interpretation of “counting” states.
Indeed, it can be shown that for a type I factor
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, there exists a factorization of the Hilbert space

{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
such that

{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L},\quad\mathop{\rm tr}={\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}},\quad{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{1}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})\ .

(38)

Conversely, if
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be written in the form (
38
), it is clearly type I.

Thus, Hilbert space factorization exists if and only if the von Neumann algebra associated with a subsystem is a type I factor. This provides a precise mathematical criterion for determining when Hilbert space factorization is well-defined. Consequently, the conventional notion of a subsystem defined via Hilbert space factorization is a special case of the more general algebraic formulation given in (
12
).

2.

Type II:

{{\mathcal{M}}}
contains finite projections, but does not contain any minimal projection.

In the absence of minimal projections, equation (
32
) implies that the dimension function
d(P)

can take arbitrarily small values and spans a continuous range beginning at zero. This allows us to assign a
real
number as the dimension of the Hilbert subspace
P{{\mathcal{H}}}
, in contrast to the discrete dimensions familiar from finite-dimensional settings.

Depending on whether
d({\mathbf{1}})
is bounded or not, we can subdivide type II factors into:

(a)

II
1
:
d({\mathbf{1}})
is bounded. We can normalize the trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
such that

d({\mathbf{1}})=\mathop{\rm tr}{\mathbf{1}}=1

(39)

and thus

d(P)\in(0,1]
.

(b)

II
∞
:
d({\mathbf{1}})
is unbounded. In this case,
d(P)\in(0,\infty]
.

3.

Type III
: All nonzero projections are infinite.

In this case, equation (
33
) implies
d(P)=\infty,\forall P\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
. Therefore, a meaningful dimension function and trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
do not exist. New conceptual and mathematical tools are required to describe and classify such algebras—these will be discussed later in Sec.
IV
.

Type II and III algebras may appear unintuitive, but we will see they arise naturally in many physical contexts, including the entangled spin example and quantum field theory discussed in Sec.
II.1
.

The definitions of type II and III algebras have some immediate implications. For example, it can be shown that the absence of a minimal projection for type II and III factors implies that such an algebra does not admit a pure state (recall the definition below (
27
)).
This can be intuitively understood in terms of lack of factorization of Hilbert space in such situations.
When there is factorization of Hilbert space, as in (
38
), a pure state on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
exists, corresponding to a product state in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
(i.e., an unentangled state). Non-existence of a pure state on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can thus be interpreted as non-existence of a product state. (Recall that in the examples of entangled spins and quantum field theory of Sec.
II.1

all states in the Hilbert space are infinitely entangled).

For type III algebras, that all projections are infinite implies for any projection
P\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
there exists an isometry
W\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that

W^{\dagger}W={\mathbf{1}},\qquad WW^{\dagger}=P\ .

(40)

This has some interesting consequences. For example, given any state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, we can use the isometry operation
W
to construct a new state

\omega_{W}
which is an eigenstate of
P
,
15
15

15

We say a state
\varphi
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is an eigenstate of a projection
P
if

\varphi(P)=1
.

\omega_{W}(A)\equiv\omega(W^{\dagger}AW)\quad\to\quad\omega_{W}(P)=1\ .

(41)

The ‘‘local’’
16
16

16

Here “local” simply refers to that it belongs to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
isometry operation
W
does not affect the commutant of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, with
\omega_{W}(A^{\prime})=\omega(A^{\prime})
for
A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
.

II.4
“Emergent” Hilbert space and von Neumann algebra: GNS construction

The definition of von Neumann algebras requires a Hilbert space as its completeness is defined in terms of matrix elements. In contrast,
C^{*}
algebras can be defined without a Hilbert space as only a norm is needed. In this subsection we discuss a construction where a Hilbert space and thus von Neumann algebras can emerge from a
C^{*}
algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and a state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
. This construction will play important roles in later discussions.

The basic result is that given a
C^{*}
algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and a state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, it is possible to construct a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
and a representation
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
acting on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
(i.e., an operator
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
is represented by an operator
\pi_{\omega}(A)\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})
). Heuristically,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
can be viewed as being obtained by “acting” elements of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
on
\omega
. Given the algebra
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})\subseteq{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})
, there is a natural von Neumann algebra associated with it by taking
the double commutant in
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})
(or equivalently weak closure in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
),

{{\mathcal{M}}}=\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})^{\prime\prime}\ .

(42)

That is, from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
\omega
, there emerges a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
and a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

Below we first give the abstract construction of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
and
\pi_{\omega}
, which is called the Gelfand-Naimark-Segal (GNS) construction, and then consider a simple example.

For
A,B\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
, we can define an inner product as

{\left\langle{A|B}\right\rangle}\equiv\omega(A^{\dagger}B),\quad{\left\langle{A|A}\right\rangle}\geq 0,\quad{\left\langle{B|A}\right\rangle}=\omega(B^{\dagger}A)=(\omega(A^{\dagger}B))^{*}={\left\langle{A|B}\right\rangle}^{*},

(43)

where the last two equations follow respectively from the positivity of
\omega
and (
20
).

This does not yet define a Hilbert space as there may be elements
X\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
with
\omega(X^{\dagger}X)=0
.
Denote
{\mathcal{J}}\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}
to be set of all such elements. Note that
if
X\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then

AX\in{\mathcal{J}},\qquad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\ .

(44)

To see (
44
), we have from (
22
)

0\leq\omega((AX)^{\dagger}(AX))=\omega(X^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}AX)\leq\left(\omega(X^{\dagger}X)\omega((A^{\dagger}AX)^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}AX)\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}=0\ .

(45)

Similarly,
\omega(XB)=0
and
\omega(AX)=0
, for any
A,B\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
It then follows that

{\left\langle{A|B}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{A+X_{1}|B+X_{2}}\right\rangle}
with

X_{1,2}\in{\mathcal{J}}
. Accordingly, we can define an equivalence relation

A\sim A+X,\qquad A\in{{\mathcal{A}}},\quad\forall X\in{\mathcal{J}}

(46)

and

{\left\langle{A|A}\right\rangle}=0,\quad{\rm iff}\quad A\sim 0\ .

(47)

Denote the equivalence class of
A
as
[A]
. The set of
[A]
, which is the quotient
{{\mathcal{A}}}/{\mathcal{J}}
,
has a positive norm for any nonzero element.

The completion of
{{\mathcal{A}}}/{\mathcal{J}}
then defines a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
.
Denote
\lvert{C}\rangle
as the vector associated with
[C]
. The representation
\pi_{\omega}(A)
of
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
can be defined as

\pi_{\omega}(A)\lvert{C}\rangle=\lvert{AC}\rangle,\quad\forall C\in{{\mathcal{A}}}

(48)

which satisfies by definition

\pi_{\omega}(A)\pi_{\omega}(B)=\pi_{\omega}(AB)\ .

(49)

The vector
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle
for the equivalence class
[{\mathbf{1}}]
of the identity is special, and we introduce a special notation for it

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\equiv\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle\ .

(50)

It then follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{\omega}(A)\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\lvert{A}\rangle,\quad A\in{{\mathcal{A}}},

(51)

\displaystyle\omega(A)={\left\langle{\Omega|\pi_{\omega}(A)|\Omega}\right\rangle}\ .

(52)

Thus
\omega
is represented by the state vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
.

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
has the property that the set
\{\pi_{\omega}(A)\lvert{\Omega}\rangle,A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\}
is dense in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
as by definition

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
is the completion of
\{[A],A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\}
. A vector with such property is called
cyclic
with respect to
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
, and a representation of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
with a cyclic vector is called a
cyclic representation
.
Thus the trio
({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}}),\lvert{\Omega}\rangle)
provides a cyclic representation for
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

It can further be shown that the representation
({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}}),\lvert{\Omega}\rangle)
is
unique
up to unitary equivalence.
Properties of
\omega
affect the nature of the representation
({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}}),\lvert{\Omega}\rangle)
, and it can be readily shown that:

Proposition II.1
.

The representation
({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}}),\lvert{\Omega}\rangle)
is irreducible iff
\omega
is pure, i.e.,

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})=(\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}}))^{\prime\prime}\ .

(53)

Proposition II.2
.

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is separating with respect to
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
if and only if
\omega
is faithful.

We define a vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
to be
separating
with respect to an algebra, if
A\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\neq 0
for any nonzero element
A
of the algebra. When
\omega
is faithful, the set
{\mathcal{J}}
is trivial, i.e.
\omega(A^{\dagger}A)\neq 0
for any nonzero
A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and the separating property of
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
then follows from (
52
).

To get some intuition on the GNS construction, it is instructive to consider a simple example.
Suppose
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is the matrix algebra
M_{n}
of
n\times n
matrices, and take
\omega
to be

\omega(A)\equiv\mathop{\rm Tr}(\rho A),\quad A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}=M_{n}

(54)

where
\rho
is a positive-definite Hermitian
n\times n
matrix with unit trace and
\mathop{\rm Tr}
is the matrix trace. Clearly,
\omega
satisfies the conditions for a state given in Sec.
II.2.3
. Suppose
\rho
has rank
m
with
1\leq m\leq n
.
From our definitions around (
27
), when
m=1
,
\omega
corresponds to a pure state, while
\omega
is a
faithful state for
m=n
, i.e., for an invertible
\rho
.

The constructions of the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
and the representation
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
are straightforward and are left as an exercise. Here we merely state the results (up to unitary equivalence). We find
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{n}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{m}
where
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{m}
is the Hilbert space of
m
complex vectors, and
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
and
\lvert{A}\rangle
corresponding to
[A]
can be written as

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\rho_{a}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\lvert{a}\rangle_{R}\lvert{a}\rangle_{L},\quad\lvert{A}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{a=1}^{m}A_{ia}\rho_{a}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\lvert{i}\rangle_{R}\lvert{a}\rangle_{L}\ .

(55)

In the above equations we have labelled basis vectors of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{n}
with a subscript
R
, while those of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{m}

with a subscript
L
.

\{\rho_{a},\,a=1,\cdots,m\}
are the nonzero eigenvalues of
\rho
,
\{\lvert{i}\rangle,i=1,\cdots n\}
is the basis where
\rho
is diagonalized, and
A_{ij}
are the matrix elements of
A\in M_{n}
in that basis. The representation of
A
in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
is

\pi_{\omega}(A)=A\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{m}\ .

(56)

From (
56
), the action of
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
is irreducible only for
m=1
. This is consistent with Proposition
II.1
and equation (
53
). For
m=n
,
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is both cyclic and separating with respect to
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
, consistent with Proposition
II.2
.

This simple example also illustrates another important application of the GNS construction. Here

{{\mathcal{A}}}
can also be viewed as a von Neumann algebra acting on the Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{n}
spanned by
n
complex vectors, and
\rho
is a density operator on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{n}
. For
m>1
,
\rho
is not pure, and
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
provides a canonical purification of
\rho
(the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
is larger than the Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{n}
we start with).

More generally, consider a von Neumann algebra algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and a normal state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Using the GNS construction we can construct a GNS Hilbert space

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
in which
\omega
is represented by a vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
. Thus
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
provides a purification of
\omega
. When
\omega
is faithful,
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to
\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})
.

II.5
Emergent von Neumann algebras of the entangled spin example

Consider the entangled spin example of Sec.
II.1
in the
N\to\infty
limit. There we mentioned that for a generic Hamiltonian, the space of finite-energy excitations around the state (
9
) forms a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
. With the GNS construction, we can now give an explicit description of this Hilbert space.

For this purpose, consider the algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
of finite-energy operations, which consists of operators of the form

\displaystyle O={\alpha}_{1}\otimes{\alpha}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\alpha}_{n}\otimes\cdots,

(57)

\displaystyle\text{all but finitely many of the ${\alpha}_{i}$ are equal to ${\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}$},

where
{\alpha}_{i}
is an operator acting on
i
-th spin pair and
{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
is the identity operator acting on a single spin.
The requirement that only a finite number of
{\alpha}_{i}
can be nontrivial (not equal to the identity) comes from that finite energy processes can only flip a finite number of spins from
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
. In the
N\to\infty
limit,

O
has a well-defined norm descended from the finite
N
system.
Upon completion in terms of norm convergence,
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is then a
C^{*}
-algebra.

\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
defines a state
\omega_{\theta}({{\mathcal{A}}})
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}
as

\omega_{\theta}(O)={\left\langle{\Phi_{\theta}|O|\Phi_{\theta}}\right\rangle},\quad O\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\ .

(58)

Using the GNS construction of Sec.
II.4
, a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
can be obtained from
{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
\omega_{\theta}
. Heuristically,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
can be understood as obtained from acting
{{\mathcal{A}}}
on
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
, i.e., the completion of the set of states obtained from
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
by flipping a finite number of spins.
In this case, (
42
) is simply
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}})
, the set of bounded operators on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
.

Suppose we have access only to the
R
-system. The subalgebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}})
consisting of operators acting on the right spin system, completed under weak convergence on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
, is then a von Neumann algebra. We can similarly define a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
consisting of operators of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}})
acting on the left spin system. Since actions on the left and right spins commute, we should have

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\prime}\ .

(59)

In Sec.
III.3
and
IV.3
we will discuss entanglement between
R
and
L
systems using the structure of
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
for different
{\theta}
.

The entangled spin example is a bit special as there is a complete symmetry between the
R
and
L
spin systems. We can also obtain
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
using the algebra of the
R
-system alone. Instead of (
57
), consider the algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}
consisting of finite-energy operations on the
R
-systems, i.e. operators of the form

A=a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes\cdots,\quad\text{all but finitely many of the $a_{i}$ are equal to ${\mathbf{1}}_{2}$},

(60)

where
a_{i}
is an operator acting on the
i
-th spin in the
R
-system. With the norm descended from the large
N
limit,
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}
is again a
C^{*}
-algebra, and
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
defines a state
\omega_{\theta}
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}
as

\omega_{\theta}(A)={\left\langle{\Phi_{\theta}|A|\Phi_{\theta}}\right\rangle},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}\ .

(61)

Note that
\omega_{\theta}
is faithful state of
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}
, but not
{{\mathcal{A}}}
. Now using the GNS construction, we can build a GNS Hilbert space from
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}
and
\omega_{\theta}
, and it can be readily shown that the Hilbert space coincides with
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
.
17
17

17

To have some intuition on this, consider a single spin pair. Acting on
\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle
using operators of the right spin system can generate all the states from acting on
\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle
using operators of the left spin.
The von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
defined earlier can be thought as the von Neumann algebra corresponding to
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{R}

obtained through (
42
).

III
Von Neumann algebras and entanglement: type I and II

After giving a brief introduction to von Neumann algebras, we now proceed to discuss how they can be used to capture entanglement. As discussed earlier, a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
defines a subsystem, and vice versa.
We will show that properties of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
reflect the entanglement structure of the subsystem.
In particular, we will see type II and III algebras provide new perspectives and tools for dealing with systems with infinite amount of entanglement.

A key difference between a type I or type II algebra and a type III one is that there exists a trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
associated with the algebra, which has important implications. In this section we discuss how to capture entanglement for type I and II algebras. Type III algebras are discussed in the next section.

III.1
Density operators for type I and II algebras

Suppose the system is in a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
When a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
has a trace, it is possible to define a density operator
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
associated with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
for
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
More explicitly, define
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
as the operator satisfying the equation

\mathop{\rm tr}(A\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}})={\left\langle{\Psi|A|\Psi}\right\rangle},\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(62)

Given the positive properties of
\mathop{\rm tr}
, equation (
62
) has a unique solution
18
18

18

It is important that
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
belongs to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
, and
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is normalized and positive, thus is indeed a density operator.
19
19

19

The definition (
62
) applies to a general state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
(which from (
26
) corresponds to a general density operator
\rho
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
), with the replacement of
{\left\langle{\Psi|A|\Psi}\right\rangle}
by
\omega(A)
. Properties (
20
)–(
21
) and (
24
) of
\omega
implies that the resulting
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is normalized and positive.

We stress that the definition (
62
) of
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
does
not
require factorization structure (
4
).
We can interpret
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
as the reduced density operator of
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
with respect to the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and use it to calculate entropies and other quantum informational measures.
For example, the entanglement entropy
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is defined as

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}\equiv-\mathop{\rm tr}\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}\log\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(63)

The interpretation of
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
as a reduced density operator may seem puzzling at first sight, as the definition of (
62
) is very different from (
5
). In particular, since we have only access to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, in what sense does
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
capture the entanglement of the system? We will now answer this question based on the classification of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, which also shows how different types of von Neumann algebras capture different entanglement structures.

III.2
Type I algebras

III.2.1
Type I factor

For
{{\mathcal{M}}}
being a type I factor, as mentioned earlier, there exists a factorization of the Hilbert space

{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
such that

{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L},\quad\mathop{\rm tr}={\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}},\quad{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{1}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})\ .

(64)

If
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}
is an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space with
n
finite, then
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type I
n
, otherwise it is I
∞
.

Given a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, it can be readily seen that the definition (
62
) in fact coincides with (
5
):
using (
5
) the RHS of (
62
) is simply

{\left\langle{\Psi|A|\Psi}\right\rangle}={\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}}(\rho_{R}A),\quad\text{for all }A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(65)

Comparing (
62
) with (
64
)–(
65
), we conclude that
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}=\rho_{R}=\mathop{\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\langle{\Psi}\rvert
,
showing that (
62
) gives an equivalent description of the reduced density operator and hence captures exactly the same quantum entanglement information.

Remarks:

1.

We stress that the two approaches to obtaining the reduced density operator are conceptually very different. In (
5
) we need the knowledge of the global state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
for the whole system,
including the part in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
which we cannot access.
It is striking that using (
62
) we can deduce
\rho_{R}
and hence all the entanglement information
encoded in it using only data concerning
{{\mathcal{M}}}
(expectation values of operators in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
), without any knowledge of the complement. That is, quantum information of
\rho_{R}
is fully encoded in the structure of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

2.

That the trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
can be associated with a Hilbert space implies that it can be used to “count” states. Thus the corresponding entropy can be given a statistical interpretation.

III.2.2
General type I

Now suppose
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type I, but with a nontrivial center. It can be shown that there exists a decomposition

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}=\oplus_{\alpha}{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\alpha},\quad{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\alpha}=\left({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R_{\alpha}}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L_{\alpha}}\right),

(66)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{M}}}=\oplus_{\alpha}\left({{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R_{\alpha}})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L_{\alpha}}\right),\quad{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}=\oplus_{\alpha}\left({\mathbf{1}}_{R_{\alpha}}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L_{\alpha}})\right)\ .

(67)

A trace on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be defined as a sum of the trace in (
64
) for each factor in the decomposition
20
20

20

such that minimal projections again have dimension
1
,

\mathop{\rm tr}A=\sum_{\alpha}{\rm tr}_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}\equiv\sum_{\alpha}{\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R_{\alpha}}}A_{\alpha},\quad A=\sum_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L_{\alpha}}),\;A_{\alpha}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R_{\alpha}})\ .

(68)

Now consider a state
\rho
(which can be pure or mixed) on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, which from (
66
) can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\rho=\oplus_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha},\quad{\rm Tr}_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}=1,\quad\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}=1,\quad p_{\alpha}\in[0,1]

(69)

where
\rho_{\alpha}
is a density operator on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\alpha}
and
{\rm Tr}_{\alpha}
denotes the trace of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\alpha}
.
Equations (
68
) and (
62
) (with expectation value replaced by that in
\rho
) then give

\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}=\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}(\rho_{R{\alpha}}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L_{\alpha}}),\quad\rho_{R{\alpha}}={\rm Tr}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L_{\alpha}}}\rho_{\alpha}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R_{\alpha}})\ .

(70)

From (
63
), the entanglement entropy of the subsystem
{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the state
\rho
is then given by

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}\equiv-\mathop{\rm tr}\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}\log\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}=-\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}\log p_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}S_{\alpha},\quad S_{\alpha}=-{\rm tr}_{\alpha}\rho_{R{\alpha}}\log\rho_{R{\alpha}}\ .

(71)

The entropy consists of two parts, one part from the “statistical” entropy of
\{p_{\alpha}\}
, and the other part from weighted sum of
S_{\alpha}
.

In this case the non-factorization is rather mild, and the algebraic formulation (
62
) of the density operator gives a unified treatment
of both the factorized and non-factorized case. An example of the non-factorized type I case is the lattice gauge theory of Fig.
3
(a). The non-factorization due to gauge constraints can be understood in the algebraic language in terms of that the corresponding algebra is not a factor (see e.g.
CasHue13
).

III.3
Type II algebras

Now suppose
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a type II factor. To illustrate its entanglement structure, we consider an explicit example of
type II
1
algebra, which is provided by the entangled spin system of Sec.
II.1
with
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
. As discussed there, in the
N\to\infty
limit, the physical Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
of finite energy excitations cannot be factorized, due to infinite entanglement.
In Sec.
II.5
we discussed the construction of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
and introduced the von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
for operations on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
in the
R
-system.
Below we show that for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
is type II
1
, and use this example to illustrate physical interpretations of the reduced density operator
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and entanglement entropy
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}

introduced in (
62
)–(
63
).

III.3.1
Trace for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}

We first show that for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
, when
R
and
L
systems are maximally entangled,
it is possible to define a trace on
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
as follows,

\mathop{\rm tr}A\equiv{\left\langle{\Phi_{{\theta}={\pi\over 4}}|A|\Phi_{{\theta}={\pi\over 4}}}\right\rangle},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(72)

To see that (
72
) satisfies all the properties of a trace, note:

1.

Suppose
a
is an operator acting on the right spin of a 2-spin system

{\left\langle{\phi_{\pi\over 4}|a|\phi_{\pi\over 4}}\right\rangle}={{\frac{1}{2}}}{\rm Tr}_{2}a

(73)

where
\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle
was defined in (
8
) and
\mathop{\rm Tr}_{2}
is the standard matrix trace on
2\times 2
matrices.

2.

From (
9
) and (
60
) we have

{\left\langle{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}|A|\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\right\rangle}={1\over 2^{k}}{\rm Tr}_{2}a_{i_{1}}\cdots{\rm Tr}_{2}a_{i_{k}}

(74)

where
a_{i_{\alpha}},{\alpha}=1,\cdots,k
denote those
a
’s in
A
that are not equal to the identity.

3.

Since the expectation value of an operator in
\Phi_{\pi\over 4}
reduces to products of traces of
2\times 2
matrices, we then have

{\left\langle{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}|AB|\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}|BA|\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\right\rangle}

(75)

which establishes (
72
) as a trace.

III.3.2
Type II
1
for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}

Now consider projections in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. The maximal projection is the identity operator

{\mathbf{1}}\equiv{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\cdots,

(76)

and other examples include

P_{1}={\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes P_{\uparrow}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes\cdots,\quad P_{2}=P_{\uparrow}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes P_{\uparrow}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{2}\otimes\cdots,\quad\cdots,\quad P_{\uparrow}\equiv\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\cr 0&0\end{matrix}\right)\ .

(77)

We can similarly write down more projections by changing any finite number of
{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
to a two-dimensional projection.
