Clearly there is no minimal projection, as taking any projection
P
, we can always construct a projection
\tilde{P}
smaller than
P
by changing one of
{\mathbf{1}}_{2}
in
P
to
P_{\uparrow}
. Thus
{{\mathcal{M}}}
should be type II.

{{\mathcal{M}}}
is in fact type II
1
, as the identity has a finite dimension

d({\mathbf{1}})=\mathop{\rm tr}{\mathbf{1}}=1

(78)

which is already normalized. For those in (
77
),

d(P_{1})=\mathop{\rm tr}P_{1}={{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad d(P_{2})={1\over 4},\quad\cdots

(79)

By considering superpositions of orthogonal projections like those in (
77
) and the limits of such superpositions
we can construct
P
with
d(P)
given by any real number between
0
and
1
.

The existence of trace (
72
) comes from the right hand side of (
73
) being a trace, which only happens for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
. For
{\theta}\neq{\pi\over 4}
, a sensible trace cannot be defined, and we will see in Sec.
IV.3
the corresponding
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type III.

For a vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Phi_{\pi/4}}
, we can use the trace (
72
) and (
62
)–(
63
) to define a reduced density operator and the entanglement entropy. Consider, for example,

\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\eta_{n}\otimes\cdots,\quad\eta_{i}=\lvert{\phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle\;\;\text{except for $i=i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}$},\quad k>0

(80)

for which

\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)=2\rho_{1}\otimes 2\rho_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes 2\rho_{n}\otimes\cdots,

(81)

where
\rho_{i}
is the reduced density operator corresponding to
\eta_{i}
in the two-spin system.
The factors
2
come from the
{{\frac{1}{2}}}
factor on the right hand side of (
73
).
Except for
i=i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}
,
2\rho_{i}={\mathbf{1}}_{2}
. Note that since expectation values of
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
define
the trace on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, i.e. it is a tracial state, we have

\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Phi_{\pi\over 4})={\mathbf{1}}\ .

(82)

From (
81
), we can then have the following entanglement entropy

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)=\sum_{s=1}^{k}S_{2}(\rho_{i_{s}})-k\log 2<0

(83)

for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, where
S_{2}(\rho_{i_{s}})
is the von Neumann entropy for
\rho_{i_{s}}
in the two-spin system.
Equation (
83
) can also be written as

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(S_{2}(\rho_{i})-S_{2}({\mathbf{1}}_{2}/2)\right)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(S_{R}^{(N)}(\Psi)-S_{R}^{(N)}(\Phi_{\pi\over 4})\right)

(84)

where
S_{R}^{(N)}(\Psi)
denotes the entanglement entropy for
R
if we truncate the system to
N
pair of spins.
Equation (
84
) says that
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
can be understood as the difference between the entanglement entropy of
R
in
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
with that of the maximally entangled state
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
, although neither
S_{R}^{(N)}(\Psi)
nor
S_{R}^{(N)}(\Phi_{\pi\over 4})
has a sensible
N\to\infty
limit.
Using (
82
) we also find

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)=-S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi||\Phi_{\pi\over 4})=-\mathop{\rm tr}\left[\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)(\log\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)-\log\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Phi_{\pi\over 4}))\right],

(85)

i.e. the von Neumann entropy for
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
is equal to the negative of the relative entropy with respect to

\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
.

We can thus interpret
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
as a “renormalized” density operator that captures the entanglement structure on top of the infinite entanglement background provided by
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
, and
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
as a “renormalized” entropy relative to the maximally entangled state
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
.
The normalization of
\mathop{\rm tr}
specified in (
72
) and (
78
) give

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}=0
for the maximally entangled state
\lvert{\Phi_{\pi\over 4}}\rangle
.
We should caution that equation (
84
) has to do with the simple form of state (
80
). For a general vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, we may not be able to write
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
as such a difference, but (
85
) always holds.

Similar interpretations can be given to
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi)
for a type II
∞
algebra except in this case there does not exist a preferred way to normalize the trace, and the entropy is only defined up to a state-independent constant.

In contrast to the type I case, for type II factors the trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
does not count states in some Hilbert space.
This explains that why in (
83
) and (
85
), the entropy is negative even though
\rho
is positive definite and normalized to have trace
1
. In type I algebras, an entropy may be given a statistical interpretation as counting states, while in type II it measures a certain “distance” from the maximally entangled state.

Here we only discussed type II factors. Type II algebras that are not factors can be expressed as direct sums (or direct integrals) of factors and the discussion is similar to that of Sec.
III.2.2
.

III.4
Summary

From the above discussion of type I and type II algebras, we see that the type of a von Neumann algebra determines the general entanglement structure of all states in the physical Hilbert space. For a type I factor, the Hilbert space can be tensor factorized, meaning that any state can be expressed as a superposition of product states. In particular, pure states on the algebra (as defined below (
27
)) correspond to unentangled product states in the Hilbert space.

For a type II factor
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, the Hilbert space does not admit a tensor factorization, meaning that any state in the Hilbert space is necessarily entangled between the subsystems described by
{{\mathcal{M}}}
and its commutant
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
; no product state exists.
Correspondingly,
{{\mathcal{M}}}
does not allow a pure state, as can be seen as follows.

A state
\omega
for type II algebra can be represented by a density operator
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, which can be decomposed in terms of projections in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. However, since a type II algebra does not have a minimal projection,
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}

can always be written as a convex combination,
\lambda\rho_{1}+(1-\lambda)\rho_{2}
, with
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
density operators in
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. As a result, the corresponding state
\omega
is always expressible as a nontrivial convex combination of other states:
\omega=\lambda\omega_{1}+(1-\lambda)\omega_{2}
,
for some states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
on
\mathcal{M}
. This implies that no state on
\mathcal{M}
is pure, in contrast to the situation in type I algebras.

While each state in the Hilbert space has its own specific entanglement correlations, the structure of the algebra determines the general pattern of entanglement for all states.

IV
Von Neumann algebras and entanglement: type III

For type III algebras, there is no trace. Therefore, (
62
) cannot be applied, and
it is not possible to define a density operator
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}
(even in the renormalized sense) associated with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
for a state.
In this section we discuss how to capture their entanglement structure.
A key idea is that entanglement can manifest physically through a “local”
temperature
.
Heuristically, when a subsystem
A
is highly entangled with its complement
\bar{A}
, ignorance of
\bar{A}
leads to thermal behavior for observables in
A
.
A precise mathematical formulation of this intuitive picture is given by Tomita-Takesaki theory, which we discuss first.
We will see that type III algebras can be further divided into distinct subtypes. In the entangled spin system, the operator algebras
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
defined in Sec.
II.5
correspond to different type III subtypes for each
\theta\in(0,\pi/4)

Pow67
;
AraWoo68
. The algebra associated with a subregion in a quantum field theory (example 3 in Sec.
II.1
) belongs to yet another subtype of type III
Ara64
;
Lon82
;
Fre84
. These distinctions reflect different underlying entanglement structures.

IV.1
Emergent times from entanglement: modular flows

To motivate the description of entanglement structure for type III algebras, we first return to the type I case (
4
) and reformulate the characterization of entanglement there using a slightly different language.
Instead of studying the von Neumann and Rényi entropies (
6
) of the reduced density operator
\rho_{R}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
, we consider

\rho_{R}={\rm Tr}_{L}\left(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|\right)\equiv e^{-K_{R}},\quad K_{R}\equiv-\log\rho_{R}\ .

(86)

The information contained in
S_{R}
and
S_{R}^{(n)}
is fully captured by the spectrum of
K_{R}
(known as the entanglement spectrum), which provides a much more detailed characterization of the entanglement structure than the entanglement entropy or any finite set of Rényi entropies alone.
21
21

21

Exploring the entanglement spectrum of condensed matter systems has been very fruitful and is a large field of research (e.g., the first paper
LiHal08
investigating it has been cited 1907 times to this day).

K_{R}\geq 0
itself is referred to as the entanglement Hamiltonian.

The spectrum of
K_{R}
can also be probed by flow generated by
K_{R}
,

A(s)=e^{iK_{R}s}Ae^{-iK_{R}s}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}),\quad A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\ .

(87)

The flow acts within the system—specifically, the flow of an operator acting on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}

remains in the
R
-system. For an observer in the

R
-system using the flow parameter

s
as a notion of “time,” the system appears to be in a thermal state (
86
) with inverse temperature
\beta=1
. That is, correlation functions of flowed operators should satisfy the Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) relations, which are characteristic of thermal equilibrium.

Similarly, we can introduce
K_{L}=-\log\rho_{L},\;\rho_{L}={\rm Tr}_{R}\left(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|\right)
and study the flow generated by it for operators in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
.
In fact, we can treat the
R
and
L
systems together by introducing
22
22

22

Below
K_{R}
should be understood as
K_{R}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L}
, where for notational simplicity we always suppress the identity part. Similarly with
K_{L}
.

{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}\equiv\rho_{R}\otimes\rho_{L}^{-1},\quad-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}=K_{R}-K_{L},

(88)

that acts jointly on both subsystems, with the flow (
87
) and its counterpart in the
L
subsystem written as

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(A)\equiv{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}),\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}),\;\;s\in{\mathbb{{R}}},

(89)

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(A^{\prime})\equiv{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A^{\prime}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}),\quad\forall A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})\ .

(90)

{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
is called the
modular operator
, the action
\sigma_{s}

modular flow
, and
s
the
modular time
.

The fact that in a pure state
S_{R}=S_{L}
and
S_{R}^{(n)}=S_{L}^{(n)}
—a consequence of that
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
have the same set of eigenvalues—also has a nice reformulation in terms of
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
. Specifically,
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
leaves
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
invariant,

{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-1}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad(K_{R}-K_{L})\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=0\ .

(91)

This implies that the modular flow generated by
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
is a symmetry of the state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.

It should be noted that the definitions of
K_{R}
and
K_{L}
, and the modular operator
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
given in (
88
) are well-defined only when the reduced density matrices
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}

are full-rank (i.e., invertible). This implies that the Hilbert spaces
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
have the same dimension (which may be infinite), and in turn ensures the existence of a correspondence between states and operators in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
. In particular, it becomes possible to define a “swap” operator
J_{\Psi}
that exchange the two subsystems. Suppose
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
has the Schmidt decomposition
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\sum_{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{n}}\lvert{n}\rangle_{R}\otimes\lvert{n}\rangle_{L}
. Then, in the basis
\{\lvert{n}\rangle\}
,
for a state
\lvert{\phi}\rangle=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{mn}\lvert{m}\rangle_{R}\otimes\lvert{n}\rangle_{L}\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
,
we define
J_{\Psi}
by

J_{\Psi}\lvert{\phi}\rangle=\sum_{m,n}\phi^{*}_{mn}\lvert{n}\rangle_{R}\otimes\lvert{m}\rangle_{L},

(92)

where
J_{\Psi}
is
anti-unitary
and satisfies

J^{2}_{\Psi}=1\ .

(93)

It can be readily checked acting with
J_{\Psi}
on an operator in
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L}
yields an operator in
{{\mathcal{M}}}={\mathbf{1}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})
.
J_{\Psi}
is known as the
modular conjugation operator
.

The spectrum of
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
, the flows it generates (
89
)–(
90
), and the existence of the modular conjugation operator
J_{\Psi}
, together provide a powerful framework for characterizing the entanglement structure of the system.
Importantly, it is this modular structure—rather than the reduced density matrices
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
themselves and the associated entanglement entropies—that admits a generalization to the type III case, where
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
are no longer defined.

To describe the generalization to type III situations, we need to rephrase the condition that
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}

are full-rank using language that does not rely on the existence of these density matrices. The relevant concepts are cyclicity and separating properties, which were introduced in Sec.
II.4
.
Recall that a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is
is said to be
cyclic
with respect to an algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
if the set
\{A\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\}
is dense in the Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
.
It can be readily checked that the condition that
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
are full-rank is equivalent to the statement that
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is cyclic with respect to both
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{L}
and its commutant
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{1}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})
. The condition can then be rephrased as requiring that
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
be cyclic with respect to both
{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
.
Moreover, it is a simple exercise to show that a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is cyclic with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
, if and only if it is separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
23
23

23

Recall that a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is
separating
with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
if
A\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\neq 0
for any nonzero
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
Therefore, the condition can be expressed entirely in terms of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
:
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
This provides another manifestation of the idea that the entanglement structure of a bipartite system is fully encoded in the properties of the algebra associated with one side.

Tomita-Takesaki
theory says that for a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
with a cyclic and separating
vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, then:

1.

There exists a
positive
modular operator
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
leaving
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
invariant

{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,

(94)

and
K_{\Psi}\equiv-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
generates unitary automorphisms (modular flows) for
{{\mathcal{M}}},{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(A)\equiv e^{iK_{\Psi}s}Ae^{-iK_{\Psi}s}={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(95)

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}(A^{\prime})\equiv e^{iK_{\Psi}s}A^{\prime}e^{-iK_{\Psi}s}={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A^{\prime}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},\quad\forall A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}\ .

(96)

2.

There exists an anti-unitary modular conjugation operator
J_{\Psi}
, with the properties

\displaystyle J_{\Psi}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad J_{\Psi}=J_{\Psi}^{-1}=J_{\Psi}^{\dagger},\quad J_{\Psi}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}J_{\Psi}={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-1},

(97)

\displaystyle J_{\Psi}{{\mathcal{M}}}J_{\Psi}={{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},\quad J_{\Psi}{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}J_{\Psi}={{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(98)

3.

The vector

{{\Delta}}^{-is}_{\Psi}A\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}

(99)

can be analytically continued into the strip
{\rm Im}\,s\in(0,{{\frac{1}{2}}})
, and

{{\Delta}}^{-i(t+{i\over 2})}_{\Psi}A\lvert{\Psi}\rangle={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-it}J_{\Psi}A^{\dagger}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\ .

(100)

4.

Correlation functions of modular flowed operators

f_{AB}(s)\equiv{\left\langle{\Psi|\sigma_{s}(A)B|\Psi}\right\rangle},\quad A,B\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(101)

can be analytically continued into the strip
{\rm Im}\,s\in(-1,0)
in the complex
s
-plane, and satisfy the KMS relations

f_{AB}(s)=f_{BA}(-s-i)\ .

(102)

Some remarks:

(a)

Physically, Tomita-Takesaki theory says that, given a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
a cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, there is an “emergent” modular time evolution that operates within
{{\mathcal{M}}}
or
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}

and leaves
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
itself invariant. In particular, with respect to the modular time, observers who have access to only
{{\mathcal{M}}}
or
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
feel at a finite temperature with inverse temperature
\beta=1
.

(b)

While for type I factors the cyclic and separating condition is rather stringent—requiring
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
to have the same dimension—in type III situations of physical interest, such as quantum field theory and statistical systems, we will see that this condition is often naturally satisfied.

(c)

Tomita-Takesaki theory is usually established (see e.g.
BraRobV1
) by first defining an anti-linear operator
S_{\Psi}
, known as the Tomita operator, with the properties:

\displaystyle S_{\Psi}A\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=A^{\dagger}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad S_{\Psi}^{\dagger}A^{\prime}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=A^{\prime\dagger}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{M},\forall A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime},

(103)

\displaystyle S_{\Psi}^{2}=1,\quad S_{\Psi}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\ .

(104)

One then introduces
J_{\Psi}
and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
respectively as the unitary and self-adjoint part of the polar decomposition of
S_{\Psi}
,

S_{\Psi}=J_{\Psi}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\ .

(105)

The statements 1-4 above on
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
and
J_{\Psi}
, and the KMS relations can then be proved from (
103
).

(d)

It can be shown that if there exists an operator that generates automorphisms (
95
)–(
96
) of the algebras, and satisfies the KMS condition (
102
), then it must be the modular operator for
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle

TakV2
(see
Sor23b
for a recent discussion). This statement can be used to find the modular operator.

(e)

For
{{\mathcal{M}}}
being a type II factor, we can define density operators
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}},\rho_{{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}}
for
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi},K_{\Psi}
can still be defined using (
88
). If
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is a tracial state,
\rho_{{\mathcal{M}}}=\rho_{{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}}={\mathbf{1}}
, then it follows that
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}={\mathbf{1}}
.

(f)

For a type III factor,
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
and
K_{\Psi}
cannot be split as in (
88
).

To conclude this discussion, we mention a useful lemma
Fau25
that reflects an “ergodic” property of modular flows.

Lemma IV.1
.

Consider two von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{Y}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}
where
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is jointly cyclic and separating for both algebras. Let
\sigma_{s}
denote the modular flow associated with
{\mathcal{X}}
. Then
{\mathcal{X}}
can be generated by acting with
\sigma_{s}
on
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

IV.2
Classifications of type III factors

The existence of the modular operator and modular flows provide powerful tools for characterizing type III algebras.
In particular, properties of modular flows can be used to further classify type III factors.

Firstly, modular flows can be used distinguished type III from type I and II factors. It can be shown that:

Modular flows
\sigma_{s}({{\mathcal{M}}})
for all
s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}
are inner automorphisms of
{{\mathcal{M}}}

(106)

if and only
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type I or type II.

(107)

We say
\sigma_{s}
is an
inner automorphism
if there exists a unitary operator
U_{s}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that

\sigma_{s}(A)\equiv{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}=U_{s}^{\dagger}AU_{s},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(108)

For type I and type II factors, the modular operators can be split as in (
88
) and thus modular flows
are inner automorphisms with
U_{s}=e^{-iK_{R}s}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
for all
s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}
.
The statement (
107
) means that for a type III algebra, there should exist some
s
such that
\sigma_{s}({{\mathcal{M}}})
are not inner automorphisms.

The modular operator
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
and the corresponding flows
\sigma_{s}^{\Psi}
depend on
the specific cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
. For a different cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, we have the corresponding
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
\sigma_{s}^{\Omega}
. It is natural to wonder whether there are relations between these modular flows. Indeed, there are.
It can be shown that they are related by
inner
automorphisms of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
More explicitly, there exists a one-parameter unitaries
u_{\Psi\Omega}(s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
that relate the actions of
\sigma^{\Omega}_{s}
and
\sigma^{\Psi}_{s}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}^{\Psi}(A)=u_{\Psi\Omega}(s)\sigma_{s}^{\Omega}(A)u_{\Psi\Omega}^{\dagger}(s),\quad\forall s\in{\mathbb{{R}}},\;\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(109)

\displaystyle\text{i.e.}\quad{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}=u_{\Psi\Omega}(s)({{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{is})u_{\Psi\Omega}^{\dagger}(s)\ .

(110)

From (
109
) it follows immediately that
u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)
should satisfy the chain rule

\displaystyle u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)u_{\Omega\Phi}(t)=u_{\Psi\Phi}(t)\ .

(111)

Similarly, there exists a one-parameter family of unitaries
u^{\prime}_{\Psi\Omega}(s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
relating modular flows of
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
generated by
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
.

Now denote
T({{\mathcal{M}}})
as the collection of
\sigma_{t}^{\Psi}
that are inner automorphisms
of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, i.e.

T({{\mathcal{M}}})=\{t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}:\sigma^{\Psi}_{t}\text{ is an inner automorphism of }{{\mathcal{M}}}\}\ .

(112)

From (
109
),
T({{\mathcal{M}}})
is independent of the choice of
\Psi
, and thus is intrinsic to

{{\mathcal{M}}}
. We call such an object an invariant of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and
T({{\mathcal{M}}})
is one of Connes’ two
fundamental invariants. From (
107
), for type I and type II factors,
T({{\mathcal{M}}})={\mathbb{{R}}}
, while for a type III factor
T({{\mathcal{M}}})
is a proper subset of
{\mathbb{{R}}}
(in fact it can be shown it is a measure zero subset).

The other invariant is defined as

S({{\mathcal{M}}})=\cap_{\Psi}\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Psi})\subset{\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}\equiv[0,\infty)

(113)

where
\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Psi})
is the spectrum of
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
and intersection
\cap_{\Psi}
is taken over all cyclic and separating vectors
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
S({{\mathcal{M}}})
is state-independent and reflects intrinsic properties of the algebra itself.

For
{{\mathcal{M}}}
being type I or II,
S({{\mathcal{M}}})=\{1\}
, as the algebra contains a tracial state for which
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}={\mathbf{1}}
.
24
24

24

For type I
∞
and II
∞
, the tracial state is not a normalizable state, but can be approached by a sequence of normalizable states.

Connes proved that
S({{\mathcal{M}}})
is a closed multiplicaltive subgroup of
{\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}
, and the only other possibilities are:

Type III
0
:
S({{\mathcal{M}}})=\{0,1\}

(114)

\displaystyle\text{Type III${}_{\lambda}$: \quad$S({{\mathcal{M}}})=\{0\}\cup\{\lambda^{n}|n\in{\mathbb{{Z}}}\}$,}\quad\lambda\in(0,1)

(115)

\displaystyle\text{Type III${}_{1}$: \quad$S({{\mathcal{M}}})={\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}$}\ .

(116)

The classification of type III factors provides a classification of the entanglement structure for systems with type III factors, and thus leads to new handles for dealing with such systems.

To conclude this subsection, we mention some further properties of
u_{\Psi\Omega}(s)
introduced in (
109
).
From (
110
), it can be readily shown that
u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle u_{\Psi\Omega}(t_{1}+t_{2})=u_{\Psi\Omega}(t_{1})\left({{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{-it_{1}}u_{\Psi\Omega}(t_{2}){{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{it_{1}}\right)=u_{\Psi\Omega}(t_{1})\sigma_{t_{1}}^{\Omega}(u_{\Psi\Omega}(t_{2}))\ .

(117)

Denoting

\sigma^{\Psi\Omega}_{t}(A)\equiv\sigma_{t}^{\Psi}(A)u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)=u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)\sigma_{t}^{\Omega}(A),

(118)

we then have from (
117
)

\sigma^{\Psi\Omega}_{t+s}(A)=\sigma^{\Psi\Omega}_{t}(\sigma^{\Psi\Omega}_{s}(A))\ .

(119)

Conversely, it can be shown that for any
u(t)
satisfying the equation

u(t_{1}+t_{2})=u(t_{1})\sigma_{t_{1}}^{\Omega}(u(t_{2})),\quad\text{for all }t,s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}

(120)

then there exists a unique cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
with

u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)=u(t)
.

IV.3
The entangled spin example revisited

As an illustration, in this subsection we discuss
S({{\mathcal{M}}})
and
T({{\mathcal{M}}})
for the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
of the entangled spin example in Sec.
II.5
. We will see that each different

{\theta}\in(0,\pi/4)
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta}\equiv{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}({\theta})
belongs to a different type III
λ
algebra with
\lambda=\tan^{2}{\theta}
.

We will first work out the spectrum for the modular operator of
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta}
at a finite
N
, and then take the
N\to\infty
limit.
For a finite
N
, the operator algebra for the
R
-system is type I, and
the reduced density operators for the
R
and
L
systems are given by

\displaystyle\rho_{R}(\Phi_{\theta})=\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta})\otimes\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta})\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta}),\quad\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta})=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos^{2}{\theta}&0\cr 0&\sin^{2}{\theta}\end{matrix}\right),

(121)

\displaystyle\rho_{L}(\Phi_{\theta})=\rho_{l}(\phi_{\theta})\otimes\rho_{l}(\phi_{\theta})\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{l}(\phi_{\theta}),\quad\rho_{l}(\phi_{\theta})=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos^{2}{\theta}&0\cr 0&\sin^{2}{\theta}\end{matrix}\right),

(122)

where
\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta})
and
\rho_{l}(\phi_{\theta})
are respectively the reduced density operators of the right and left spins of a spin pair in the state
\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle
. For
{\theta}\in(0,{\pi\over 4})
, both
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
are invertible, thus

\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to the operator algebra of the
R
-system.
The modular operator is then given by

{{\Delta}}_{\Phi_{{\theta}}}=\rho_{R}(\Phi_{\theta})\otimes\rho_{L}^{-1}(\Phi_{\theta})={{\delta}}_{\theta}\otimes{{\delta}}_{\theta}\otimes\cdots\otimes{{\delta}}_{\theta},\quad{{\delta}}_{\theta}\equiv\rho_{r}(\phi_{\theta})\otimes\rho_{l}^{-1}(\phi_{\theta})\ .

(123)

{{\delta}}_{\theta}
has eigenvalues
(1,\lambda,\lambda^{-1})
with
\lambda=\tan^{2}{\theta}\in(0,1)
.
The eigenvalues of
{{\Delta}}_{\Phi_{{\theta}}}
are then given by

\otimes_{i=1}^{N}(1,\lambda,\lambda^{-1})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\lambda^{{\alpha}_{i}},\quad{\alpha}_{i}=0,1,-1\ .

(124)

Now considering the
N\to\infty
limit, we find that the spectrum of
{{\Delta}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}
is

\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Phi_{\theta}})=\{0\}\cup\{\lambda^{n}|n\in{\mathbb{{Z}}}\}\ .

(125)

Equation (
125
) does not yet give
S({{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta})
which is given by the intersection of the spectrum for all cyclic and separating vectors. It can, however, be shown that
S({{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta})
in fact coincides with (
125
). Comparing with (
115
), we thus find that
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta}
is type III
λ
. That is, different
{\theta}
’s give rise to different entanglement structures.

From (
124
),
{{\Delta}}_{\Phi_{\theta}}^{it}={\mathbf{1}}
for
t

satisfying
\lambda^{it}=1
for any
N
, and accordingly this should also be the case in the
N\to\infty
limit.
Such
t
’s are then contained
in
T({{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta})
, i.e.

\left\{{2\pi n\over\log\lambda}:n\in{\mathbb{{Z}}}\right\}\subseteq T({{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta})\ .

(126)

It can be shown that these in fact are the only inner automorphisms, i.e.

T({{\mathcal{M}}}_{\theta})=\left\{{2\pi n\over\log\lambda}:n\in{\mathbb{{Z}}}\right\},\quad\lambda=\tan^{2}{\theta}\ .

(127)

Recall that for
{\theta}={\pi\over 4}
, the algebra is type II
1
, rather than type III. For
{\theta}=0
, the two spins in a pair are unentangled. Therefore, the Hilbert space can still be factorized in the
N\to\infty
limit, and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
remains a type I algebra.
III
0
and III
1
algebras can also be obtained by slightly generalizing the example. Instead of having each pair of spins in the same entangled state
\lvert{\phi_{\theta}}\rangle
, we can take the
i
-th pair to be in
\lvert{\phi_{{\theta}_{i}}}\rangle
, with an
i
-dependent
{\theta}_{i}

AraWoo68
. Equations (
121
)–(
123
) still apply with replacing
{\theta}
by
{\theta}_{i}
, and (
124
) becomes

\otimes_{i=1}^{N}(1,\lambda,\lambda^{-1})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{{\alpha}_{i}},\quad{\alpha}_{i}=0,1,-1,\quad\lambda_{i}=\tan^{2}{\theta}_{i}\ .

(128)

In the
N\to\infty
limit, all different types, including III
0
and III
1
, can arise, depending on the asymptotic behavior of the infinite sequence
\{\lambda_{i},i=1,2,\cdots,\infty\}
.
25
25

25

Araki and Woods
AraWoo68
showed that if the sequence
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots
converges to some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, then this construction gives a Type III
λ
algebra. If the sequence converges to
0
, one gets a type I
∞
algebra, if the convergence is fast enough. If it is not fast enough, one gets type III
0
.
In fact, for a generic choice of
\{\lambda_{i}\}
, the resulting algebra is of type III
1
.

It is also possible to obtain III
1
algebra by considering another generalization. In each pair of entangled spins, instead of spin-
{{\frac{1}{2}}}
, suppose we consider two spin-
1
system in a general entangled state. For a finite
N
,
\rho_{R}
and
\rho_{L}
still have the form (
121
)–(
122
), but with
\rho_{r}
and
\rho_{l}
replaced a
3\times 3
matrix

\rho_{r}={1\over 1+\lambda+\tilde{\lambda}}{\rm diag}(1,\lambda,\tilde{\lambda})=\rho_{l},\quad\lambda,\tilde{\lambda}>0\ .

(129)

In the
N\to\infty
limit, the eigenvalues of

{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
consist of the numbers
\lambda^{n}\tilde{\lambda}^{m}
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{{Z}}}
. If
\lambda
and
\tilde{\lambda}
are generic, every non-negative real number can be approximated arbitrarily well by
\lambda^{n}\tilde{\lambda}^{m}
.
Thus
\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Psi})=[0,+\infty)={\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}
. It can be further shown that
S({{\mathcal{M}}})
in fact coincides with
\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Psi})
, which gives type III
1
.

IV.4
Local algebras in a RQFT

IV.4.1
Local algebraic structure of a relativistic QFT

We now consider Ex. 3 of Sec.
II.1
, the entanglement for the half space
x>0
in the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle

of a RQFT. For definiteness, we will now restrict to
d=2
on
{\mathbb{{R}}}^{1,1}
(with coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,x)
).

As discussed in Sec.
II.1
, the Hilbert space for the vacuum sector cannot be factorized into those for the
R
and
L
half spaces due to infinite entanglement between them in the continuum. We can characterize the entanglement between
R
and
L
regions in
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
by examining the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
of operators localized in the
R
-region.
In a RQFT, the Heisenberg evolution is causal, which means that
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
is equivalent to the algebra of operators localized in the domain of dependence
\hat{R}
of the
R
-region, which is the right Rindler region (see Fig.
4
). From causality we also have
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{L}}
, where
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
is the algebra of operators localized in the
L
-region.

Figure 4
:
Rindler regions of
(1+1)
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime, whose metric can be written as

ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+dx^{2}=-\rho^{2}d\eta^{2}+d\rho^{2}
. A Rindler observer’s worldline is
a line of constant
\rho
, corresponding to a hyperbola
parameterized by Rindler time
\eta
, which is the worldline of a Rindler observer.
The proper time of a Rindler observer is thus given by
d\tau=\rho d\eta
.
A boost generates a translation in
\eta
, and maps the right Rindler region
\hat{R}
to itself.

The Reeh-Schlieder theorem states that in a relativistic quantum field theory, states obtained by applying operators localized in an open region to the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
form a dense set in the Hilbert space. This implies that
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is cyclic respect to both
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
, and thus it is cyclic and separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
.

In this case, the modular operator
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
for
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
can be shown to be proportional to the boost operator
K

BisWic76
,

K_{\Omega}\equiv-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}=2\pi K\ .

(130)

A modular flow then corresponds to a boost.
To justify equation (
130
), one needs to show: (i) Flows generated by
K
are automorphisms of
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
. (ii)
Correlation functions, such as

{\left\langle{\Omega|e^{iK\eta}A(x^{\mu})e^{-iK\eta}B(y^{\mu})|\Omega}\right\rangle},\quad x^{\mu},y^{\mu}\in\hat{R},

(131)

where
A
and
B
are some local operators, satisfy the KMS relation with
\beta=2\pi
with respect to
\eta
. (i) can be readily checked as a boost takes a point of
\hat{R}
to another point within the region.
(ii) means that in the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
observers who use
\eta
as their time “feel” a nonzero temperature
T={1\over 2\pi}
. From Fig.
4
, such observers should follow trajectories with
\rho={\rm const}
, i.e. the so-called Rindler observers. (ii) is equivalent to Unruh’s observation
Unr76
that in
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, a Rindler observer experiences a finite temperature
T_{\rho}={1\over 2\pi\rho}
.

The boost operator
K
has a continuous spectrum
(-\infty,+\infty)
, which implies
\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Omega})={\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}
.
It can be further shown that
S({{\mathcal{M}}}_{R})
coincides with
\sigma({{\Delta}}_{\Omega})
, and then from (
116
),

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
is type III
1
.
The modular conjugation operator is also simple, given by
J_{\Omega}={{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{T}}
where
{{\mathcal{C}}}
is charge conjugation,
{\mathcal{R}}
is the reflection is the reflection
x\to-x
, and
{\mathcal{T}}
is time reversal. It can be readily checked that
J_{\Omega}
satisfies (
97
)–(
98
).

The above discussion applies to half spaces in higher dimensions, with dimensions transverse to the
t-x
plane going trivially along for the ride.

Now consider a general open region
O
on a Cauchy slice, and the algebra

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
of operators localized in
O
. From the Reeh-Schlieder theorem,
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
. It is no longer possible to construct
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
J_{\Omega}
explicitly, since they depend on the specific theory and specific shape of
O
. Nevertheless, it is possible to deduce that
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}
should be type III
1
with the assumption that the theory has a scale invariant UV fixed point
Fre84
. A rough argument is as follows. Consider the region very close to the boundary
\Sigma_{O}
of
O
, where locally
\Sigma_{O}
can be approximated by a half-plane. Assuming at such distances the physics can be locally described by that of the Rindler region discussed above, we can then conclude that when acting on operators very close to
\Sigma_{O}

-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}=2\pi K,\quad\text{very close to $\Sigma_{O}$}

(132)

where
K
is the boost operator that leaves
\Sigma_{O}
invariant, and thus
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
should again have the spectrum
{\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}
.

Type III
1
structure of a local operator algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
in a RQFT can be attributed to the local Rindler structure close to
\Sigma_{O}
, which in turn arises from the relativistic causal structure of the theory.
Accordingly, we can say that the causal structure of a RQFT requires the type III
1
structure. Indeed, for a non-relativistic QFT, algebras of local operators do not have to be type III
1
.

IV.4.2
Split property

Heuristically, the non-factorization of the Hilbert space in a RQFT with respect to an open region and the associated type III structure
can be attributed to infinite entanglement among short-distance degrees of freedom near the boundary of a region. It is possible to make this connection precise using the
split property

Buc74
;
DopLon84
;
BucWic86
;
BucFre87
(see also
Few16
for a recent discussion).
Consider the setup in Fig.
5
,
where we separate the two regions
R
and
L
by an infinitesimal distance
\epsilon_{b}
.
The split property says that there exists a tensor product decomposition of the Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
, with

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}\subset{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}},\quad{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}\subset{\mathbf{1}}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{2})\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{L_{\epsilon}},

(133)

where
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
denotes the algebra in the region
R_{\epsilon}\equiv R\cup I_{\epsilon}
(and similarly for
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L_{\epsilon}}
).

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}}
is a type I algebra sandwiched between two type III
1
algebras
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
.
Equation (
133
) means that once we separate
R
and
L
regions by an infinitesimal distance,
they can be disentangled!
That is, in
{{\mathcal{H}}}

there are unentangled products states with respect to subsystems described by
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
, and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
act respectively only on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
.

Figure 5
:
Slightly separated
R
and
L
regions on a spatial slice. We denote the green region as
I_{\epsilon}
.

R_{\epsilon}=R\cup I_{\epsilon}
denotes the union of
R
and the green region while
L_{\epsilon}=L\cup I_{\epsilon}
denotes the union of
L
and the green region.

More generally, for any two open regions
O_{1}\subset O_{2}
whose boundaries do not touch (i.e., the closure of
O_{1}
is contained in the interior of
O_{2}
), the split property says there exists a type I factor
{{\mathcal{N}}}
such that

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O_{1}}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O_{2}}\ .

(134)

The split property can be shown
BucFre87
to follow from the
nuclear property

BucWic86
, which is a condition on the high energy behavior and is believed to be satisfied by “reasonable” RQFTs.

If we additionally assume a RQFT to have the split property, it can be shown
BucFre87
that

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
for an open region
O
is not only type III
1
, but also hyperfinite.
A von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is hyperfinite if it can be written as the weak closure of an increasing sequence of finite-dimensional matrix algebras. Hyperfinite type III
1
has been shown to be unique up to isomorphism
Haa87
. That is, local algebras in a RQFT are isomorphic to one another, and thus different theories are distinguished only by the relations among local algebras.

The combination of split property and type III structure of local algebras also implies a surprising property called
strong local preparability

BucDop86
(here our discussion follows that of
Yng14
).
Consider a general state
\omega
acting on
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
of Fig.
5
, we expect in general

\omega(AB)\neq\omega(A)\omega(B),\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}

(135)

which reflects correlations between operators in
R
and
L
. We may ask whether it is possible to use local operations
W
in
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
to construct a new state
\omega_{W}
from
\omega
such that: (i) there is no correlation between operators in
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}

i.e.,
\omega_{W}(AB)=\omega_{W}(A)\omega_{W}(B)
; (ii) on
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
it coincides with some desired state
\phi
, but on
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
it remains unchanged from
\omega
, i.e.,
\omega_{W}(A)=\phi(A)
,
\omega_{W}(B)=\omega(B)
. These are very strong requirements, and it is hard to imagine that they could be satisfied.
It turns out these requirements can be achieved if
W
lies in a slightly larger region, in
R_{\epsilon}
.
More explicitly, for any states
\omega
and
\phi
we can choose an
isometry

W\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
(which depends on
\phi
but not
\omega
) such that

\omega_{W}(AB)=\phi(A)\omega(B),\quad\omega_{W}(\cdot)\equiv\omega(W^{\dagger}\cdot W),\quad W^{\dagger}W={\mathbf{1}},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}\ .

(136)

Proof:
From the split property (
133
),
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
is contained in
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})
. It can be shown there exists a vector
\lvert{\xi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}
such that
\phi(A)={\left\langle{\xi|A|\xi}\right\rangle}
, which will be justified later in Sec.
IV.7
. Consider the projection
P_{\xi}=\lvert{\xi}\rangle\langle{\xi}\rvert\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
.
Since
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
is type III, any projection is equivalent to the identity.
There then exists an isometry
W\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}
with
WW^{\dagger}=P_{\xi},W^{\dagger}W={\mathbf{1}}
,
and

\omega_{W}=\omega(W^{\dagger}BW)=\omega(B),\quad B\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R_{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\ .

(137)

We also have for
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}

P_{\xi}AP_{\xi}=\phi(A)P_{\xi}\quad\to\quad WW^{\dagger}AWW^{\dagger}=\phi(A)WW^{\dagger}\quad\to\quad W^{\dagger}AW=\phi(A){\mathbf{1}}\ .

(138)

We thus find

\omega(W^{\dagger}ABW)=\omega(W^{\dagger}AWB)=\phi(A)\omega(B)\ .

(139)

Another similar, but distinct property of type III
1
algebras is the so-called Connes-Stormer transitive property
ConSto78
:

Theorem (Connes-Stormer)
If
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a type III
1
factor, then for every
\epsilon>0
and
any state
\phi,\omega
, there exists a
unitary

W\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
such that

||\phi-\omega_{W}||<\epsilon\ .

(140)

In other words: Every state can be prepared
locally
, with arbitrary precision, from any other state. This can also be viewed as a statement of ergodicity. The state space is called
homogeneous
.

IV.4.3
Collection of von Neumann algebras in a RQFT

The von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)
associated with an open region
O
encodes all the physics associated with the region including the local spacetime geometry and its entanglement structure. The collection
\{{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)\}
for all choices of open regions
O
in a RQFT should then
provide a complete description of the theory. We conclude this subsection with a brief discussion of relations among
{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)
’s.

By definition, we should have

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2}),\quad{\rm for}\quad O_{1}\subseteq O_{2},

(141)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)={{\mathcal{M}}}(\hat{O}),

(142)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{M}}}(O^{\prime})\subseteq({{\mathcal{M}}}(O))^{\prime}\ .

(143)

Equation (
141
) is a statement of locality, i.e., if a region
O_{1}
is contained in
O_{2}
then all operations that can be performed in
O_{1}
should be a subset of those in
O_{2}
.
Equation (
142
) is a consequence of the causal nature of the equations of motion, i.e., they evolve operators within the lightcone of the initial points. We can thus express an operator in the domain of dependence
\hat{O}
of
O
in terms of those in
O
via equations of motion. It implies that, to describe
\{{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)\}
, it is enough to restrict to a single Cauchy slice, which is also known as the time-slice axiom.
Equation (
143
) is also statement of causality: operators in the causal complement
O^{\prime}
of
O
(i.e., the set of spacetime points spacelike separated from
O
), should commute with those in
O
.

When the equality sign in (
143
) is satisfied we have

{{\mathcal{M}}}(O^{\prime})={{\mathcal{M}}}(O)^{\prime},

(144)

which is called Haag’s duality. It implies a stronger form of locality, as it says that all operators that commute with those in

{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)
lie in
O^{\prime}
. Haag’s duality is not always true, but for topologically trivial regions, the duality is expected to hold for a general RQFT in the vacuum sector.

Now consider
O_{1},O_{2}
on a Cauchy slice. From (
141
), we have

{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\lor{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2})\equiv\left({{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\cup{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2})\right)^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}\cup O_{2}),

(145)

which follows since the left hand side (LHS) is the smallest von Neumann algebra containing both
{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2})
, while from (
141
), both are contained in
{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}\cup O_{2})
. In fact, for topologically trivial regions,
it is expected that the algebras of
\{{{\mathcal{M}}}(O)\}
are additive, i.e.,

{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}\cup O_{2})={{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\lor{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2})\ .

(146)

Equation (
146
) is a stronger statement of locality as it says that there are no additional operators in a joint subregion that cannot separately be expressed in terms of (limits of) sums of products of operators in the individual subregions making up the union. For topologically nontrivial regions, there can be nonlocal operators such as non-contractible Wilson lines that cannot be generated by local operators, which can spoil the additivity property. See
CasHue19
;
CasHue20
for a recent discussion.

If both (
144
) and (
146
) are satisfied, by taking the commutant of (
146
) on both sides,
we also have the intersection property

{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}\cap O_{2})={{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2}),

(147)

which follows from

\displaystyle({{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}\cup O_{2}))^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}((O_{1}\cup O_{2})^{\prime})={{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}^{\prime}\cap O_{2}^{\prime}),

(148)

\displaystyle({{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})\lor{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2}))^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1})^{\prime}\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2})^{\prime}={{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{1}^{\prime})\cap{{\mathcal{M}}}(O_{2}^{\prime})\ .

(149)

When the Haag duality (
144
) and the additivity (
146
) are satisfied for all regions (not just topologically trivial regions), we refer to such theories as ‘complete’ theories (see e.g., a discussion in
Wit23
).

IV.5
Emergent times from subalgebras

In this subsection, we show that additional notions of “emergent” time beyond the modular flow can arise from subalgebras of a von Neumann algebra. In particular, we will introduce a unique feature of type III
1
algebras called half-sided modular inclusion/translation
Bor92
;
Bor93
;
Wie93a
;
Wie93b
;
Bor96
;
Bor98
;
Bor00
;
Ara05
.

Suppose
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a von Neumann algebra and
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is a cyclic and separating vector for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, with the associated modular and modular conjugation operators denoted as
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}\equiv e^{-K_{{\mathcal{M}}}}
and
J_{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
Now suppose there exists a von Neumann subalgebra
{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is cyclic for
{{\mathcal{N}}}
(it is automatically separating for
{{\mathcal{N}}}
given
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is a subalgebra of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
).
We will denote the modular and modular conjugation operators of
{{\mathcal{N}}}
with respect to
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
as
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{N}}}=e^{-K_{{\mathcal{N}}}}
and
J_{{\mathcal{N}}}
. The existence of such a subalgebra brings new structures.

Firstly, there exists a positive operator that annihilates
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
. Since
{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}
, from the definition (
103
) of the Tomita operator,
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
provides an extension of
S_{{\mathcal{N}}}
.
26
26

26

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{N}}}
act respectively on
{{\mathcal{M}}}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
and
{{\mathcal{N}}}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, and
{{\mathcal{N}}}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
.

There is a mathematical theorem saying that if an (unbounded operator)
X_{1}
is an extension of
X_{0}
, then
X_{0}^{\dagger}X_{0}\geq X_{1}^{\dagger}X_{1}
(see e.g. Sec. II.1 of
Bor00
). Given that
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}=S_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\dagger}S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
, we thus have

{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{N}}}\geq{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(150)

Therefore (the
{1\over 2\pi}
factor in the definition of
G
is for later convenience),

\displaystyle G\equiv{1\over 2\pi}(K_{{\mathcal{M}}}-K_{{\mathcal{N}}})\geq 0,\quad G\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=0\ .

(151)

Given that
G
is bounded from below, it can be interpreted as a “Hamiltonian” and the flow
e^{iGs}
generated by it can be interpreted as a “time” flow.
In particular, the flow leaves
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
invariant.
We thus see that existence of a subalgebra with the same cyclic vector leads to an “emergent” time
s
and the vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is time translation invariant.

Another interesting object is
Lon84
;
Lon87

V=J_{{\mathcal{M}}}J_{{\mathcal{N}}},

(152)

which leads to
27
27

27

Note that
V{{\mathcal{M}}}V^{\dagger}
and
VJ_{{\mathcal{N}}}V^{\dagger}
cannot be defined.

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{1}\equiv V^{\dagger}{{\mathcal{M}}}V=J_{{\mathcal{N}}}{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}J_{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}}

(153)

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{2}\equiv V^{\dagger}{{\mathcal{N}}}V=J_{{\mathcal{N}}}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}{{\mathcal{N}}}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}J_{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{1},

(154)

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{3}\equiv V^{\dagger}\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{1}V=J_{{\mathcal{N}}}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{1}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}J_{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{2}

(155)

and so on. By acting repeatedly
V^{\dagger}\cdot V
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
we get a sequence
\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{2n-1},n=1,2,\cdots
, while
acting repeatedly
V^{\dagger}\cdot V
on
{{\mathcal{N}}}
we get another sequence
\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{2n},n=1,2,\cdots
, with the combined sequence nested

\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\subset\hat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{b},\quad a<b\in{\mathbb{{N}}}\ .

(156)

A third object is
Bor99

D(t)\equiv{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{N}}}^{it}\ .

(157)

For small
t
, we have

D(t)=1+2\pi iGt+O(t^{2})\ .

(158)

Using
D(t)
we can also generate new subalgebras of
{{\mathcal{M}}}

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}\equiv D(t){{\mathcal{N}}}(D(t))^{\dagger}={{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}{{\mathcal{N}}}{{\Delta}}_{{{\mathcal{M}}}}^{it}\subseteq{{\mathcal{M}}},

(159)

for which
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is cyclic and separating. It can shown that
D(t)
the following nice properties:

1.

It is unitary and strongly continuous in
t
, with
D(0)={\mathbf{1}}
.

2.

D(t)\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
for all
t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}
.

3.

It can be analytically continued to the strip
S(0,{{\frac{1}{2}}})=\{z\in{\mathbb{{C}}};{\rm Im}z\in(0,{{\frac{1}{2}}})\}
as holomorphic
functions with values in
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
. Furthermore, in the strip,

||D(z)||\leq 1\ .

(160)

4.

At the upper boundary
E(t)\equiv D(t+{{\frac{1}{2}}}i)
is unitary and strongly continuous in
t
.

5.

It satisfies the relation

D(s+t)={{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}D(s){{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{it}D(t)\ .

(161)

6.

If we introduce the anti-unitary operator

Q(t)\equiv E(t)^{\dagger}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}D(t),

(162)

then
Q(t)=(Q(t))^{\dagger}
and is independent of
t
.

7.

Defining a unitary operator

P(t)\equiv D(t)E(0)^{\dagger}

(163)

we have

P(t){{\mathcal{M}}}P^{\dagger}(t)\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(164)

Most of the above statements follow straightforwardly from the definition (
157
). In particular, for item
4
, it can be shown that

E(t)=J_{{\mathcal{M}}}D(t)J_{{\mathcal{N}}}\ .

(165)

It then immediately follows that
Q(t)=J_{{\mathcal{N}}}
. Also note from (
152
) and (
165
)

V=E(0)=D\left({i\over 2}\right)\ .

(166)

It turns out the converse is also true
Bor99
: for any function
D(t)
satisfying all the above properties, there exists a unique von Neumann subalgebra
{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}
for which
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is a cyclic vector, and
D(t)={{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{N}}}^{it}
.

From (
158
), for an infinitesimal
t
,
D(t)
is related to the positive operator
G
defined in (
151
), but for finite
t
, there is in general no simple relation between them.

Surprisingly,
D(t)
turns out be
generated by
G
, if
{{\mathcal{N}}}
satisfies the following additional condition:

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}\equiv{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{{\mathcal{N}}}{{\Delta}}^{it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}=D(t){{\mathcal{N}}}(D(t))^{\dagger}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}},\quad t\leq 0\ .

(167)

This condition says that
{{\mathcal{N}}}
is preserved under modular flows generated by
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}
for half of the
t
-axis, and is called half-sided modular inclusion.
It can then be shown that
Bor00
;
Wie93a
;
Wie93b
;
Bor96
;
Bor98
;
Ara05
:

1.

K_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
K_{{\mathcal{N}}}
obey the following algebra

\displaystyle[K_{{\mathcal{M}}},K_{{{\mathcal{N}}}}]=-2\pi i(K_{{\mathcal{M}}}-K_{{\mathcal{N}}})=-i4\pi^{2}G,

(168)

\displaystyle J_{{\mathcal{M}}}e^{iGs}J_{{\mathcal{M}}}=e^{-iGs},\quad J_{{\mathcal{N}}}e^{iGs}J_{{\mathcal{N}}}=e^{-iGs}\ .

(169)

2.

The flow generated by
G
takes
{{\mathcal{M}}}
to itself for half of the
s
-axis,

e^{iGs}{{\mathcal{M}}}e^{-iGs}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad s<0,

(170)

and in particular,

{{\mathcal{N}}}=e^{-iG}{{\mathcal{M}}}e^{iG}\ .

(171)

3.

{{\mathcal{M}}}
must be type III
1
.

Fom (
168
)

\displaystyle[K_{{\mathcal{M}}},G]=2\pi iG,\quad\Delta^{-it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}G\Delta^{it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}=e^{-2\pi t}G\ .

(172)

Using the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula in (
157
), we find that

D(t)=e^{iG\lambda_{-}(t)},\quad E(t)=D\left(t+{i\over 2}\right)=e^{iG\lambda_{+}(t)}\quad\lambda_{\pm}(t)=1\pm e^{-2\pi t},

(173)

and thus
D(t)
is generated by
G
. Note that
\lambda_{-}(t)\in(-\infty,1)
for
t\in(-\infty,+\infty)
, and
\lambda_{-}(t)<0
for
t<0
, while
\lambda_{+}(t)\in(1,+\infty)
. In particular,

D(+\infty)=e^{iG}=E(+\infty),\quad V=E(0)=e^{2iG}

(174)

where in the second equation we have used (
166
).

From (
171
) and (
173
)

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}={{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}{{\mathcal{N}}}{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{it}={{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-it}e^{-iG}{{\mathcal{M}}}e^{iG}{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{it}=e^{-iGe^{-2\pi t}}{{\mathcal{M}}}e^{iGe^{-2\pi t}}

(175)

which in turn implies

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{\infty}={{\mathcal{M}}},\quad{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t_{1}}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t_{2}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad t_{1}<t_{2}\in{\mathbb{{R}}},

(176)

where in the second equation we have used (
170
). That is, the set
\{{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t},t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\}
can be obtained by acting “time translation” generated from
G
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and has a nested structure. The statement (
170
) is referred to as half-sided modular translation.

To summarize, the half-sided modular inclusion structure (
167
): (i) relates both
D(t)
of (
157
) and
V
of (
152
) to
G
; (ii) leads to the half-sided translation structure (
170
), which in turn extends the discrete nested sequence (
156
) into a continuous nested family labelled by
t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}
(i.e. not only makes it continuous, but also extends it to the other direction).

It can be shown that the half-sided modular translation structure, if exists, is unique.

Theorem
Bor93
Suppose we have (i) nested von Neumann algebras
{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a},a\in{\mathbb{{R}}},\;{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}}_{b},a<b

with common cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
; (ii) a one-parameter unitary group
T(a)
with a positive generator
and
T(a)\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
; (iii)
T(a)
translates the algebras

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}=T(a){{\mathcal{N}}}_{0}T(-a)\ .

(177)

Then
T(a)
is unique.

We can also consider half-sided modular inclusion for the positive half
t
-axis,

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}\equiv{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}{{\mathcal{N}}}{{\Delta}}^{it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad t\geq 0\ .

(178)

Then we have (with certain sign changes from the previous case)

\displaystyle[K_{{\mathcal{M}}},K_{{{\mathcal{N}}}}]=i4\pi^{2}G,\quad[K_{{\mathcal{M}}},G]=-2\pi iG,\quad\Delta^{-it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}G\Delta^{it}_{{\mathcal{M}}}=Ge^{2\pi t},

(179)

\displaystyle D(t)=e^{iG\eta_{+}(t)},\quad E(t)=e^{iG\eta_{-}(t)},\quad\eta_{\pm}(t)=-1\pm e^{2\pi t},\quad V=E(0)=e^{-2iG}\ .

(180)

As an illustration, consider the example of
(1+1)
-dimensional QFT in the vacuum state of Sec.
IV.4
.
We take
{{\mathcal{M}}}
to be the operator algebra in the Rindler
R
-region, and
{{\mathcal{N}}}

to be the operator algebra in the region
\{x^{+}>0,x^{-}<-1\}
(see Fig
6
Left), where
x^{\pm}=x^{0}\pm x^{1}
are light-cone coordinates. From the identification (
130
), we thus have

{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}\equiv e^{iK_{{\mathcal{M}}}t}{{\mathcal{N}}}e^{-iK_{{\mathcal{M}}}t}=\text{operator algebra in region }\{x^{+}>0,x^{-}<-e^{-2\pi t}\}

(181)

with
{{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}\subset{{\mathcal{N}}}
for
t<0
. We thus have the half-sided modular inclusion structure (
167
).

Figure 6
:
Left: The algebra of the subregion
{{\mathcal{N}}}
that leads to the half-sided modular inclusion structure for
x^{-}
translation. Right: The algebra of the subregion
{{\mathcal{N}}}
that leads to the half-sided modular inclusion structure for
x^{+}
translation.

In this case the modular operator of
{{\mathcal{N}}}
can be found explicitly and existence of the positive generator
G
can be directly verified.
More explicitly, flows generated by the modular operator of
{{\mathcal{N}}}
correspond to boosts which which leave the point
a^{\mu}=(a^{+},a^{-})=(0,-1)
invariant.
Denote the translation operator of the QFT as
P^{\mu}=(P^{0}=H,P^{1}=P)
and in the light-come coordinates as
P^{\pm}={{\frac{1}{2}}}(P^{0}\pm P^{1})
. Then
K_{{\mathcal{N}}}
is given by

e^{itK_{{\mathcal{N}}}}=e^{-ia^{\mu}P_{\mu}}e^{itK_{{\mathcal{M}}}}e^{ia^{\mu}P_{\mu}}=e^{-iP^{+}}e^{itK_{{\mathcal{M}}}}e^{iP^{+}}\ .

(182)

From the commutations between
P^{\pm}
and the boost operator
K

\displaystyle[K,P^{\pm}]=\pm iP^{\pm},

(183)

we thus find

K_{{\mathcal{N}}}=K_{{\mathcal{M}}}-2\pi P^{+}\ .

(184)

From (
151
) we conclude that the corresponding
G
is given by

G=P^{+}\ .

(185)

Thus in this case,
G
simply generates a translation in
x^{-}
. All the statements (
168
)–(
171
) can be readily verified.

By taking
{{\mathcal{N}}}
to be the operator algebra associated with the region in Fig.
6
(b), there is a half-sided modular inclusion structure (
178
) with
t\geq 0
, and the corresponding modular translation operator is given by
G=P^{-}
.

IV.6
Relative modular flows and relative entropy

Tomita-Takesaki theory can be generalized to give relative quantum information between two states
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
and
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle

that are cyclic and separating with respect to a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
As a generalization of (
103
) we introduce the Tomita operator
S_{\Psi\Omega}
between two states

S_{\Psi\Omega}A\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=A^{\dagger}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\qquad S_{\Psi\Omega}^{\dagger}A^{\prime}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=A^{\prime{\dagger}}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\,\forall A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}\ .

(186)

By definition
S_{\Psi\Omega}
is again anti-linear, and satisfies

S_{\Psi\Omega}S_{\Omega\Psi}={\mathbf{1}}\ .

(187)

Consider the polar decomposition

S_{\Psi\Omega}=J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}^{{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}=S^{\dagger}_{\Psi\Omega}S_{\Psi\Omega}\ .

(188)

where
J_{\Psi\Omega}
(relative conjugation) is an anti-unitary operator and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}
(relative modular operator) is a positive operator. Equation (
187
) implies

J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}J_{\Omega\Psi}={{\Delta}}^{-{{\frac{1}{2}}}}_{\Omega\Psi}\ .

(189)

Writing the left hand side of the above equation as
J_{\Psi\Omega}J_{\Omega\Psi}J_{\Omega\Psi}^{\dagger}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}J_{\Omega\Psi}
, from
the uniqueness of the polar decomposition of
{{\Delta}}^{-{{\frac{1}{2}}}}_{\Omega\Psi}
we find

J_{\Psi\Omega}J_{\Omega\Psi}={\mathbf{1}}\quad\to\quad J_{\Psi\Omega}=J_{\Omega\Psi}^{\dagger},\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}J^{\dagger}_{\Psi\Omega}={{\Delta}}^{-1}_{\Omega\Psi}\ .

(190)

There is an analogue of the KMS relation

{\left\langle{\Psi|AB|\Psi}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\Omega|B{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}A|\Omega}\right\rangle},\quad A,B\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(191)

which follows from

{\rm LHS}={\left\langle{A^{\dagger}\Psi|J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\Psi\Omega}B^{\dagger}|\Omega}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\Psi\Omega}A\Omega|J_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\Psi\Omega}B^{\dagger}|\Omega}\right\rangle}={\rm RHS}\ .

(192)

Equation (
191
) means that using the relative modular operator we can convert correlation functions in
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
to those
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
and vice versa, which can be very useful.

We can also consider flows generated by the relative modular operator
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Phi}
, which can be shown to satisfy

\displaystyle{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Phi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Phi}^{is}={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\;s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}

(193)

\displaystyle{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Phi}^{-is}A^{\prime}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Phi}^{is}={{\Delta}}_{\Phi}^{-is}A^{\prime}{{\Delta}}_{\Phi}^{is}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},\quad A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},\;s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\ .

(194)

Introduce

u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)\equiv{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Phi}^{-is}{{\Delta}}^{is}_{\Psi\Phi}\ .

(195)

It can be shown that the definition does not depend on the choice
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
.
Acting
u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)\cdot u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)^{\dagger}
on both sides of (
193
), we find

{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Phi}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Phi}^{is}={{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{-is}A{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{is}=u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)\sigma_{s}^{\Psi}(A)u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)^{\dagger}\ .

(196)

That is,
u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)
relates the modular flows generated by
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
. A nontrivial statement is that

u_{\Omega\Psi}(s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}

(197)

and is independent of
\Phi
. This provides a justification of the claim made around (
109
) that
modular flows of
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
are related by inner automorphisms, and (
195
) gives an explicit construction of the inner automorphisms using relative modular flows.
It can be readily checked that the properties (
111
) and (
117
)–(
119
) indeed follow from the definition (
195
).
Similarly,

u^{\prime}_{\Omega\Psi}(t)={{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Phi\Omega}{{\Delta}}^{it}_{\Phi\Psi}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}

(198)

relate modular flows of
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
generated by
{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}
and
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
. By choosing
\Phi
to be either
\Omega
or
\Psi
we can also write
u_{\Psi\Omega}
and
u_{\Psi\Omega}^{\prime}
in various ways

\displaystyle u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)={{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Psi\Omega}{{\Delta}}^{it}_{\Omega}={{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Psi}{{\Delta}}^{it}_{\Omega\Psi},

(199)

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{\Psi\Omega}(t)={{\Delta}}^{it}_{\Omega\Psi}{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Omega}={{\Delta}}^{it}_{\Psi}{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Psi\Omega}\ .

(200)

Introduce

u_{\Psi\Omega}(t)=e^{ih_{\Psi\Omega}t},\quad u^{\prime}_{\Psi\Omega}(t)=e^{ih^{\prime}_{\Psi\Omega}t},\quad K_{\Psi\Omega}=-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}\ .

(201)

Taking derivatives on on
t
and then setting
t
to zero in (
199
)–(
200
), we find that

h_{\Psi\Omega}=K_{\Psi\Omega}-K_{\Omega}=K_{\Psi}-K_{\Omega\Psi},\quad h_{\Psi\Omega}^{\prime}=-K_{\Omega\Psi}+K_{\Omega}=-K_{\Psi}+K_{\Psi\Omega}\ .

(202)

For a type III algebra, while no entropy can be associated with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
in a state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, a relative entropy between two states
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle

and
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
can be defined using the relative modular operator

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi\|\Omega)\equiv-{\left\langle{\Psi|\log{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Psi}|\Psi}\right\rangle}\ .

(203)

The above definition reduces to the standard relative entropy in situations where a trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
is defined. More explicitly,
suppose
\rho_{\Omega},\rho_{\Omega}^{\prime}
and
\rho_{\Psi},\rho_{\Psi}^{\prime}
are respectively density operators associated with
{{\mathcal{M}}},{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
in
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle

and
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, and let

{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Psi}=\rho_{\Omega}\rho^{\prime-1}_{\Psi}\ .

(204)

We then find that
28
28

28

That
\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho^{\prime}_{\Psi}\log\rho^{\prime}_{\Psi})=\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho_{\Psi}\log\rho_{\Psi})
follows from
{\left\langle{\Psi|\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}|\Psi}\right\rangle}=0
.

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Psi\|\Omega)=\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho^{\prime}_{\Psi}\log\rho^{\prime}_{\Psi})-\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho_{\Psi}\log\rho_{\Omega})=\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho_{\Psi}\log\rho_{\Psi})-\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho_{\Psi}\log\rho_{\Omega})\ .

(205)

The relative entropy (
203
) is non-negative as can be seen from

-{\left\langle{\Psi|\log{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Psi}|\Psi}\right\rangle}\geq{\left\langle{\Psi|1-{{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Psi}|\Psi}\right\rangle}=-{\left\langle{\Psi|\Psi}\right\rangle}+{\left\langle{\Omega|\Omega}\right\rangle}=0

(206)

where we have used that
\log x\leq x-1
for a positive
x

and (
191
).

IV.7
A canonical purification: the natural cone

Consider a von Neumann algebra algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
acting on a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
, and a normal state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
From (
26
),
\omega
can be associated with a density operator
\rho
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}
. In general, there can be an infinite number of choices. As an illustration, consider a simple type I example, with
{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}}
.
A state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be associated with a reduced density operator
\rho_{\omega}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{1})
.
On
{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
, there is in general an infinite number of
\rho
’s (which can be either pure or mixed) that reduce to
\rho_{\omega}
when tracing out
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}
.
It is then natural to ask: does there exist a canonical choice of
\rho
? Can
\rho
be a pure state, i.e., a vector in
{{\mathcal{H}}}
?

It turns out these questions can be answered explicitly, if an additional condition is imposed:
{{\mathcal{H}}}
contains a cyclic and separating vector with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. In such a case,
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is said to be in the
standard form
, and there is a canonical
vector

\lvert{\xi_{\omega}}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
such that

\omega(A)={\left\langle{\xi_{\omega}|A|\xi_{\omega}}\right\rangle},\quad\forall A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(207)

An algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
associated with an open region
O
in a RQFT (in the vacuum sector) is in the standard form as the vacuum state is a cyclic and separating vector. From the discussion of Sec.
II.4
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}=\pi_{\omega}({{\mathcal{A}}})^{\prime\prime}
constructed from a
faithful
state
\omega
on a
C^{*}
algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is in the standard form on the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
.

We will now describe
\lvert{\xi_{\omega}}\rangle
explicitly. Suppose there exists a cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. Denote
{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
as the closure of the set of vectors

\{Aj_{\Omega}(A)\lvert{\Omega}\rangle,\;\;A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\},\quad j_{\Omega}(A)\equiv J_{\Omega}AJ_{\Omega},

(208)

which is called the natural cone of
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
. An equivalent definition of
{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
is the closure of the set

\{{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{1\over 4}A^{\dagger}A\lvert{\Omega}\rangle,\;\;A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\}\ .

(209)

It can be shown that every normal state
\omega
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
has a unique representative
\lvert{\xi_{\omega}}\rangle
in
{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
.
We stress that this “canonical” purification
\lvert{\xi_{\omega}}\rangle
depends on
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
; a different cyclic and separating state can
lead to a different purification.

From (
208
)–(
209
) it can be readily checked that for a
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle{{\Delta}}_{\Omega}^{it}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega},\forall t\in{\mathbb{{R}}},\quad J_{\Omega}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad Bj_{\Omega}(B)\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega},\;B\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(210)

If it is separating for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
then it is also cyclic for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
and vice versa.

(211)

Vectors in the natural cone have many nice properties, which we list here (see
BraRobV1
for proofs)

1.

For any
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle,\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
,

{\left\langle{\Phi|\Psi}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\Psi|\Phi}\right\rangle}\geq 0\ .

(212)

2.

If
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
satisfies
J_{\Omega}\lvert{\Phi}\rangle=\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
, then it can be uniquely decomposed as

\lvert{\Phi}\rangle=\lvert{\Phi}\rangle_{+}-\lvert{\Phi}\rangle_{-},\qquad\lvert{\Phi}\rangle_{+},\lvert{\Phi}\rangle_{-}\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega},\qquad{\left\langle{\Phi_{+}|\Phi_{-}}\right\rangle}=0\ .

(213)

3.

Let
\lvert{\Phi_{1,2}}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
be the vector representatives of the states
\varphi_{1,2}
, then

||\,\lvert{\Phi_{1}}\rangle-\lvert{\Phi_{2}}\rangle||^{2}\leq||\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}||^{2}\leq||\,\lvert{\Phi_{1}}\rangle-\lvert{\Phi_{2}}\rangle||\,||\,\lvert{\Phi_{1}}\rangle+\lvert{\Phi_{2}}\rangle||\ .

(214)

4.

Suppose
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
and is cyclic and separating for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, then

J_{\Psi\Omega}=J_{\Omega}=J_{\Omega\Psi}=J_{\Psi}\equiv J,\quad{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}={{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Psi}\ .

(215)

From (
190
) and (
186
) we then have

\displaystyle J{{\Delta}}_{\Psi\Omega}J={{\Delta}}_{\Omega\Psi}^{-1},\quad J\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\quad J\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\lvert{\Omega}\rangle,

(216)

\displaystyle{{\Delta}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\Psi\Omega}\lvert{\Omega}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle,\qquad{{\Delta}}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\Omega\Psi}\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\ .

(217)

5.

Suppose
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
is a cyclic and separating vector not in the natural cone
{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}
. We can write it as

\lvert{\Phi}\rangle=u^{\prime}\lvert{\Phi_{c}}\rangle,\quad u^{\prime}=J_{\Phi\Omega}J_{\Omega}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},\quad\lvert{\Phi_{c}}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}\ .

(218)

6.

Every automorphism of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be written in terms of a unitary action,

{\alpha}(A)=U_{\alpha}AU^{\dagger}_{\alpha},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},

(219)

and the unitary
U_{\alpha}
may be chosen so that

U_{\alpha}{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega}={{\mathcal{P}}}_{\Omega},\qquad JU_{\alpha}=U_{\alpha}J\ .

(220)

V
Crossed product by modular group

Consider a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
acting on Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}
. In many physical contexts, there are “symmetries” acting on the algebra. This means that there exists a group
G
, and a continuous unitary action
{\alpha}
of
G
on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
,

{\alpha}_{g}(A)=U_{g}AU_{g}^{\dagger},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\;g\in G,\;U_{g}\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}),\text{unitary}\ .

(221)

For simplicity, we will assume that
G
is locally compact.
Given such a triple
({{\mathcal{M}}},G,{\alpha})
, it is possible to construct a new von Neumann algebra, called the crossed product of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
by the action
{\alpha}
of
G
, denoted as
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{M}}}\otimes_{\alpha}G
.
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
acts on the extended Hilbert space
\widehat{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}(G)
. Reviews on crossed product can be found in
BraRobV1
;
Dae78
;
Wit21b
.

For example, take
G={\mathbb{{R}}}
, with generator
K
, and the action on
{{\mathcal{M}}}

{\alpha}_{t}(A)=e^{iKt}Ae^{-iKt},\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},\;t\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\ .

(222)

The crossed product
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}={{\mathcal{M}}}\otimes_{\alpha}{\mathbb{{R}}}
acts on
\widehat{{\mathcal{H}}}={{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
.

For our later applications, we will be mainly interested in the case
K=-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
, where
{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
is the modular operator of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
for a cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, and (
222
) then corresponds to the action of the modular group. In this case, it turns out that, for a type III algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
,
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type II
Tak73
.
This means that a trace can be defined for

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
, allowing for the introduction of density operators and entanglement entropies. Moreover,

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
depends only on the algebra

{{\mathcal{M}}}
itself, not on the choice of the state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
. Thus, the type III algebra

{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be studied through its type II counterpart

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

In Sec.
IX
, we will see that the crossed product with the modular group arises naturally in various gravitational contexts, and can be used to shed light on the black hole and de Sitter entropies.

Below we will discuss: (i) how to construct
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
; (ii) show that it is type II by demonstrating the existence of a trace; (iii) use the trace to define the density operator for the subsystem
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in a class of states in
\widehat{{\mathcal{H}}}
; (iv) use the density operator to calculate the entanglement entropy of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the class of states. We will see that the entanglement entropy of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
reduces to the relative entropy of the original type III algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

V.1
Construction of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}

To describe
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
, we add a one-dimensional quantum mechanical system to the original system,
with the new Hilbert space given by
{{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
. We denote
\hat{q}
and
\hat{p}
respectively as the position and momentum operators acting on
L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
(with eigenvalues
q
and
p
and eigenbasis
\lvert{q}\rangle,\lvert{p}\rangle
), with

[\hat{q},\hat{p}]=i\ .

(223)

The crossed product
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be defined as the subalgebra of
{{\mathcal{M}}}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}}))
consisting of elements that are invariant under the action of unitaries generated by

C\equiv K+\hat{q}\ .

(224)

That is,
a\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
iff
[a,C]=0
. Mathematically, this condition mixes nontrivially
{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}}))
, generating new structure. Physically, since
C
generates diagonal translations of
p
and time
t
of (
222
), the invariance condition can be interpreted as “gauging” such translations. In our later examples of Sec.
IX
,
\hat{q}
can be the
O(1)
part of an asymptotic Hamiltonian or the Hamiltonian of an external observer, in which case
p
is the time of such an observer, and invariance under the actions generated by
C
becomes time diffeomorphism invariance.

Clearly
\hat{q}
commutes with
C
, thus operators generated by
e^{-i\hat{q}s},s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}
should belong to
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
29
29

29

\hat{q}
is not bounded, so we will use its Weyl form
e^{-i\hat{q}s}
.

{{\mathcal{M}}}
does not commute with
C
, but it can be readily checked that the following dressed form of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
commutes with
C
,

[e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{-iK\hat{p}},C]=0,\quad A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(225)

Thus
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be described as

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}=\{e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{-iK\hat{p}},e^{-i\hat{q}s}|A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\}^{\prime\prime}\ .

(226)

A general element of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
can be written as

\hat{A}=\int ds\,A(\hat{p};s)e^{-i\hat{q}s},\quad A(\hat{p};s)\equiv e^{iK\hat{p}}A(s)e^{-iK\hat{p}}

(227)

where
A(s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
is an operator-valued function on
{\mathbb{{R}}}
.
Recall
K
generates automorphisms on
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, so
A(\hat{p};s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
; it is a “function” of
\hat{p}
which is now an operator. We may say
A(\hat{p};s)
lives in a quantum “spacetime.”
Now consider the commutant
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
commutes with
A(\hat{p})\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and it trivially commutes with
\hat{q}
. Also by definition
C
commutes with
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
, thus
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
can be written as

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}=\{A^{\prime},e^{iCs}|A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\}^{\prime\prime}\ .

(228)

Note that
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
does not commute with
C
.

We can also consider
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in other forms (in “different frames”) by performing a unitary rotation
U
, i.e.

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}\to U\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}U^{\dagger},\quad C\to UCU^{\dagger},\quad\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}\to U\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}U^{\dagger}\ .

(229)

A convenient choice is
U=e^{-iK\hat{p}}
, which gives

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}=\{A,e^{i(K-\hat{q})s}|A\in{{\mathcal{M}}},s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\}^{\prime\prime},\quad C=\hat{q},

(230)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}=\{e^{-iK\hat{p}}A^{\prime}e^{iK\hat{p}},e^{i\hat{q}s}|A^{\prime}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime},s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\}^{\prime\prime}\ .

(231)

A general element then has the form

\hat{A}=\int ds\,A(s)e^{is(K-\hat{q})},\quad A(s)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(232)

We will often use this form below.

V.2
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type II for modular group

So far the discussion applies to any generator
K
that generates automorphisms of
{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
We now consider
K=-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}
, and show that the corresponding
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
is type II. For this purpose, consider the following state

\lvert{\hat{\Psi}}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\otimes\lvert{p=0}\rangle,

(233)

where
\lvert{p=0}\rangle
is the zero eigenvalue state for
\hat{p}
.
30
30

30

Strictly speaking,
\lvert{\hat{\Psi}}\rangle
is only plane wave normalizable and thus does not lie in
{{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
. Thus
\lvert{\hat{\Psi}}\rangle
is a weight, not a state on
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
But our conclusion is not affected by this. We use the normalization
{\left\langle{q|p=0}\right\rangle}={1\over\sqrt{2\pi}}
.

\lvert{\hat{\Psi}}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
. We can find the corresponding modular operator
\hat{{\Delta}}
by “solving” the KMS relation

{\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{A}\hat{B}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{B}\hat{{\Delta}}\hat{A}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle},\quad\hat{A},\hat{B}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(234)

Using the KMS relation for
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, it can be shown that the above equation is solved by (see Appendix
B
for a derivation)

\hat{{\Delta}}={{\Delta}}_{\Psi}\ .

(235)

This result does not depend on whether we use (
226
) or (
230
). Below we will use (
230
).

Now here is an elementary but key observation
