\displaystyle\hat{{\Delta}}=e^{-K}=e^{-(K-\hat{q})}e^{-\hat{q}}=\rho\rho^{\prime-1},\quad\rho=e^{-(K-\hat{q})},\quad\rho^{\prime}=e^{\hat{q}}

(236)

\displaystyle\hat{{\Delta}}^{-is}=e^{i(K-\hat{q})s}e^{i\hat{q}s}

(237)

Now comparing with (
230
)–(
231
), equation (
237
) is a product of unitary elements of
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
. In other words, acting on
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
,
\hat{{\Delta}}^{-is}
corresponds to an inner automorphism.
Thus we conclude that
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
cannot be type III. Since
\hat{{\mathcal{H}}}
cannot be factorized with respect
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
(which follows from that
{{\mathcal{H}}}
cannot be factorized with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
), then it must be type II.
While
\hat{{\Delta}}
does not depend on whether we use (
226
) or (
230
).
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
do.
In going from (
226
) to (
230
) we need to take

\rho\to U\rho U^{\dagger},\quad\rho^{\prime}\to U\rho^{\prime}U^{\dagger}

(238)

Indeed for (
226
) we have instead

\hat{{\Delta}}=e^{-K}=e^{\hat{q}}e^{-(K+\hat{q})}\quad\to\quad\rho=e^{\hat{q}},\quad\rho^{\prime}=e^{K+\hat{q}}=U^{\dagger}e^{\hat{q}}U

(239)

Since the algebra is type II, it should allow a trace, which can be defined as
31
31

31

The definition can be understood as follows. If the algebra allows a trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
, we expect
{\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{A}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle}=\mathop{\rm tr}(\hat{A}\rho)
. To obtain the trace, we thus multiply a factor
\rho^{-1}
.

\mathop{\rm tr}\hat{A}\equiv{\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{A}\rho^{-1}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{A}\rho^{\prime-1}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle},

(240)

where in the second equality we have used that
\hat{\Psi}
is invariant under
\hat{{\Delta}}
. For an element of the form (
232
), we can write the above expression more explicitly as

\mathop{\rm tr}\hat{A}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-q}{\left\langle{\Psi|\hat{A}|\Psi}\right\rangle},\quad\hat{A}\equiv\int ds\,A(s)e^{i(K-q)s}\ .

(241)

It can readily checked that
\mathop{\rm tr}
indeed satisfies the cyclic permutation property,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm tr}(\hat{A}\hat{B})={\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\hat{A}\hat{B}\rho^{-1}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{B}\rho^{-1}\hat{{\Delta}}\hat{A}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{B}\rho^{\prime-1}\hat{A}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{B}\hat{A}\rho^{\prime-1}}\right\rangle}=\mathop{\rm tr}(\hat{B}\hat{A}),

(242)

where we have used (
234
) in the second equality and suppressed
\hat{\Psi}
in subsequent expectation values for notation simplicity.
We can equivalently write (
240
) as

\mathop{\rm tr}\hat{A}={\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}_{\rm T}|\hat{A}|\hat{\Psi}_{\rm T}}\right\rangle}

(243)

where
\lvert{\hat{\Psi}_{\rm T}}\rangle
can be written as

\lvert{\hat{\Psi}_{\rm T}}\rangle=\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\otimes\int dq\,e^{-{q\over 2}}\lvert{q}\rangle\ .

(244)

Since

\mathop{\rm tr}{\mathbf{1}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-q}{\left\langle{\Psi|{\mathbf{1}}|\Psi}\right\rangle}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-q}=\infty,

(245)

the algebra is type II
∞
.

Interestingly, we can obtain a type II
1
algebra if we restrict the spectrum of
\hat{q}
to be bounded below, e.g. imposing
q\geq 0
. This can be achieved by introducing a projector
\Pi={\theta}(\hat{q})

on
L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
, where
{\theta}(x)
is the step function that is
1
for
x\geq 0
and
0
for
x<0
.
An operator
\hat{A}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
then becomes
\Pi\hat{A}\Pi
acting on
{{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}}_{+})
(where
{\mathbb{{R}}}_{+}
is the half-line
q>0
), and the algebra becomes
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{+}=\Pi\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}\Pi
. The identity operator in the algebra
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{+}
is now given by
\Pi={\theta}(\hat{q})
, and its trace is given by

{\rm Tr}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{+}}{\mathbf{1}}={\rm Tr}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}}\Pi=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-q}{\theta}(q)=\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\,e^{-q}=1\ .

(246)

As discussed earlier in Sec.
II.3
, a type II algebra with a normalizable trace is type II
1
, and thus
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{+}
is type II
1
.

To conclude this subsection, we note that while in the construction above, the cyclic and separating state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle

played a central role, the crossed product, in fact, does not depend on the choice of
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, and can be viewed as intrinsically defined for the algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
itself. Consider
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Phi}
obtained from a different cyclic and separating state
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
. It can be shown to be unitarily equivalent to
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Psi}
obtained from
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
,

\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Phi}=u^{\prime}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Psi}u^{\prime{\dagger}}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})

(247)

where
u^{\prime}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})
is the unitary (
200
) relating modular flows of
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}
in state
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
and
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle

with the flow parameter taken to be
\hat{p}
, i.e.

u^{\prime}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})={{\Delta}}^{i\hat{p}}_{\Psi\Phi}{{\Delta}}^{-i\hat{p}}_{\Psi}={{\Delta}}^{i\hat{p}}_{\Phi}{{\Delta}}^{-i\hat{p}}_{\Phi\Psi}\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\prime}\ .

(248)

To see (
247
), consider
\hat{A}=Ae^{it(K_{\Psi}-\hat{q})}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Psi}
,

\displaystyle

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})Ae^{i(K_{\Psi}-\hat{q})t}u^{\prime{\dagger}}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})=Au^{\prime}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p})e^{iK_{\Psi}t}u^{\prime{\dagger}}_{\Phi\Psi}(\hat{p}+t)e^{-i\hat{q}t}

(249)

\displaystyle=A{{\Delta}}^{i\hat{p}}_{\Psi\Phi}{{\Delta}}^{-i\hat{p}}_{\Psi}e^{iK_{\Psi}t}{{\Delta}}^{i(\hat{p}+t)}_{\Psi}{{\Delta}}^{-i(\hat{p}+t)}_{\Psi\Phi}e^{-i\hat{q}t}=A{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Psi\Phi}e^{-i\hat{q}t}=A{{\Delta}}_{\Phi}^{-it}{{\Delta}}_{\Phi}^{it}{{\Delta}}^{-it}_{\Psi\Phi}e^{-i\hat{q}t}

\displaystyle=Ae^{i(K_{\Phi}-\hat{q})t}u_{\Phi\Psi}(t)\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Phi}

where in the last line we have used (
199
) and that
u_{\Phi\Psi}(t)\in{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

V.3
Density operator for
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in a general semi-classical state

Now consider states in
{{\mathcal{H}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}})
of the form

\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle=\lvert{\Phi}\rangle\otimes\lvert{g}\rangle\equiv\lvert{\Phi,g}\rangle,\quad\lvert{\Phi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}},\quad\lvert{g}\rangle\in L^{2}({\mathbb{{R}}}),

(250)

where
\lvert{g}\rangle
has coordinate space wave function
g(q)
which is taken to be nonzero everywhere and normalized as

\int dq\,|g(q)|^{2}=1\ .

(251)

The density operator
\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}})
associated with
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the state
\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle
can be found from

\mathop{\rm tr}(\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}\hat{A})={\left\langle{\hat{\Phi}|\hat{A}|\hat{\Phi}}\right\rangle},\quad\forall\hat{A}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}},

(252)

where the trace
\mathop{\rm tr}
is defined by (
240
).
It is convenient to use the form (
230
), with
\hat{A}=Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}
and
\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle\to e^{-iK\hat{p}}\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle
and (
252
) becomes

{\left\langle{\hat{\Psi}|\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}e^{-\hat{q}}|\hat{\Psi}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\hat{\Phi}|e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\hat{\Phi}}\right\rangle}\ .

(253)

We can further write the RHS of (
253
) as

\displaystyle RHS={\left\langle{\Phi,g|e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\Phi,g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\Psi,g|{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\Psi,g}\right\rangle}

(254)

\displaystyle=\int dq\,{\left\langle{\Psi,g|{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}e^{iK\hat{p}}Ae^{is(K-\hat{q})}|q}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{q|e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\Psi,g}\right\rangle}

(255)

\displaystyle=\int dq\,{\left\langle{\Psi|{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}g^{*}(q-K)Ae^{is(K-q)}g(q-K)|\Psi}\right\rangle}

(256)

where we have used (
191
) in the second equality of (
254
) and used

{\left\langle{q|e^{-iK\hat{p}}|g}\right\rangle}=g(q-K)

(257)

in obtaining (
256
). The LHS of (
253
) can be written as

\displaystyle LHS={1\over 2\pi}\int dqdq^{\prime}\,e^{-q}{\left\langle{\Psi,q^{\prime}|\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}Ae^{is(K-q)}|\Psi,q}\right\rangle}

(258)

Comparing (
256
) with (
258
), we conclude that

\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}=2\pi g(\hat{q}-K)e^{\hat{q}}{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}g^{*}(\hat{q}-K),

(259)

which is the unique expression that is Hermitian.
32
32

32

To see that with (
259
), (
258
) is equal to (
256
), use (
191
) twice in the resulting (
258
).
To see that (
259
) is affiliated with
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, note that

\left({{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}e^{\hat{q}}\right)^{is}={{\Delta}}^{is}_{\Phi\Psi}{{\Delta}}_{\Psi}^{-is}e^{i(\hat{q}-K)s}=u_{\Phi\Psi}(s)e^{i(\hat{q}-K)s}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}\ .

(260)

To obtain
\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}
for the representation (
226
), we conjugate (
259
) by
U^{\dagger}
,

\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}=2\pi e^{iK\hat{p}}g(\hat{q}-K)e^{\hat{q}}{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}g^{*}(\hat{q}-K)e^{-iK\hat{p}}\ .

(261)

V.4
Entanglement entropy for
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}
in a general semi-classical state

Now consider the entanglement entropy for
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the state
\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle=\lvert{\Phi,g}\rangle
, which is defined as the von Neumann entropy of
\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}
,

S_{\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}}=-\mathop{\rm tr}\left(\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}\log\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}\right)=-{\left\langle{\hat{\Phi}|e^{iK\hat{p}}\log\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\hat{\Phi}}\right\rangle}\ .

(262)

Note that the entropy does not depend on which representation we use.
Below we will assume that
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
is also cyclic and separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and that
g
is slowly varying, i.e.,
g^{\prime}(q)\propto\epsilon
where
\epsilon
is a small parameter. We will work to zeroth order

\epsilon
.

We then find that

-\log\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}=-\hat{q}-\log|g(\hat{q}-K)|^{2}+K_{\Phi\Psi}+O(\epsilon)

(263)

where the
O(\epsilon)
contribution comes from commuting
g(\hat{q}-K)
with
K_{\Phi\Psi}=-\log{{\Delta}}_{\Phi\Psi}
.
Now using (
202
)

K_{\Phi\Psi}=K_{\Phi}+K-K_{\Psi\Phi},

(264)

we have

\displaystyle-\log\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}=-(\hat{q}-K)-\log|g(\hat{q}-K)|^{2}+K_{\Phi}-K_{\Psi\Phi}+O(\epsilon)

(265)

\displaystyle-e^{iK\hat{p}}\log\rho_{\hat{\Phi}}e^{-iK\hat{p}}=-\hat{q}-\log|g(\hat{q})|^{2}+e^{iK\hat{p}}(K_{\Phi}-K_{\Psi\Phi})e^{-iK\hat{p}}

(266)

Recall that

{\left\langle{q|e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\Phi,g}\right\rangle}=g(q-K)\lvert{\Phi}\rangle

(267)

Using (
257
), we then find
thus

\displaystyle{\left\langle{\Phi,g|e^{iK\hat{p}}(K_{\Phi}-K_{\Psi\Phi})e^{-iK\hat{p}}|\Phi,g}\right\rangle}=\int dq\,{\left\langle{\Phi|g^{*}(q-K)(K_{\Phi}-K_{\Psi\Phi})g(q-K)|\Phi}\right\rangle}

(268)

\displaystyle=-S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Phi||\Psi)+O(\epsilon)\ .

(269)

In the last step we have expanded
g(q-K)
around
g(q)
(note the normalization (
251
)), and used
K_{\Phi}\lvert{\Phi}\rangle=0
and (
203
).

We then have

\displaystyle S_{\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}}=-S_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\Phi||\Psi)-\bar{q}+S_{o},

(270)

\displaystyle\bar{q}=\int dq\,q|g(q)|^{2},\quad S_{o}=-\int dq\,|g(q)|^{2}\log|g(q)|^{2}\ .

(271)

Thus, up to some (
\lvert{g}\rangle
-dependent) constants, the entanglement entropy for the type II
∞
algebra
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
in the state
\lvert{\hat{\Phi}}\rangle
is given by the negative of the relative entropy between
\lvert{\Phi}\rangle
and
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
This result can in fact be expected, as
\widehat{{\mathcal{M}}}
depends only on the
{{\mathcal{M}}}
, and the only entropy that can be defined for
{{\mathcal{M}}}
is its relative entropy.

VI
AdS/CFT duality in the large
N
limit: algebraic formulation

Having elucidated the connections between entanglement and von Neumann algebras, and having introduced the relevant background on von Neumann algebras, we now turn to their applications in the context of AdS/CFT duality and quantum gravity.
In this section, we examine the AdS/CFT correspondence in the large-
N
limit from an operator-algebraic perspective
LeuLiu21b
;
LeuLiu22
;
FauLi22
. Some of the earliest results along these lines were obtained by Rehren and collaborators
Reh99a
;
Reh00
;
DueReh02a
;
DueReh02b
shortly after the discovery of the duality. Notably, it was argued that the duality requires the boundary CFT to violate the additivity condition (
146
)
Reh99a
, and that it should be formulated as a theory of generalized fields that fails to satisfy the time-slice axiom (
142
)
DueReh02b
.

VI.1
General description

In the AdS/CFT duality, a bulk quantum gravity system in AdS
d+1
is equivalent to a CFT
d
on
{\mathbb{{R}}}\times S_{d-1}
(which is the conformal boundary of AdS
d+1
). A prototypical example is the duality between
the
{{\mathcal{N}}}=4
Super-Yang-Mills (SYM) theory with gauge group
SU(N)
on
{\mathbb{{R}}}\times S_{3}
and
the IIB superstring in AdS
{}_{5}\times S_{5}
. Here are some entries of the dictionary between the two theories:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}\quad

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT}

(272)

\displaystyle G_{N}\to 0\quad

\displaystyle{\leftrightarrow}

\displaystyle\quad N\to\infty

(273)

\displaystyle{{\alpha^{\prime}}}\to 0\quad

\displaystyle{\leftrightarrow}

\displaystyle\quad\lambda\to\infty\;\text{($\lambda$: t' Hooft coupling)}

(274)

elementary field

\displaystyle{\leftrightarrow}

single-trace operator

(275)

classical bulk geometry
\phi_{c}

\displaystyle{\leftrightarrow}

\displaystyle\quad\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\;\text{(semi-classical state)}

(276)

Since the bulk and boundary Hilbert spaces are identified, a reference to a state implies both the bulk and boundary. Below we simply denote
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT}
as
{{\mathcal{H}}}
.
The correspondence (
275
) can be written explicitly in terms of the extrapolation dictionary,

{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{-{{\Delta}}}\phi(X),\quad X=(r,x)

(277)

where
\phi(X)
denotes a bulk elementary field, with
r
the bulk radial coordinate and
x
denoting a boundary point. For the
{{\mathcal{N}}}=4
SYM theory,
{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)
is a boundary operator of the form

{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)=\mathop{\rm Tr}(\cdots),

(278)

and
{{\Delta}}
is the conformal dimension of
{{\mathcal{O}}}
. For general holographic systems where such a description is not known explicitly, we will use (
277
) as the
definition of single-trace operators
, i.e., operators in the boundary system corresponding to the boundary limit of bulk elementary fields.
In (
276
), we use
\phi
to collectively denote all bulk fields—a notation we will continue to use below—with
\phi_{c}
denoting their configurations in a classical solution.

As mentioned in the Introduction, our goal is to understand how bulk geometric concepts emerge in the semi-classical
G_{N}\to 0
limit. We will thus be mainly concerned with the
N\to\infty
limit of the boundary theory, where we will see that the structures of the Hilbert space and operator algebras undergo significant changes. Unless mentioned explicitly, we will also restrict to the
\lambda\to\infty
limit, where the bulk theory is described by the Einstein gravity coupled to matter fields. In some theories, such as theories from M2-branes or M5-branes,
N
is the only parameter, for which we only need to take the
N\to\infty
to get the Einstein gravity regime.

We refer to a state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
that has a bulk description in terms of a classical geometry as a semi-classical state.
By definition, such a state has a well-defined large
N
limit.
That is,
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
can be regarded as the
N\to\infty
limit of a sequence of states
\{\lvert{\Psi_{N}}\rangle\}
, one for each
SU(N)
theory.
An example of a semi-classical state is the CFT vacuum
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
which corresponds to the empty AdS spacetime.
Another example is the thermofield double state
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
which at a sufficiently high temperature is dual to the eternal black hole
Mal01
. A single-sided black hole formed from gravitational collapse should also correspond to a semi-classical state, even though finding an explicit boundary description of this state is in general difficult.

We say that an operator
A
has a well-defined large
N
limit in a semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
if it can be regarded as the
N\to\infty
limit
33
33

33

In light of the recent discussion of
SchWit22
;
Liu25
, the
N\to\infty
limit may involve averages over
N
.
of a sequence of operators
\{A_{N}\}
in the finite-
N
theory, with corresponding states
\{\lvert{\Psi_{N}}\rangle\}
, such that the expectation value converges to a finite result

\lim_{N\to\infty}\langle{\Psi_{N}}\rvert A_{N}\lvert{\Psi_{N}}\rangle<\infty\ .

(279)

The collection
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
of such operators may depend on the specific semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, which we will discuss more explicitly below. From the extrapolation dictionary (
277
), a universal set that exists for all semi-classical states is

{\mathcal{S}}\equiv\text{$*$-algebra generated by single-trace operators}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}\ .

(280)

{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
may coincide with
{\mathcal{S}}
, but may in principle contain additional operators whose existence depends on the specific semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
. Below, we will denote the restriction of
{\mathcal{S}}
to a boundary subregion
O
as
{\mathcal{S}}_{O}
.

We will further assume that
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
is closed under products of operators, in which case
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
is a
*
-algebra. Operators in
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
should in principle also carry a norm inherited from the finite-
N
theories. Completing it with respect to this norm, we then obtain a
C^{*}
-algebra.
Expectation values in
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
define a state
\omega_{\Psi}
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
.
We can then build a GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
using the action of
\omega_{\Psi}
on
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
, which can be viewed as the space of “small” excitations around
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, with the representation of
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
given by
\pi_{\Psi}({{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi})
.

If the bulk geometry dual to
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is smooth and
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is pure, we expect
\omega_{\Psi}
to be a pure state with respect to the algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
. From Proposition
II.1
, this implies

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})})=\bigl(\pi_{\Psi}({{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi})\bigr)^{\prime\prime}\ .

(281)

When the corresponding bulk geometry contains a singularity, however, (
281
) is not guaranteed. If the geometry contains a complete smooth Cauchy slice (e.g. with spacelike singularities only in the past and/or future), then (
281
) should still hold. Unless otherwise noted, we will assume (
281
) below.
34
34

34

In Sec.
VI.5
, we discuss a diagnostic of “firewalls” at horizons, which is associated with the failure of (
281
).

We will denote (where
O
is a boundary region)

{\mathcal{Y}}=(\pi_{\Psi}({\mathcal{S}}))^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{O}=(\pi_{\Psi}({\mathcal{S}}_{O}))^{\prime\prime}\ .

(282)

In the case that
{\mathcal{S}}
is a proper subalgebra of
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
,
\omega_{\Psi}
should be a mixed state for
{\mathcal{S}}
, which can be used to diagnose existence of operators in
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
outside
{\mathcal{S}}
. More explicitly, we can build a GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,{\mathcal{S}}}^{({\rm GNS})}
using the action of
\omega_{\Psi}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
. If
\omega_{\Psi}
is not pure, the completion of the representation of
{\mathcal{S}}
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,{\mathcal{S}}}^{({\rm GNS})}
is a strict subset of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,{\mathcal{S}}}^{({\rm GNS})})
. Note also that for
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,{\mathcal{S}}}^{({\rm GNS})}
is different from
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
.

On the gravity side, we take all bulk fields to be gravitationally coupled, with the
{1\over 16\pi G_{N}}
factor
in front of the full bulk action. Let

\phi=\phi_{c}+\kappa{{\delta}}\phi,\quad\kappa=\sqrt{8\pi G_{N}},

(283)

and expand the gravity action
S[\phi]
in
{{\delta}}\phi
to get, schematically,

\displaystyle S[\phi]=S[\phi_{c}]+S_{2}[{{\delta}}\phi]+S_{\rm int}[{{\delta}}\phi],

(284)

\displaystyle S_{\rm int}=\kappa S_{3}+\kappa^{2}S_{4}+\cdots+\kappa^{n-2}S_{n}+\cdots,

(285)

where
S_{2}
is the quadratic action of
{{\delta}}\phi
with no dependence on
\kappa
, and
S_{n}
contains terms with
n
factors of
{{\delta}}\phi
. At the leading order in the
\kappa\to 0
limit, only the quadratic action
S_{2}
survives, resulting in a free quantum field theory of
{{\delta}}\phi
in the curved spacetime described by
\phi_{c}
.
S_{2}
can be quantized using the standard formalism.
With the appropriate selection of a “vacuum” state
\lvert{0}\rangle_{\phi_{c}}
,
a Fock space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)}
can be constructed by applying
{{\delta}}\phi
’s to
\lvert{0}\rangle_{\phi_{c}}
.

For the duality to hold, we must have

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})},

(286)

which means that the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
must have the structure of a Fock space, i.e.
the representation of
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
should be described by a Gaussian theory.
For products of single-trace operators in the vacuum or the thermofield double state, this is the standard story of the large
N
factorization. The duality (
286
) implies that this should be true also for any semi-classical states and for those operators that lie outside
{\mathcal{S}}
.
35
35

35

In fact, we could use the large
N
factorization as the definition of
a semi-classical state purely from the boundary perspective. For example, that is the definition used in
LeuLiu22
.

It is also natural to identify the vacua on the two sides, i.e.

\lvert{0}\rangle_{\phi_{c}}=\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Psi},

(287)

where
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Psi}
denotes the state corresponding to the identity operator in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
.

Each semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
gives rise to a GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
(or equivalently, a Fock space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm Fock)}
). Distinct classical geometries typically differ in energy by an amount of order
O(1/G_{N})
, whereas states within
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
differ in energy from
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
only by
O(G_{N}^{0})
by construction. Consequently, the GNS Hilbert spaces
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{1}}
and

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{2}}
, associated with two such classical geometries, cannot overlap at any finite order in
G_{N}
-perturbation theory.
Moreover, even if two semi-classical states have the same energy, they may still belong to different GNS Hilbert spaces if they are separated by an infinite energy barrier in the configuration space.

Therefore, in the large
N
limit, the system no longer has a single Hilbert space. Instead, the space of states separates into

disjoint sectors
around different semi-classical states. See Fig.
7
.
This is rather similar to the entangled spin example in the thermodynamic limit discussed in Sec.
II.1
and
II.5
. For different values of
{\theta}
, the states
\lvert{\Phi_{\theta}}\rangle
lead to disjoint sectors, each with its own GNS Hilbert space and distinct operator algebraic structures.

Figure 7
:
In the large
N
limit, the space of states separates into disjoint sectors around semi-classical states.

The bulk theory described by
S_{2}[{{\delta}}\phi]
can be regarded as an ordinary field theory in a curved spacetime with evolution of
{{\delta}}\phi
governed by Heisenberg equations of motions. In contrast, on the boundary, correlation functions of operators in
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
are obtained by taking the large
N
limit; there are no equations of motion governing the corresponding Gaussian fields. Thus
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
should be generated by generalized free fields.
36
36

36

A generalized free field is a Gaussian field specified solely by its two-point correlation functions, with no equations of motion governing its evolution. For example, there is no Heisenberg equations governing the evolution of single-trace operators.

That there are no equations of motion governing generalized free fields has important implications
for operator algebras: operator algebras associated with different Cauchy slices on the boundary are inequivalent. This leads to many new emergent algebras in the large
N
limit that are not present at finite
N
. See Fig.
8
.

Figure 8
:
The cylinder represents the boundary spacetime.
Left: Algebras
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
generated by generalized free fields on two different Cauchy slices are inequivalent. Right:
At finite
N
, the algebra associated with the time band indicated in the plot is equivalent to that on a single Cauchy slice. But in the large
N
limit, it is inequivalent and is in general a proper subalgebra of the full algebra.

Another way to understand the independence of algebras at different times is to note that the boundary Hamiltonian
H
does not survive the large-
N
limit.
H
is defined as

H=\int_{\sigma}d^{d-1}x\,T^{00},

(288)

where
\sigma
is a boundary Cauchy slice.
T^{\mu\nu}
is the boundary stress tensor, which has the form

T^{\mu\nu}=N\mathop{\rm Tr}(\cdots)\ .

(289)

Due to the prefactor of
N
in (
289
), the stress tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
—and the Hamiltonian
H
—does not have a well-defined large-
N
limit in
any
sector. As a result, single-trace operators at different times become independent in the large-
N
limit. The rescaled operators

\hat{T}^{\mu\nu}\equiv{1\over N}T^{\mu\nu},\quad\hat{H}\equiv{H\over N},

(290)

do survive the large
N
limit. However, they do not generate finite time translations,

i[\hat{H},{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)]={1\over N}\partial_{t}{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)\ .

(291)

In a semiclassical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
(such as the vacuum), where time-translation symmetry is present, there should exist a corresponding time-translation operator
\hat{h}_{\Psi}
within that sector, which acts on single-trace operators as
37
37

37

Below,
{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)
should be understood as
\pi_{\Psi}({{\mathcal{O}}}(x))
. For notational simplicity, we will suppress the symbol
\pi_{\Psi}
, as we will often do in later discussions.

i[\hat{h}_{\Psi},{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)]=\partial_{t}{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)\ .

(292)

This is not in tension with the independence of operator algebras on different time slices. Unlike (
288
), the operator
\hat{h}\Psi
cannot be expressed as the integral of a local operator over a
single
Cauchy slice. We will present an explicit example in the vacuum sector in the next subsection. On the gravity side, the corresponding bulk geometry
\phi_{c}
should admit a timelike Killing vector, and
\hat{h}\Psi
corresponds to the bulk generator associated with that symmetry.

Clearly, the above discussion applies to any symmetry in the large-
N
limit, where the symmetry generator can no longer be expressed as an integral of local operators over a single time slice.

We now illustrate the general structure discussed above more explicitly using a few examples.

VI.2
The vacuum sector

For a CFT
d
on
{\mathbb{{R}}}\times S_{d-1}
(with
S_{d-1}
having radius
R
), the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
is dual to empty global AdS, with metric

ds^{2}=-\left(1+{\rho^{2}\over R^{2}}\right)dt^{2}+{d\rho^{2}\over 1+{\rho^{2}\over R^{2}}}+\rho^{2}d\Omega_{d-1}^{2},

(293)

together with the bulk
vacuum state
\lvert{0}\rangle_{\rm AdS}\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm AdS}^{\rm(Fock)}
that is invariant under AdS isometries

\lvert{\Omega}\rangle\quad{\leftrightarrow}\quad(\text{empty AdS},\lvert{0}\rangle_{\rm AdS})\ .

(294)

In this case, both the bulk Fock space and the boundary GNS Hilbert space can be constructed explicitly, and the identification (
286
) can be directly verified.

More explicitly, a bulk field
\phi(X)
has the mode expansion

\phi(X)=\sum_{k}\left(u_{k}(X)a_{k}+u_{k}^{*}(X)a_{k}^{\dagger}\right),\quad a_{k}\lvert{0}\rangle_{\rm AdS}=0,

(295)

where
\{u_{k}(X)\}
is the complete set of normalizable bulk wave functions in empty AdS.
The Fock space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm AdS}^{\rm(Fock)}
is generated by acting
a_{k}^{\dagger}
on
\lvert{0}\rangle_{\rm AdS}
.

On the boundary,
connected
vacuum correlation functions of single-trace operators have large-
N
scalings of the form

{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}}\right\rangle}=0,\quad{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{2}}\right\rangle}_{c}\sim O(N^{0}),\quad{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{n}}\right\rangle}_{c}\sim N^{2-n}\ .

(296)

Thus, at the leading order of the
N\to\infty
limit,

{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{n}}\right\rangle}=\sum(\text{products of two-point functions})\sim O(N^{0}),

(297)

defining a Gaussian theory in terms of two-point functions, which are known explicitly from conformal symmetry up to normalization constants. Each single-trace operator can be viewed as a generalized free field, and accordingly the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)}
obtained from acting with the single-trace operator algebra (
280
) on
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle

has the structure of a Fock space. Furthermore, it can be shown that

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)})=(\pi_{\Omega}({\mathcal{S}}))^{\prime\prime}\ .

(298)

From the diagnostic discussed below (
282
),
\omega_{\Omega}
is a pure state for
{\mathcal{S}}
, and

{\mathcal{S}}={{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Omega}
, i.e., there is no other operator surviving the large
N
limit in the vacuum sector.

In
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)}
,
the representation of a single-trace operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}
can be expanded as

\pi_{\Omega}({{\mathcal{O}}}(x))=\sum_{k}(v_{k}(x)b_{k}+v_{k}^{*}(x)b_{k}^{\dagger}),\quad b_{k}\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Omega}=0,

(299)

where
v_{k}(x)
is a complete set of basis functions on
{\mathbb{{R}}}\times S_{d-1}
.

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)}
is generated by acting
b_{k}^{\dagger}
on
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Omega}
.
For
\phi
and
{{\mathcal{O}}}
dual to each other, it can be shown that (with an appropriate choice of normalization for
{{\mathcal{O}}}
and basis functions)

v_{k}(x)=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{{\Delta}}u_{k}(X),\quad X=(r,x),

(300)

and (
277
) implies that we should identify

a_{k}=b_{k}\ .

(301)

This establishes (
286
), i.e., we can identify

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm AdS}^{\rm(Fock)}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)},\quad\lvert{0}\rangle_{\rm AdS}=\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Omega},\quad{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm AdS}^{\rm(Fock)})={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)})\ .

(302)

Equation (
301
) can also be written in coordinate space as

\phi(X)=\int d^{d}x\,K(X;x)\pi_{\Omega}({{\mathcal{O}}}(x)),

(303)

where the kernel
K(X;x)
satisfies the bulk equations of motion and (
277
), and is known as the global HKLL construction
BanDou98
;
Ben99
;
HamKab05
;
HamKab06
.
From the fact that energies in a conformal representation are equally spaced with interval
2
, it can be shown
that
LeuLiu22

{{\mathcal{B}}}\left({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{\rm GNS}\right)={\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}},\quad w\geq\pi R,

(304)

where
I_{w}
is a uniform time band on the boundary of width
w
. Thus in (
303
), it is enough to restrict the

x
-integration to a time band of width
\pi R
.

Quantum field theory in the geometry (
293
) possesses a time-translation global symmetry, with an associated bulk Hamiltonian
\hat{h}_{\Omega}
given by

\hat{h}_{\Omega}=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}d\rho d^{d-1}\Omega\,\rho^{d-1}{\mathcal{T}}^{t}_{t}

(305)

where
{\mathcal{T}}^{t}_{t}
is the bulk stress tensor, which is quadratic in the bulk fields
\phi
in the free theory limit.
Plug (
295
) into (
305
) we obtain a quadratic expression in terms of
a_{k}
, which can be viewed as a boundary operator with the identification (
301
). This is the boundary time-translation operator, and acts as (
292
) by construction. It is quadratic in
\pi_{\Omega}({{\mathcal{O}}})
and involves integrals over the boundary spacetime across a time band of width
\pi R
, as implied by (
304
).

For a semiclassical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
whose bulk dual is a geometry with a single asymptotic boundary and no event horizon, the discussion parallels that of the vacuum sector. We next consider the simplest example that includes a horizon.

VI.3
Thermofield double state

Now consider two copies of the boundary CFT, denoted as CFT
R
and CFT
L
, whose Hilbert spaces and Hamiltonians are denoted respectively as
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R,L}
and
H_{R,L}
. The thermofield double (TFD) state
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
is defined at finite
N
as

\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle={1\over\sqrt{Z_{\beta}}}\sum_{n}e^{-{\beta\over 2}E_{n}}\lvert{n}\rangle_{R}\lvert{{\Theta}n}\rangle_{L},\quad Z_{\beta}=\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\ .

(306)

Here
\lvert{n}\rangle
denotes the energy eigenstates with eigenvalues
E_{n}
, and
{\Theta}
represents the
{{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{T}}
operator.
In the TFD state, tracing out one copy of the CFTs (say CFT
L
) yields the thermal density operator

\rho_{\beta}={1\over Z_{\beta}}e^{-\beta H_{R}}
. That is, from the perspective of CFT
R
, the system is in a thermal state with
free energy
F(\beta)=-{1\over\beta}\log Z_{\beta}
.
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
is type I, and

\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to it, with the modular operator given by

-\log{{\Delta}}_{\beta}=\beta(H_{R}-H_{L})\ .

(307)

In the large
N
limit, the system undergoes a first-order transition
at a temperature
T_{\rm HP}
, known as the Hawking-Page temperature
HawPag83
, where the free energy
F(\beta)
jumps from
O(N^{0})
to
O(N^{2})
and the bulk dual transitions from thermal AdS to a black hole geometry
Wit98b
.

More explicitly, for
T<T_{\rm HP}
, the system has free energy of order
O(N^{0})
, and the thermal ensemble is dominated by states with energies of order
O(N^{0})
. From the perspective of the
R
-system, the bulk geometry is described by a thermal gas in global AdS, commonly referred to as thermal AdS.
The full
R
and
L
system can then be described by a
bulk
thermofield double state
\lvert{\psi_{\beta}}\rangle
living in two copies of global AdS
Mal01
,

\lvert{\psi_{\beta}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{Z}_{\beta}}}\sum_{n}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\tilde{E}_{n}}\lvert{\tilde{n}}\rangle_{R}\lvert{\Theta\tilde{n}}\rangle_{L},\quad\tilde{Z}_{\beta}=\sum_{n}e^{-\beta\tilde{E}_{n}},,

(308)

where
\lvert{\tilde{n}}\rangle
and
\tilde{E}_{n}
are the eigenstates and eigenvalues of the bulk Hamiltonian governing excitations around global AdS. See Fig.
9
(a).
Tracing out the
L
-system in (
308
) then yields a thermal gas in global AdS for the
R
-system.

For
T>T_{\rm HP}
, the free energy
F(\beta)
becomes
O(N^{2})
, and the thermal ensemble is dominated by states with energies of order
O(N^{2})
. The bulk dual is an eternal AdS black hole
Mal01
, described by the metric

ds^{2}=-f(\rho)dt^{2}+\frac{d\rho^{2}}{f(\rho)}+\rho^{2}d\Omega^{2}_{d-1},\quad f(\rho)=1+\frac{\rho^{2}}{R^{2}}-\frac{\mu}{\rho^{d-2}}\ .

(309)

See Fig.
9
(b). CFT
R
and CFT
L
live on the two asymptotic boundaries of this connected geometry. The thermal density matrix
\rho_{\beta}=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}e^{-\beta H_{R}}
may be interpreted as dual to the
R
-region—i.e., the exterior of the black hole shown in Fig.
9
(b).

Figure 9
:
(a) For
T<T_{\rm HP}
,

\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is dual to two disconnected global AdS spacetimes entangled in a thermofield double state.
(b) For
T>T_{\rm HP}
,
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is dual to an eternal black hole in AdS.
The black horizontal line represents a Wilson line stretching from the left to the right boundary. Bulk degrees of freedom originating in the
R
and
L
regions of the black hole can cross the horizon—illustrated by arrows—and interact in the
F
-region.

While the identification with the black hole geometry for
T>T_{\rm HP}
had passed many nontrivial checks and was widely regarded as correct beyond reasonable doubt, the following conceptual puzzles had been noted:

1.

Factorization puzzle
.
While the boundary system factorizes as a tensor product of CFT
R
and CFT
L
, the bulk black hole is described by a connected spacetime and does not admit a corresponding factorization. For instance, consider a Wilson line extending from the left boundary to the right in the bulk black hole geometry
Har15
(see Fig.
9
(b)). There appears to be no way to represent the associated boundary operator as a product of operators in
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{L})
.

2.

Meeting-behind-the-horizon puzzle
. While there is no interaction between CFT
R
and CFT
L
, the corresponding bulk degrees of freedom originating from the
R
and
L
regions of the black hole can, however, “meet”—and thus interact—after crossing the horizon
MarWal12
(see Fig.
9
(b)). This puzzle is intimately connected to the boundary realization of time evolution generated by a Kruskal-like flow—i.e., evolution capable of traversing the horizon.

These puzzles can be addressed by formulating the duality from an algebraic perspective. In this subsection, we focus on the factorization puzzle, deferring the discussion of the meeting-behind-the-horizon puzzle to Sec.
VIII.2
, where we explore the emergence of the bulk horizon from the boundary theory.

To set the stage, we first examine the
T<T_{\rm HP}
phase more closely. In this regime, the large-
N
limit of TFD state
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
can be understood by taking the
N\to\infty
limit directly in the sum of (
306
). States with energies
E_{n}
that scale nontrivially with
N
drop out of the sum due to exponential suppression from the Boltzmann factor
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta E_{n}}
. The remaining contributions come entirely from states with
E_{n}\sim O(N^{0})
, which, in the large-
N
limit, can be interpreted as elements of the vacuum-sector GNS Hilbert spaces
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{R}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{L}
of CFT
R
and CFT
L
, respectively.

Denote the single-trace operator algebras from the CFT
R
and CFT
L
as
{\mathcal{S}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{L}
. The GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}
, obtained from acting with
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{R}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}_{L}
on
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
, factorizes as

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}={{\mathcal{H}}}^{R}_{\Omega}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{L}\ .

(310)

Denoting

{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=(\pi_{\Psi_{\beta}}({\mathcal{S}}_{R}))^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}=(\pi_{\Psi_{\beta}}({\mathcal{S}}_{L}))^{\prime\prime},

(311)

we can further identify

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}^{R}_{\Omega}),\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}^{L}_{\Omega})\ .

(312)

Thus, at such temperatures, where the entanglement between CFT
R
and CFT
L
is of order
O(N^{0})
,

{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
—the large
N
limit of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
—remains type I.

Given the identification
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm AdS}^{(\rm Fock)}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)}
, equation (
310
) then implies that
the gravity dual of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}
corresponds to two copies of global AdS, with the identification

\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\beta}=\lvert{\psi_{\beta}}\rangle,\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}\ .

(313)

where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
denote the bulk operator algebras associated with the right and left global AdS spacetimes, respectively.

Now consider
T>T_{\rm HP}
. With the thermal ensemble dominated by states of energies of order
O(N^{2})
, the expression (
306
) becomes ill-defined in the
N\to\infty
limit as neither the energies nor the associated eigenstates admit a well-defined limit. Moreover, a single-trace operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}=\mathop{\rm Tr}(\cdots)\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
can have a divergent one-point function in the state
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
,

{\left\langle{\Psi_{\beta}|{{\mathcal{O}}}|\Psi_{\beta}}\right\rangle}\sim O(N),\quad N\to\infty\ .

(314)

Nevertheless, we can define the
N\to\infty
limit of the TFD state (
306
)
in terms of the limiting behavior of its correlation functions.
To this end, we introduce “renormalized” single-trace operators by subtracting their thermal expectation values

\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}\equiv{{\mathcal{O}}}-{\left\langle{\Psi_{\beta}|{{\mathcal{O}}}|\Psi_{\beta}}\right\rangle},

(315)

and define the algebra
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(R)}
(
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(L)}
) as being generated by such operators.
From the large-
N
behavior of thermal correlation functions, we have

{\left\langle{\Psi_{\beta}|\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}|\Psi_{\beta}}\right\rangle}=0,\quad{\left\langle{\Psi_{\beta}\left|\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}(x_{1})\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{2}(x_{2})\right|\Psi_{\beta}}\right\rangle}\sim O(N^{0}),\quad N\to\infty\ .

(316)

Furthermore, higher-point functions factorize into products of two-point functions, as in (
297
).

We can build a GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}
using
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(L)}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(R)}
.
Due to the large
N
factorization, at the leading order, we have a generalized free field theory and the GNS Hilbert space has the structure of a Fock space. It can be checked that the state
\omega_{\beta}
defined by expectation values of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}
in
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is pure with respect to
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}
, and thus
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}
coincides with the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}
generated by products of single-trace operators. Denoting

{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=(\pi_{\Psi_{\beta}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(R)}))^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}=(\pi_{\Psi_{\beta}}({\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}^{(L)}))^{\prime\prime},

(317)

we have

{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\prime}={\mathcal{Y}}_{L},\quad{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)})={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\lor{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}\ .

(318)

Now the nature of
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
, and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}
is qualitatively different: the entanglement between CFT
R
and CFT
L
becomes of order
O(N^{2})
. As a result, it has been argued that
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
—the large-
N
limit of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
—becomes a type III
1
von Neumann algebra
LeuLiu21a
;
LeuLiu21b
, and the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}
no longer factorizes into separate components associated with CFT
R
and CFT
L
.

On the gravity side, small perturbations around the black hole geometry can be described using the standard formalism of quantum field theory in a curved spacetime.
Quantizing these perturbations yields a Fock space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm BH}^{\rm(Fock)}
, with the corresponding vacuum state given by the Hartle-Hawking vacuum
\lvert{HH}\rangle
. We then have the identifications

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi_{\beta}}^{(\rm GNS)}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm BH}^{\rm(Fock)},\quad\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\beta}=\lvert{HH}\rangle,

(319)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L},

(320)

where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
are respectively the bulk operator algebras associated with the
R
and
L
exterior regions
the black hole. The identifications (
320
) can be made explicitly by matching the bulk QFT oscillators with those of the boundary generalized free field theory.
Since
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{R}
and
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{L}
are operator algebras associated with subregions in a continuum QFT, they are of type III
1
, matching the type of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}
.

Thus, the non-factorization of the bulk dual description in terms of the black hole geometry can be directly understood as a consequence of the change in the large-
N
limit of the boundary algebra, induced by the divergent entanglement in this limit.

We close this subsection with a few additional remarks:

1.

Similar to (
295
) and (
299
) in the vacuum sector, we can express
a single-trace operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{R,L}
in CFT
R
or CFT
L
in terms of a mode expansion as

{{\mathcal{O}}}_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{k}v_{k}^{({\alpha})}(x)a_{k}^{({\alpha})},\quad{\alpha}=R,L,\quad k=(\omega,{\vec{k}}),\quad a_{k}^{(R)}\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\beta}=e^{-{\beta\omega\over 2}}a_{-k}^{(L)}\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\beta},

(321)

where
\omega
is the frequency and
{\vec{k}}
denotes the spatial momentum. From the identifications (
320
), the oscillators
a_{k}^{(R,L)}
are identified with those for the corresponding bulk field
\phi_{R,L}
in the
R
and
L
regions,

\phi_{\alpha}(X)=\sum_{k}u_{k}^{({\alpha})}(X)a_{k}^{({\alpha})},\quad{\alpha}=R,L,\quad k=(\omega,{\vec{k}})\ .

(322)

2.

Although the bulk dual of
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is already known, one could have deduced its key geometric features purely from the algebraic structure: the fact that
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R,L}
are type I for
T<T_{\rm HP}
and become type III
1
for
T>T_{\rm HP}
implies that the bulk geometry must be disconnected in the former case and connected in the latter. This forms the basis of the algebraic formulation of ER=EPR, which we will elaborate on in Sec.
VIII.3
.

3.

For
T>T_{\rm HP}
, each temperature corresponds to a distinct sector with a non-overlapping GNS Hilbert space. This is because the states
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
at different values of
\beta
have entanglement entropies that differ by
O(N^{2})
, and are thus separated by an infinite amount of entanglement in the large-
N
limit.
In contrast, for
T<T_{\rm HP}
, there exists a single GNS Hilbert space common to all
\beta
, with the states
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\beta}
representing different vectors within the same Hilbert space.

4.

At any temperature, the CFT Hamiltonians
H_{R}
and
H_{L}
do not belong to the operator algebras in the large-
N
limit, and therefore equation (
307
) no longer holds. However, the modular operator
\Delta_{\beta}
does survive the large-
N
limit and continues to generate time translations on the two boundaries (in opposite directions).
For
T<T_{\rm HP}
, the modular operator factorizes as

-\log\Delta_{\beta}=\beta(\hat{h}_{R}-\hat{h}_{L}),

(323)

where
\hat{h}
denotes the bulk Hamiltonian of quantum fields in global AdS, and corresponds to the time-translation operator in the boundary generalized free field theory in the vacuum sector (recall the discussion around (
305
)).
For
T>T_{\rm HP}
, by contrast, the modular operator no longer factorizes, reflecting the emergent III
1
structure of boundary algebras.

VI.4
General black holes

We now consider an example in which there exist operators, in addition to single-trace operators, that survive the large-
N
limit; that is, the inclusion (
280
) is strict.

The thermofield double state is special due to the presence of a bifurcating horizon. This is no longer the case for a general two-sided semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
—the large-
N
limit of a certain entangled state in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
—which is dual to a “long” black hole, as illustrated in Fig.
10
. For simplicity, we have taken the geometry to be time-reflection and left-right symmetric; the discussion readily generalizes to less symmetric cases.

Figure 10
:
A long two-sided black hole. The black hole interior region is labeled by
I
.

As in the TFD state above the Hawking-Page temperature, we can define “renormalized” single-trace algebras
{\mathcal{S}}^{(R)}_{\Psi}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{(L)}_{\Psi}
for the CFT
R
and CFT
L
, respectively. In analogy with the identification (
320
), by studying the bulk QFT outside the horizon, we can establish

{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\equiv(\pi_{\Psi}({\mathcal{S}}^{(R)}_{\Psi}))^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R},\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}\equiv(\pi_{\Psi}({\mathcal{S}}^{(L)}_{\Psi}))^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L},

(324)

where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
denote the bulk operator algebras associated with the
R
and
L
exterior regions of the black hole. However, unlike in the TFD case, the
R
and
L
regions no longer cover a full Cauchy slice. Bulk operators in the interior region
I
—as indicated in Fig.
10
—should still correspond to boundary operators that survive the large-
N
limit, but they do not belong to the algebra of single-trace operators.

Thus, in this case we must have

{\mathcal{S}}\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}\ .

(325)

The additional operators required to describe bulk operators in region
I
must be related in some way to single-trace operators, since a bulk field
\phi
in region
R
and in region
I
represents the same underlying field.
In Sec.
VII.2
, we will see that these extra operators can be related to single-trace operators via modular flow.

A similar conclusion of
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
applies to a “long” single-sided black hole, illustrated in Fig.
11
(a): bulk operators in the interior region
I
should correspond to boundary operators that survive the large-
N
limit, but do not belong to the algebra of single-trace operators. However, the case of a single-sided black hole formed from gravitational collapse (see
Fig.
11
(b)) is different: as we will see later in Sec.
VII.5
, one finds that
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}={\mathcal{S}}
.

Figure 11
:
(a) A “long” single-sided black hole. The vertical dashed line marks the location where the spacetime either ends smoothly or terminates at an end-of-world brane.
(b) A black hole spacetime formed from gravitational collapse.

VI.5
A diagnostic of firewalls in generic highly excited states

So far, we have considered semi-classical states—states that admit a semi-classical bulk description. Now, consider a generic highly excited state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
with energy
E_{\Psi}\sim O(N^{2})
. At such energies, the boundary theory is expected to be chaotic, and a generic state should exhibit thermal behavior in the large-
N
limit. That is, correlation functions of single-trace operators are expected to satisfy

{\left\langle{\Psi|{{\mathcal{O}}}|\Psi}\right\rangle}\approx{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}}\right\rangle}_{\beta},\quad{\left\langle{\Psi|{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{2}\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{n}|\Psi}\right\rangle}\approx{\left\langle{{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{2}\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{n}}\right\rangle}_{\beta}

(326)

to leading order in the
1/N
expansion, where the right-hand sides represent thermal correlation functions at inverse temperature
\beta
. The value of
\beta
is determined by energy matching:
E_{\Psi}=E_{\beta}
, where
E_{\beta}
is the energy of the thermal density matrix at temperature
\beta
.

Equations (
326
) suggest that the state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
admits a geometric description corresponding to the exterior region of a black hole. But does it also admit a smooth geometric extension behind the horizon, as in the case of Fig.
11
? Alternatively, the interior geometry may not exist, in which case we say there is a firewall at the horizon.

As discussed in the previous subsection, describing the portion of a Cauchy slice behind the horizon requires the existence of operators in the large-
N
limit that lie outside the single-trace operator algebra.
This is a highly nontrivial condition. If the state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
does not satisfy it, then a smooth bulk geometry behind the horizon cannot be defined, and there is a “firewall” at the horizon. See Fig.
12
.

The diagnostic can be stated more formally as follows:

Suppose a generic excited state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
satisfies (
326
) and
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}={\mathcal{S}}
. Then the bulk dual corresponds to a black hole with a “firewall” at the horizon.

Note that in this case
\omega_{\Psi}
is a mixed state with respect to
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
, in violation of (
281
).

Similar statements apply to generic two-sided states in
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{L}
. Moreover, for a general two-sided state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, in addition to (
326
) (where all operators there should be understood to belong to a single CFT), we also expect

{\left\langle{\Psi|{{\mathcal{O}}}_{R}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}|\Psi}\right\rangle}_{c}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty,\quad{{\mathcal{O}}}_{R}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\Psi}^{(R)},\ {{\mathcal{O}}}_{L}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\Psi}^{(L)}\ ,

(327)

which is consistent with the interpretation that no smooth, connected geometry exists between the
L
and
R
boundaries.

Figure 12
:
Generic states do not have a smooth geometric beyond the horizon, i.e., there is a firewall at the horizon.

VI.6
Perturbative
1/N
corrections: conserved charges and bulk nonlocality

So far we have restricted to the leading order in the
G_{N}
expansion.
On the gravity side, perturbative
G_{N}
corrections can be obtained by including higher order terms in (
284
)–(
285
). The bulk field theory is now interacting, with effects of interactions treated perturbatively.
On the boundary, three-point and higher-point functions are non-vanishing, suppressed by powers of
1/N
, resulting in a generalized field theory with interactions.
To any finite order in the
1/N
(or
G_{N}
) perturbative expansion, the leading-order characterization of the algebras remains valid, as the spectrum of the modular operator is dominated by its zeroth-order contribution.
38
38

38

This is analogous to the standard perturbative treatment of
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory, where the Hilbert space at any finite order in perturbation theory remains that of the free theory.
For example, in the TFD state above the Hawking-Page temperature,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}
should still be type III
1
.

In this subsection we discuss two new features from
1/N
(or
G_{N}
) perturbative corrections. The first deals with
1/N
corrections to the relation (
303
) between bulk and boundary operators, while the second concerns boundary conserved quantities, which are closely tied to bulk nonlocal behavior arising from Gauss laws.
For definiteness, we will consider the vacuum sector. A similar discussion can be applied to other sectors.

VI.6.1
1/N
corrections to bulk reconstruction

One immediate consequence of including higher order corrections in (
284
) is that the relation (
303
) between bulk and boundary operators becomes more complicated
KabLif11
;
KabLif13
. As an illustration here we consider a simple example of a real scalar field
\phi
dual to a boundary operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}
. Suppose
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
in (
284
)–(
285
)
for
\phi
have the form

S_{2}=-{{\frac{1}{2}}}\int d^{d+1}X\,\sqrt{-g}\left((\partial\phi)^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2}\right),\quad S_{3}=-{1\over 3}\int d^{d+1}X\sqrt{-g}\,\phi^{3}+\cdots

(328)

where
\cdots
include couplings of
\phi
to other fields, such as metric perturbations.
The equation of motion for
\phi
is then given by

\partial^{2}\phi=m^{2}\phi+\kappa\phi^{2}+\cdots\ .

(329)

We can expand the above equation perturbatively in
\kappa
as

\displaystyle\phi=\phi^{(0)}+\kappa\phi^{(1)}+\cdots

(330)

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}-m^{2})\phi^{(0)}=0,\quad(\partial^{2}-m^{2})\phi^{(1)}=(\phi^{(0)})^{2},

(331)

and
\phi^{(1)}
can be solved as
39
39

39

The homogenous solution to the equation for
\phi^{(1)}
can be set to zero by absorbing it into the definition of
\phi^{(0)}
, and
(\phi^{(0)}(X^{\prime}))^{2}
should be understood as being normal ordered.

\phi^{(1)}(X)=\int d^{d+1}X^{\prime}\sqrt{-g}\,G(X,X^{\prime})(\phi^{(0)}(X^{\prime}))^{2},

(332)

where
G(X,X^{\prime})
is a bulk propagator for
\phi
. The zeroth order equation (
331
) leads to (
303
), and (
332
) then gives

\displaystyle\phi(X)=\int d^{d}x\,K(X;x){{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}(x)+\kappa\int d^{d}x_{1}d^{d}x_{2}\,K(X;x_{1},x_{2}){{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}(x_{1}){{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}(x_{2})+\cdots,

(333)

\displaystyle K(X;x_{1},x_{2})\equiv\int d^{d+1}X^{\prime}\sqrt{-g}\,G(X,X^{\prime})K(X^{\prime};x_{1})K(X^{\prime};x_{2}),

(334)

where we have added a superscript to
{{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}
to stress that it is the generalized free field dual to
\phi^{(0)}
.
Including higher order corrections, we expect the following expansion in
1/N
,

\displaystyle\phi(X)

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}x\,K(X;x){{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}(x)

(335)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over N^{n}}\int d^{d}x_{1}\cdots d^{d}x_{n+1}\,K_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n+1}}(X;x_{1},\cdots x_{n+1}){{\mathcal{O}}}_{i_{1}}^{(0)}(x_{1})\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{i_{n+1}}^{(0)}(x_{n+1})\ .

Applying the boundary limit of (
335
) to (
277
), we then find that

{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)={{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{1\over N^{n}}\int d^{d}x_{1}\cdots d^{d}x_{n+1}\,\tilde{K}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n+1}}(x;x_{1},\cdots x_{n+1}){{\mathcal{O}}}_{i_{1}}^{(0)}(x_{1})\cdots{{\mathcal{O}}}_{i_{n+1}}^{(0)}(x_{n+1}),

(336)

where
\tilde{K}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n+1}}(x;x_{1},\cdots x_{n+1})
is obtained from
K_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n+1}}(X;x_{1},\cdots x_{n+1})
by taking
X
to the boundary. Equation (
336
) gives the expansion of the single-trace operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)
perturbatively in terms of generalized free fields
{{\mathcal{O}}}^{(0)}_{i}
.

VI.6.2
Conserved charges and bulk nonlocality

We now discuss qualitative new features concerning conserved quantities when including
1/N
corrections.
We will first discuss the boundary story (emphasized in
Wit21b
), and then the corresponding bulk picture.

The boundary CFT possesses global symmetries, including conformal symmetries and potentially additional internal symmetries.
As an illustration, we assume that the CFT has a
U(1)
internal symmetry with a corresponding conserved current
J^{\mu}
and conserved charge (
\sigma
is a boundary Cauchy slice)

Q=\int_{\sigma}d^{d-1}x\,J^{0}(x)\ .

(337)

Acting on an operator
{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)
of charge
1
, we have

i[Q,{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)]={{\mathcal{O}}}(x)\ .

(338)

In close parallel with the discussion of the Hamiltonian below (
288
), the conserved current

J^{\mu}
takes the form
N\mathop{\rm Tr}(\cdots)
. As a result, only the rescaled operators

\hat{J}^{\mu}\equiv\frac{1}{N}J^{\mu},\quad\hat{Q}\equiv\frac{Q}{N}

(339)

survive in the large-
N
limit. The commutator of
\hat{Q}
with a single-trace operator is suppressed by
1/N

i[\hat{Q},{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)]=\frac{1}{N}{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)\ .

(340)

In the boundary generalized free field theory, there should exist an operator
\hat{q}
that generates charge rotations via

i[\hat{q},{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)]={{\mathcal{O}}}(x)\ .

(341)

As in the case of the Hamiltonian,
\hat{q}
cannot be written as an integral of a local operator over a single Cauchy slice.

The
U(1)
global symmetry on the boundary is dual to the asymptotic symmetry associated with a bulk
U(1)
gauge symmetry. In empty AdS in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit, this asymptotic symmetry extends to the full bulk as the global part of the gauge symmetry, and
\hat{q}
corresponds to the Noether charge associated with this bulk global symmetry.

Now consider going beyond the leading order in the
G_{N}
(or
1/N
) expansion.
Equation (
340
) implies that the bulk theory should develop some
nonlocal
behavior. More explicitly, suppose
A_{M}=(A_{z},A_{\mu})
is the bulk
U(1)
gauge field dual to the boundary current
\hat{J}^{\mu}
, we have the extrapolation dictionary
40
40

40

For convenience we use the Poincare coordinates near the boundary (
z=0
).

\hat{J}^{\mu}(x)=\lim_{z\to 0}j^{\mu}(z,x),\quad j^{\mu}(z,x)\equiv\sqrt{-g}F^{z\mu}(z,x),

(342)

where
F_{MN}
is the field strength associated with
A_{M}
. Suppose
\phi
is the charged scalar dual to
{{\mathcal{O}}}
. From (
340
) and (
303
),
we have

i[\hat{Q},\phi(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})]={1\over N}\phi(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})\propto\kappa\phi(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})\ .

(343)

Taking
\sigma
in (
337
) to be on the same bulk Cauchy slice as
(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, from (
342
), we find that (
343
) requires nonzero commutator between
F^{z\mu}(z,x)
and
\phi(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})
at
spacelike separations
.

Equation (
343
) can be understood on the gravity side as a consequence bulk gauge symmetries and the corresponding Gauss law constraints.
More explicitly, consider the lowest two orders of the bulk action of
\phi
and
A_{M}
, which should have the form

\displaystyle S=S_{2}+\kappa S_{3}+O(\kappa^{2}),\quad S_{2}=-\int d^{d+1}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\left[{1\over 4}F_{MN}F^{MN}+|\partial\phi|^{2}+m^{2}|\phi|^{2}\right],

(344)

\displaystyle S_{3}=A_{M}J^{M}+\cdots,\quad J^{M}=i\left(\phi^{*}\partial^{M}\phi-\phi\partial^{M}\phi^{*}\right)\ .

(345)

J^{M}
is the Noether current for the
U(1)
global symmetry of
S_{2}
. In
S_{3}
we suppressed other possible cubic terms, e.g., the interactions between
\phi
and metric perturbations.
Equations of motion for the gauge field are

\displaystyle{\nabla}_{M}F^{MN}=\kappa J^{N}+O(\kappa^{2}),

(346)

and gauge transformations have the form

A_{M}\to A_{M}+\partial_{M}\lambda,\quad\phi\to\phi^{\prime}=e^{i\kappa\lambda}\phi=\phi+i\kappa\lambda\phi+O(\kappa^{2}),

(347)

where the gauge parameter
\lambda(z,x)
should go to zero approaching the boundary
z=0
.
\phi(z,x)
is not gauge invariant, but we can construct a gauge invariant operator by attaching to it a Wilson line going to the boundary, for example,

\Phi(z,x)=e^{-i\kappa V}\phi(z,x)=\phi(z,x)-i\kappa V\phi+O(\kappa^{2}),\quad V=\int_{0}^{z}dz^{\prime}\,A_{z}(z^{\prime},x)\ .

(348)

\Phi(z,x)
is known as a dressed observable, and it exhibits nonlocal commutation relations due to the inherent nonlocality of the Wilson line used in its construction.

Alternatively, we can fix a convenient gauge; in any gauge that completely fixes the gauge freedom,
\phi(z,x)
corresponds to a gauge invariant observable. More explicitly, suppose a gauge has been fixed.
Solving (
346
) then leads to a solution of the form

A_{M}(X)=A_{M}^{(0)}(X)+\kappa\int d^{d+1}X\,\sqrt{-g}\,G_{MN}(X,X^{\prime})J^{N}(X^{\prime})+\cdots

(349)

where
G_{MN}(X,X^{\prime})
is a propagator for the gauge field.
The second term of (
349
) can then lead to nonlocal commutation relation of the form (
343
) (see e.g.
Hee12
for details). As in (
336
), the boundary limit of (
349
) leads to a corrected extrapolation dictionary of the form

\hat{Q}=\hat{Q}^{(0)}+{1\over N}\hat{q}+\cdots,

(350)

where
\hat{Q}^{(0)}
denotes the corresponding operator at the generalized free field level.

Parallel discussions can be given by time translations and other spacetime symmetries in which case we need to deal with bulk diffeomorphisms. We can either consider dressed observables by attaching to
\phi(z,x)
a “gravitational Wilson line” going to the boundary, or work with a specific gauge. In the latter case, we have a rather parallel story to (
346
) and (
349
), with (
346
) replaced by the Einstein equations and
J^{N}
by the bulk stress tensor of
\phi
. In particular, we expect the
\hat{H}
(as defined in (
290
)) to have the
1/N
expansion

\hat{H}=\hat{H}^{(0)}+{1\over N}\hat{h}_{\Omega}+\cdots\ .

(351)

VII
Subregion-subalgebra duality

The algebraic formulation of AdS/CFT in the large-
N
limit, discussed in the previous section, leads directly to a simple but powerful principle: the subregion-subalgebra duality
LeuLiu22
, which
equates
an arbitrary bulk spacetime subregion with an emergent type III
1
von Neumann subalgebra of the boundary CFT.

In this section, we first present the general formulation of the subregion-subalgebra duality, followed by several classes of examples—including a reformulation of entanglement wedge reconstruction. We then introduce a general theorem that provides a powerful method for identifying bulk subregions with boundary subalgebras. Finally, we argue that if the generalized entropy of a bulk region admits a finite-
N
extension, then the corresponding subalgebra in the duality should extend to a type I algebra at finite
N
.

The subregion-subalgebra duality offers a framework for addressing long-standing questions about the emergence of bulk locality and the reconstruction of general bulk regions. We explore its further implications for holography in Sec.
VIII
.

VII.1
General formulation

In Sec.
VI.1
, we discussed how the boundary theory Hilbert space separates into disjoint sectors in the large
N
limit, each corresponding to a distinct bulk geometry. The Fock space obtained by quantizing perturbations around a given bulk geometry can then be identified with the GNS Hilbert space of the corresponding boundary state, as expressed in (
286
).

The identification (
286
) implies that

{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)})={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})\ .

(352)

Note that
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)})
is defined on a single bulk Cauchy slice, while
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})
is defined for the full boundary spacetime (or at least a finite time band as in (
304
)), see Fig.
13
.
Since the bulk has one more dimension than the boundary,
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)})
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})
depend on the same number of dimensions.

Figure 13
:
For a general asymptotic AdS geometry, the bulk algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)})
can be defined on a single bulk Cauchy slice
\Sigma
, while the boundary algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})
is defined for the full boundary spacetime. In the plot, the vertical line represents the boundary.

At the quadratic level, we can diagonalize bulk fields so that different fields decouple from one another. As a result, both
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)}
and
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})
exhibit a tensor product structure, decomposing into factors corresponding to these different fields (labeled by
i
)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)}=\otimes_{i}{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{(\rm GNS)},\quad{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)})=\otimes_{i}{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{(\rm GNS)}),

(353)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{(\rm Fock)}={{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{(\rm GNS)},\quad{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{\rm(Fock)})={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi,i}^{(\rm GNS)})\ .

(354)

Equation (
352
) further implies that operator subalgebras acting on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)}
and
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})}
must have one-to-one correspondence. In particular, consider an open subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}
on a bulk Cauchy slice, and the von Neumann algebra
41
41

41

All the bulk subalgebras are denoted with a
\widetilde{}
to distinguish them from boundary algebras.

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}\subset{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)})
associated with the region. Then there must be a corresponding boundary subalgebra

{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}\subset{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})})
that is identified with it, i.e.,

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}={{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}\ .

(355)

The equivalence (
355
) implies that the boundary subalgebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
captures all the bulk operations which one can perform in
{\mathfrak{b}}
, and from our discussion of Sec.
IV.4
,
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
also encodes the causal structure, geometric information, as well as the entanglement structure of
{\mathfrak{b}}
. For example, entanglement of a bulk subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}
with its causal complement is captured by eigenvalues of the modular operators (entanglement Hamiltonian), which can be probed by modular flows. The equivalence (
355
) identifies the bulk modular operator associated with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
with the boundary modular operator for
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
. We can then obtain the bulk entanglement structure from modular flows of
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
.

We therefore conclude that a bulk subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}
can be fully “reconstructed” using the corresponding boundary algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
.
This is the
subregion-subalgebra duality
.

We have already encountered examples of subregion-subalgebra duality in Sec.
VI.3
and Sec.
VI.4
(e.g. (
320
) and (
324
)), where the region outside a black hole was identified with the single-trace operator algebra on the corresponding boundary.

While the statement itself may appear simple, it provides a powerful framework to reconstruct the bulk system from the boundary. Furthermore, as we will see later, it may be used to “define” the bulk subregions and causal structure in the stringy regime when we take the boundary theory to be at finite ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
.
The subregion-subalgebra duality also leads to highly nontrivial requirements on the boundary system, implying that, in the large
N
limit,
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{({\rm GNS})})
must be rich enough to contain all the subalgebras associated to local bulk subregions.

Since at the leading order in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit, the bulk is described by a quantum field theory in a curved spacetime,
we assume that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
satisfies various general properties discussed in Sec.
IV.4
:

1.

It obeys the bulk Reeh-Schlieder theorem, i.e., the “vacuum”
\lvert{0}\rangle_{\phi_{c}}\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(Fock)}
is cyclic and separating with respect to
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
. This implies that the GNS vacuum
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Psi}
is cyclic and separating with respect to
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
.

2.

The algebras for different regions respect (bulk) causality, i.e.

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{b}}

(356)

where
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}
is the bulk domain of dependence of
{\mathfrak{b}}
. Hence, the corresponding boundary subalgebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
can in fact reconstruct
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}
.

3.

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
is type III
1
, which implies that the corresponding boundary algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
must also be type III
1
.

4.

For topologically trivial regions
{\mathfrak{b}}_{1},~{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}
, the algebras are additive,

\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}\cup{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}}=\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}}\vee\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}}\ .

(357)

5.

The bulk algebras satisfy the Haag duality (for topologically trivial regions)

\displaystyle\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{b^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\right)^{\prime},

(358)

where
{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}
denotes the complement of
{\mathfrak{b}}
on its bulk Cauchy slice.

We will see later that these properties have important boundary implications.

VII.2
Entanglement wedge reconstruction: algebraic reformulation

Two immediate classes of examples of subregion-subalgebra duality are provided by the entanglement wedge and causal wedge reconstructions associated with a boundary region. The entanglement wedge reconstruction states that the physics in the entanglement wedge of a boundary subregion
A
(on a Cauchy slice) can be fully described by that in
A

Van09
;
CzeKar12
;
CzeKar12b
;
Wal12
;
HeaHub14
;
AlmDon14
;
JafSuh14
;
PasYos15
;
JafLew15
;
DonHar16
;
Har16
;
FauLew17
;
CotHay17
. Causal wedge reconstructions
BanDou98
;
Ben99
;
BalKraLaw98
;
HamKab05
;
HamKab06
;
KabLif11
;
Hee12
;
HeeMar12
;
PapRaj12
;
Mor14
;
Hub14
;
EngPen21a
;
Wit23
, also known as local HKLL reconstructions, express bulk local operators in the causal wedge of
A
in terms of single-trace operators supported in the domain of dependence of
A
.

Below, we reformulate these statements in algebraic terms
42
42

42

The entanglement wedge reconstruction has also been formulated in algebraic terms within the framework of holographic quantum error correction
Har16
;
KanKol18
;
Fau20
;
KanKol21
;
GesKan21
;
FauLi22
, which we will comment on in Sec.
VII.3.2
.
as special cases of subregion-subalgebra duality
LeuLiu21b
;
LeuLiu22
, and highlight several new insights that emerge from this perspective in subsequent subsections.

VII.2.1
Entanglement and causal wedge reconstructions

For illustration, consider a
static
asymptotic AdS spacetime corresponding to a semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.

A
is a boundary subregion lying on a constant time bulk Cauchy slice
\Sigma
. Denote
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
as the bulk subregion between
A
and the Ryu-Takayanagi (RT) surface
{{\gamma}}_{A}
on the Cauchy slice. See Fig.
14
. The entanglement wedge of
A

is the bulk domain of dependence of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
, which we denote as
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
.

Figure 14
:
A constant-time Cauchy slice
\Sigma
of a static asymptotic AdS is shown, with the boundary indicated by the black circle.

{{\gamma}}_{A}
is the RT surface of the boundary subregion
A
and the region between
{{\gamma}}_{A}
and
A
is the bulk dual subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
of
A
. The entanglement wedge
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
(not shown in figure) is the (bulk) domain of dependence of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
.

Since physics of a region can be fully described by the operator algebra in the region, the entanglement wedge reconstruction can be stated algebraically as

{\mathcal{X}}_{A}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}},

(359)

where
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}\subset{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{\rm(GNS)})
denotes the boundary algebra in the region
A
in the large
N
limit.

The definition of
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
requires some elaboration. At finite
N
, we have a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
of operators localized in
A
, which acts on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT}
, and satisfies various properties discussed in Sec.
IV.4
. In particular, we have

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}={{\mathcal{B}}}_{\hat{A}}^{(N)}\ .

(360)

As
N\to\infty
, many operators in
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
may not have a sensible limit and drop out of the large
N
theory, so are not the right objects to consider. We define
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
as
LeuLiu22

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{A}=\left(\pi_{\Psi}(\lim_{N\to\infty,\Psi}{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)})\right)^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{X}}_{\hat{A}},

(361)

where
\lim_{N\to\infty,\Psi}{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
is the collection of operators in
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
that survive the large
N
limit in
the semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
43
43

43

\lim_{N\to\infty,\Psi}{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
is the counterpart of
{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Psi}
, introduced in Sec.
VI.1
, but restricted to the region
A
.
From (
360
),
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}={\mathcal{X}}_{\tilde{A}}={\mathcal{X}}_{\hat{A}}
where
\tilde{A}
is any Cauchy slice of
\hat{A}
.

There is another algebra that can be naturally associated with
A
in the large
N
limit:
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
(recall (
282
)), which is the von Neumann algebra generated by single-trace operators localized in the domain of dependence
\hat{A}
of
A
.
Since the collection of single-trace operators restricted to
\hat{A}
always survives the large
N
limit, we have

{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{A}\ .

(362)

The causal wedge reconstruction states that bulk local operators in the causal wedge of
\hat{A}
can be expressed in terms of single-trace operators supported in
\hat{A}
, i.e., elements of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
. For example, a bulk field
\Phi(X)
in the causal wedge of
A
can be expressed as

\Phi(X)=\int_{\hat{A}}d^{d}x\,{{\mathcal{K}}}_{A}^{(C)}(X;x)\pi_{\Psi}({{\mathcal{O}}}(x)),\quad X\in{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}},\;x\in\hat{A},

(363)

where
{{\mathcal{O}}}(x)
is the boundary single-trace operator dual to
\Phi
, and
{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}=\left(\tilde{J}^{+}(\hat{A})\cap\tilde{J}^{-}(\hat{A})\right)^{\prime\prime}
is the causal wedge of
\hat{A}
.
44
44

44

Here
\tilde{J}^{\pm}(Y)
denotes the causal future/past of a region
Y
in the conformal completion
of the bulk spacetime, and the double prime denotes the (bulk) causal completion. Our definition corresponds to the causal completion of what is usually referred to as the causal wedge
HubRan12
.

{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(C)}_{A}(X;x)
is a kernel, which should in general be understood as a distribution and be invertible when
X
is restricted to a Cauchy slice of
{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}
.
We can therefore identify

\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\ .

(364)

Equations (
320
) and (
324
) are special cases of (
364
), with
A
taken to be the full
R
or
L
boundary, respectively.

For highly symmetric boundary regions
A
, such as a half-space or a spherical region, the causal wedge reconstruction—and thus (
364
)—can be demonstrated explicitly by solving bulk equations of motion with appropriate boundary conditions at

\hat{A}

HamKab05
;
HamKab06
;
Mor14
.
For more general regions
A
, it can be established using the time-like tube theorem
Bor61
;
Ara63
;
StrWit23b
;
Str00
, as shown in
Wit23
.

From (
359
) and (
364
), equation (
362
) is consistent with the geometric fact that
the causal wedge always lies inside the entanglement wedge
Wal12
;
HeaHub14
,

{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}\subseteq\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}\ .

(365)

VII.2.2
Modular flows and emergent type III
1
structure

Equation (
361
) provides an abstract definition that, by itself, does not specify which additional operators—beyond those in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
—are present, if any. On the gravity side, it is known that the causal wedge is generally a proper subregion of the entanglement wedge, except in special cases such as spherical boundary regions in the vacuum state. Therefore, based on their gravitational identifications, we expect that
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
is, in general, a proper subset of
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
.

Using Lemma
IV.1
stated at the end of Sec.
IV.1
, we can in fact show that the full entanglement wedge—and therefore
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
—can be generated from single-trace operators via modular flow. More explicitly, since the entanglement wedge is a bulk local region, the state
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Psi}
is cyclic and separating with respect to the algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}={\mathcal{X}}_{A}
. Let the corresponding modular operator be denoted by
\Delta_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}
.
45
45

45

Note from (
359
), we also have

{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}=\widetilde{{\Delta}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}
, where
\widetilde{{\Delta}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}
is the bulk modular operator for
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}
.

From Lemma
IV.1
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
can be generated by applying modular flow to any subalgebra of it for which
\lvert{{\mathbf{1}}}\rangle_{\Psi}
is cyclic and separating. One choice is the causal wedge algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
. Alternatively, we may choose a smaller subalgebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}^{\epsilon}
—the single-trace operator algebra supported in an infinitesimal time band of width
\epsilon
within
\hat{A}
centered around
A
, as illustrated in Fig.
15
. Then
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
can be written as

{\mathcal{X}}_{A}=\text{the algebra generated by}\;\{{{\mathcal{O}}}(s,{\vec{x}})\equiv{{\Delta}}^{-is}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{\epsilon}({\vec{x}}){{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}^{is},\;\;{\vec{x}}\in A,s\in{\mathbb{{R}}}\},

(366)

where
{{\mathcal{O}}}({\vec{x}})
denotes single-trace operators in
A
,
and
