{{\mathcal{O}}}_{\epsilon}
means that it should be smeared within a time band of width
\epsilon
around
A
.
For notational simplicity we have suppressed
\pi_{\Psi}
on the RHS of (
366
).
Using (
366
), we can write (
359
) more explicitly as

\Phi(X)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}ds\!\int_{A}d{\vec{x}}\,{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(E)}_{A}(X;s,{\vec{x}}){{\mathcal{O}}}(s;{\vec{x}}),\quad X\in\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}

(367)

for some kernel
{{\mathcal{K}}}^{(E)}_{A}(X;s,{\vec{x}})
which should be understood as a distribution. Equation (
367
) was first conjectured in
JafLew15
, with additional support provided in
FauLew17
based on arguments different from those presented here.

Figure 15
:
Consider an infinitesimal time band in
\hat{A}
of width
\epsilon
around
A
, as shown by the shaded region, and denote the algebra generated by single-trace operators in this band as
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}^{\epsilon}
. The semi-classical state under consideration is cyclic and separating for both
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}^{\epsilon}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
. Lemma
IV.1
of Sec.
IV.1
then implies that
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
can be generated by acting with modular flows associated with
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}^{\epsilon}
, which gives equation (
366
), where
{{\mathcal{O}}}({\vec{x}})
should be understood as being smeared within the time band (local operators must be smeared to be well-defined).

Denote as
{{\Delta}}_{A,\Psi}
(with
N
-dependence suppressed) the modular operator for
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
in state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
.
The modular flow generated by
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}
can be understood as the large
N
limit of
the flow generated by
{{\Delta}}_{A,\Psi}
, i.e.,

\pi_{\Psi}\left(\lim_{N\to\infty,\lvert{\Psi}\rangle}{{\Delta}}_{A,\Psi}^{-is}{{\mathcal{O}}}(x){{\Delta}}_{A,\Psi}^{is}\right)={{\Delta}}^{-is}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}\pi_{\Psi}({{\mathcal{O}}}(x)){{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}^{is}\ .

(368)

Equation (
368
) was first conjectured in
LeuLiu22
, and more support will be given in upcoming work
BerLiu25
.

Equation (
368
) implies that operators in
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
that do not belong to
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
arise from the large
N
limit
of modular-flowed operators.
From equations (
366
) and (
368
), when the modular flows generated by
\Delta_{A,\Psi}
are geometric—i.e., they act as pointwise transformations—the set (
366
) should coincide with
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
, as such geometric flows simply fill the domain
\hat{A}
. In this case, we have

{\mathcal{X}}_{A}={\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}={{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}\ .

(369)

This situation arises when
A
is a half-space or a spherical region in the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, or when
A
is the full
R
boundary in the thermofield double state.
In more general situations, however, modular flows are not geometric, and the inclusion
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
is proper.

Now consider a general boundary subregion
A
in the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, for which
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
. The operators in
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
but not in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
—or equivalently, those supported in
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
but not in
{{\mathcal{C}}}_{A}
—can be generated by applying modular flow to single-trace operators in
A
. Since in the vacuum state,
{\mathcal{S}}={{\mathcal{A}}}_{\Omega}
, these operators must be expressible in terms of single-trace operators, and therefore must involve single-trace operators supported
outside

\hat{A}
. That is, the large-
N
limit of operators in
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
may include contributions from single-trace operators outside the domain of dependence
\hat{A}
. This does not contradict causality, as there are no equations of motion that relate single-trace operators at different times.

The equivalences (
359
) and (
364
) imply that both
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
should be type III
1
. We stress that these type III
1
structures have nothing to do with the type III
1
of
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
at finite
N
. Consider putting the boundary theory on a lattice, in which case
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
becomes type I, but
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
are still type III
1
. That is, these type III
1
structures are emergent in the large
N
limit.

In the continuum limit, the type III
1
nature of
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
at finite
N
arises from an infinite amount of short-range entanglement near
\partial A
, stemming from the infinite number of short-distance degrees of freedom present there in the CFT. In the bulk, this is reflected in the infinite proper size of AdS near the boundary. By contrast, the type III
1
nature of
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
originates from an infinite amount of long-range entanglement between
A
and its complement, which emerges in the
N\to\infty
limit (as illustrated in Fig.
1
). In the bulk, this infinite entanglement is mirrored by an infinite amount of short-range entanglement localized near
{{\gamma}}_{A}
or the edge of
{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}
(denoted
\chi_{\hat{A}}
), as illustrated in Fig.
16
.

Figure 16
:
Shown in the plot is a constant-time bulk Cauchy slice
\Sigma
, with
\chi_{\hat{A}}=\partial{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\hat{A}}\cap\Sigma
.
The infinite long-range entanglement between
A
and its complement in the large-
N
limit—responsible for the type III
1
nature of
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
—manifests in the bulk as infinite short-range entanglement localized near
\gamma_{A}
and
\chi_{\hat{A}}
, respectively, as depicted in the plot by short dotted lines.

The algebraic reformulation (
359
) of the entanglement wedge reconstruction also offers an alternative way to
characterize the RT-surface
{{\gamma}}_{A}
for a boundary region
A
.
From the discussion of Sec.
IV.4.1
, near the RT surface
{{\gamma}}_{A}
, bulk modular flows associated with
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
should act geometrically as local boosts, and
{{\gamma}}_{A}
is the invariant submanifold
of the local boosts.
Given the identification
{{\Delta}}_{{\mathcal{X}}_{A}}=\tilde{{\Delta}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}
(see footnote
45
),
{{\gamma}}_{A}
can then be understood as an
asymptotic invariant submanifold
of the boundary modular flow,
46
46

46

The word “asymptotic” is important: since
{{\gamma}}_{A}
lies on the boundary of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
, operators on
{{\gamma}}_{A}
do not belong to the local subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
.
{{\gamma}}_{A}
can only be obtained as a limit of points in
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
.
which can be used as an alternative definition of
{{\gamma}}_{A}
. In fact,
the derivation
LewMal13
of the RT-formula can be reinterpreted and adapted to show that the bulk asymptotic invariant submanifold of boundary modular flows is indeed the minimal surface anchored on the boundary at
A

BerLiu25
.

VII.2.3
Entanglement and causal wedges for extended gravitational systems

The algebraic formulation of entanglement and causal wedge algebras provides a natural generalization of these concepts that does not rely on geometric descriptions. For instance, instead of specifying a subsystem of the boundary CFT at finite
N
by a geometric subregion, we may specify it by a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
. The corresponding entanglement wedge algebra
{\mathcal{X}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}
can then be defined in direct analogy with (
361
). In general, such an
{\mathcal{X}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}
need not admit a bulk dual described by a geometric region. Indeed, even when
{{\mathcal{M}}}
corresponds to a geometric boundary region
A
, a bulk geometric description of
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
does not always exist, when the entanglement between
A
and its complement is of order
O(N^{0})
(see
Liu25
for an example). Nevertheless, the algebraic definition always applies.

We may also consider extended gravitational systems in which a gravitational sector with a boundary dual is entangled or coupled to a non-gravitational sector. Denote the gravitational sector (together with its boundary dual) by
B
, and the non-gravitational sector by
R
, so that the full system is
B\cup R
. When focusing only on
R
, we can assume its quantum information is fully accessible. In contrast, for questions involving
B
, two levels of description arise: a fully quantum one, which is not precisely understood, and a semi-classical one. When
B
is treated semi-classically, consistency requires that the
R
sector be handled at the same level of approximation. For convenience, we will refer to this regime as the
G_{N}\to 0
limit, even though
R
itself does not possess a gravitational coupling.

Now consider a subsystem
Q
of the full system
B\cup R
.
Q
may be a subregion of the boundary CFT, a subsystem of
R
, or
a union of both
. Denote the operator algebra of
Q
at the fully quantum level (i.e., finite
G_{N}
) by
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{Q}
, and define its
G_{N}\to 0
limit as

{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}=\lim_{G_{N}\to 0,\lvert{\Psi}\rangle}{{\mathcal{B}}}_{Q},

(370)

where
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is the state under consideration. Equation (
370
) should be understood in the same sense as (
361
). We interpret
{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
as the entanglement wedge algebra of
Q
, whether or not a geometric description exists. Whenever such a description is available, the algebraic and geometric definitions should be regarded as equivalent.

We may also define a causal wedge algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Q}
associated with
Q
, namely the algebra appearing in the low-energy effective description of
Q
. While somewhat abstract in general, this definition becomes concrete in specific settings. For example, consider an evaporating black hole, where the full system consists of the black hole plus its radiation. Taking
Q
to be the radiation subsystem,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Q}
corresponds to the algebra of Hawking quanta as described in the low-energy effective theory, while
{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
arises as the
G_{N}\to 0
limit of the exact radiation algebra.

VII.3
Quantum informational aspects of EWR from algebraic perspective

In this subsection, we discuss various quantum informational aspects of EWR from the perspective of its algebraic formulation.

VII.3.1
Entanglement wedge nesting and superadditivity of boundary algebras

An important geometric property of entanglement wedges, which follows from the extremality of the RT/HRT surface
\gamma_{A}
, is entanglement wedge nesting
Wal12
: for two boundary regions
A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}
, the corresponding entanglement wedges satisfy
\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{A_{1}}\subseteq\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{A_{2}}
. This property can be naturally understood from the boundary perspective via the identification (
359
): since
A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}
, it follows by definition that
{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{2}}
, which in turn implies
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{A_{1}}}\subseteq\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{A_{2}}}
.

Entanglement wedge nesting has an immediate corollary: the superadditivity of entanglement wedges. We now elaborate on this property and make its boundary origin more explicit. For simplicity of illustration, we will take the bulk spacetime to be static and the boundary regions to lie on constant-time slices.

Consider two boundary spatial regions
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
on a constant-time slice. Entanglement wedge nesting implies that

{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{1}}\cup{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}\subseteq{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}},\quad{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{1}\cap A_{2}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{1}}\cap{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{2}}\ .

(371)

Equations (
371
) imply that the entanglement wedge of the union
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is no smaller than the union of the entanglement wedges for
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
. In fact, the inclusions in (
371
) are generically proper, i.e., the entanglement wedges for boundary subregions are
superadditive
. See Fig.
17
for simple examples.

Figure 17
:
Examples of superadditivity of entanglement wedges (
371
) in the AdS
3
/CFT
2
duality, shown on a constant-time bulk Cauchy slice in the Poincare patch: (a) two intersecting intervals
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
; (b) two disjoint intervals
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
placed sufficiently close together. The RT surfaces
\gamma_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}
are shown as solid lines.

From the identification (
359
), equations (
371
) imply that the corresponding boundary subalgebras should be superadditive, i.e.,

{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{2}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}},\qquad{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}\cap A_{2}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}}\land{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{2}}\ .

(372)

However, recall from Sec.
IV.4.3
that for a general RQFT—that is, at finite

N
—the algebras associated with topologically trivial spatial regions are expected to be additive, namely,

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{1}}^{(N)}\lor{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{2}}^{(N)}={{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}^{(N)},\qquad{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{1}}^{(N)}\land{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{2}}^{(N)}={{\mathcal{B}}}_{A_{1}\cap A_{2}}^{(N)}\ .

(373)

Thus, for consistency with (
372
), the additivity property (
373
) must be violated in the large
N
limit. This violation can indeed be demonstrated explicitly on the boundary side for the example of Fig.
17
(a)
LeuLiu22
;
LeuLiu24
. See Fig.
18
for illustrations.
47
47

47

It is worth emphasizing that the field-theoretic discussion in Fig.
18
apply to any CFT
2
in the large central charge limit, independent of the coupling strength.
Thus, the superadditivity of entanglement wedges originates from, and can be viewed as, a bulk geometric manifestation of the superadditivity of boundary subalgebras in the large

N
limit.

Figure 18
:
Consider the example of Fig.
17
(a), where we take the two intervals
A_{1,2}
to each have half-width
a
, respectively centered at
x=\pm b
, with
b<a
.
For an interval
A
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}={\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
(recall (
369
)),
the algebra generated by single-trace operators in
\hat{A}
. Hence,
{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{\widehat{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}}
, i.e., the algebra generated by single-trace operators in the right plot. Determining
{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{2}}
is nontrivial. Fortunately, it was worked out in an old result of Araki
Ara63
, and is given by the algebra of single-trace operators in the region shown in the left plot, where the curves at the top and bottom are given by

t=\pm\sqrt{x^{2}+a^{2}-b^{2}}
for
x\in(-b,b)
.
We thus find
{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{2}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}
. It can be further checked that the causal wedge of the region in the left plot is precisely
\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{1}}\cup{\mathfrak{b}}_{A_{2}}}

LeuLiu22
.

Recall from the discussion of Sec.
IV.4
that the additivity property (
373
) can be understood from the fact that the algebras
{{\mathcal{B}}}^{(N)}_{A_{1}},{{\mathcal{B}}}^{(N)}_{A_{2}}
are locally generated. Equations (
372
) imply that in the large
N
limit, the algebras associated with local regions are no longer locally generated. This has important implications for quantum informational properties of the boundary system
LeuLiu22
;
LeuLiu24
. For example, it can be leveraged to provide a boundary explanation of, and generalize, the enhanced quantum tasks on the boundary enabled by bulk operations
May19
;
MayPen19
;
May21
;
MaySor22
.
Below we will discuss its connection to quantum error correction.

At finite
N
, the boundary subalgebra for a region
A
is also expected to satisfy the Haag duality (
144
), i.e.,

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{\bar{A}}^{(N)}=({{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)})^{\prime}\ .

(374)

With the assumption that the bulk algebras satisfy the Haag duality, we can deduce that

{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{\prime}={\mathcal{X}}_{\bar{A}},

(375)

i.e., the Haag duality survives the large
N
limit. More explicitly, from the bulk Haag duality (
358
),

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{\bar{A}}},

(376)

where in the second equality we have used that for the system in a pure state,
A
and
\bar{A}
share the same RT surface, and thus

\overline{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\bar{A}}\ .

(377)

Equation (
375
) then follows from (
376
) upon using the identification (
359
).

In contrast, the algebra of single-trace operators associated with a spacetime region is by definition additive, but does
not
satisfy the Haag duality.

VII.3.2
Explanation of quantum error correcting features

Entanglement wedge reconstruction has been interpreted in terms of quantum error correction
AlmDon14
.
Heuristically, the ability to recover all the quantum information in a bulk subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
with knowledge of only the subregion
A
on the boundary implies that the quantum information in
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
is robust even if we “make errors” in or even completely “erase” the complement,
\bar{A}
, of
A
. Furthermore, superadditivity of entanglement wedges (
371
) implies that the union of two subsystems
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
contains more quantum information than the “combination” of the respective quantum information for two subsystems, and quantum information in a bulk subregion is “stored” in the boundary system in a highly redundant way. See Fig.
19
for an illustration.

The quantum error correction perspective has provided
an important guiding principle to construct toy models of holographic duality using systems of finite dimensional Hilbert spaces, such as various types of tensor networks (see e.g.
PasYos15
;
HayNez16
), as well as an interpretation of the RT formula
Har16
.
48
48

48

See also
KanKol18
;
Fau20
;
KanKol21
;
GesKan21
;
FauLi22
for further development using von Neumann algebras.

Figure 19
:
Superadditivity of entanglement wedges (
371
) can be interpreted in terms of quantum error correcting properties.
Here the figure should be understood as representing a single time slice in AdS with the circle denoting the boundary while the interior of the circle is the bulk of AdS.
(a) That
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}\cup{\mathfrak{b}}_{\tilde{A}}\subsetneq{\mathfrak{b}}_{A\cup\tilde{A}}
implies that there exist operations, e.g. the operator
O(X)
, that cannot be reconstructed from those in either
A
or
\tilde{A}
, but can be reconstructed from those
A\cup\tilde{A}
.
(b) Quantum operations in a local subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}
are encoded in the boundary system in a highly redundant way as
they can be contained in the entanglement wedges of many possible boundary subregions, such as
A
and
\tilde{A}
,
and in principle infinitely many others. Here it is important
{\mathfrak{b}}
lies in the intersection of entanglement wedges of
A
and
\tilde{A}
, but not in the entanglement wedge of
A\cap\tilde{A}
.

The identification of subsystems (
359
) in terms of subalgebras, together with the superadditivity of subalgebras (
372
) in the large
N
limit, provides a boundary explanation for the origin of quantum error correcting properties. The quantum error correcting features illustrated in Fig.
19
can be attributed to the fact that boundary subalgebras associated with boundary subregions are non-locally generated in the large
N
limit.

Building on these considerations, let us now examine a bulk operator
\Phi(X)
with
X
lying in region
b
of Fig.
19
(b).
It can be reconstructed from both regions
A
and
\tilde{A}
, but lies outside the entanglement wedge of
A\cap\tilde{A}
. For definiteness, suppose we are in the vacuum sector, and that for
A
and
\tilde{A}
the entanglement and causal wedges coincide.
49
49

49

The discussion below can be readily extended to more general situations using (
367
)–(
368
).
From (
363
), we then have

\displaystyle\Phi(X)=\pi_{\Omega}({{\mathcal{O}}}_{A}(X))=\pi_{\Omega}({{\mathcal{O}}}_{\tilde{A}}(X)),

(378)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{O}}}_{A}(X)\equiv\int_{\hat{A}}d^{d}x\,K_{A}^{(C)}\left(X;x\right){{\mathcal{O}}}(x),

(379)

with
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{\tilde{A}}(X)
defined by replacing
A
with
\tilde{A}
in (
379
).

{{\mathcal{O}}}_{A}(X)
and
{{\mathcal{O}}}_{\tilde{A}}(X)
are completely different boundary operators, involving distinct boundary degrees of freedom
50
50

50

We stress that
X
does not lie in the entanglement wedge of
A\cap\tilde{A}
.
, even though their representations on the GNS Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\mathrm{(GNS)}}_{\Omega}
coincide. By considering other regions whose entanglement wedge encloses
X
, there is an infinite number of ways to describe
\Phi(X)
, all of which coincide in their representations on
{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\mathrm{(GNS)}}_{\Omega}
.

This implies that we cannot associate
\Phi(X)
—that is, any bulk degrees of freedom in region
b
—to
A
, to
\tilde{A}
, or to any boundary region whose entanglement wedge encloses
b
. This further elucidates the emergent nature of bulk degrees of freedom: they are collectively encoded in the boundary system and can, in principle, be reconstructed in infinitely many ways, each involving distinct sets of boundary degrees of freedom.

The algebraic formulation of entanglement wedge reconstruction also clarifies the nature of holographic quantum error correction codes discussed in
AlmDon14
;
PasYos15
;
HayNez16
. In that setup, the Hilbert space describing bulk physics in the semi-classical regime,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}
, is embedded into the full CFT Hilbert space as a “code” subspace, i.e.,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}\simeq{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm code}\subset{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT}
. Since the full boundary CFT Hilbert space is involved, this formulation is only meaningful at finite
N
. However, the bulk Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}
in the semi-classical regime can be precisely defined only in the
N\to\infty
limit.
51
51

51

As discussed in Sec.
VI.1
,
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}
should be identified with the GNS Hilbert space on the boundary.

Thus, holographic quantum error correction codes should be understood not as a literal implementation of the holographic dictionary, but rather as a framework for understanding how semi-classical bulk physics connects to the finite-
N
quantum regime. While in the original toy models
AlmDon14
;
PasYos15
;
HayNez16
, the codes were isometric and exact, various arguments suggest
Kel16
;
Fau20
that realistic codes must be approximate.
52
52

52

For example, there are tensions between exact quantum error correction and the Reeh-Schlieder theorem. We note that
HayPen18
also argues for the necessity of approximate codes, though for different reasons: their argument is based on considering “large” code spaces that include black hole states with dimension
O(N^{2})
—a situation we do not consider here.
A formulation of holographic quantum error correction that can apply to realistic holographic systems (beyond toy models) was proposed in
FauLi22
. There, the authors introduce asymptotically isometric codes—codes that become isometric only in the
N\to\infty
limit—within a von Neumann algebraic framework. This asymptotic setup provides a powerful way to relate semi-classical bulk physics to finite-
N
boundary descriptions for realistic holographic systems.

VII.4
An algebraic formulation of entanglement islands

Recent significant progress has been made in understanding the emergence of the Page curve for an evaporating black hole within the AdS/CFT duality
Pen19
;
AlmEng19
. A striking implication of this discussion is the “entanglement island” phenomenon
Pen19
;
AlmEng19
;
AlmMah19a
: specifically, after the Page time
t_{P}
, the black hole interior becomes part of the radiation system and is referred to as an entanglement island of the radiation. Despite its unintuitive and still mysterious nature, the entanglement island phenomenon appears to be an inevitable consequence of the self-consistency of entanglement wedge reconstruction. In this subsection, we provide an algebraic formulation of entanglement islands through the lens of subregion-subalgebra duality. We present a general definition of an island associated with a subsystem, formulated in terms of operator algebras, which reveals that it is a common feature of holographic systems. Moreover, this algebraic definition provides an intrinsic boundary diagnostic for identifying its presence.

Now consider the example of an evaporating black hole, as illustrated in Fig.
20
.
The full system separates into the black hole system (
B
) which is described by a boundary CFT and the emitted Hawking radiation (
R
). Before the Page time
t_{P}
, the minimal quantum extremal surface (QES) associated with the boundary subsystem
B
is the empty set, with the entanglement wedge
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{B}
of the boundary including a full bulk Cauchy slice, i.e.,
I\cup O
in Fig.
20
. For
t>t_{P}
, the minimal QES is given by
{\alpha}
in Fig.
20
, and
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{B}
includes only the part of a Cauchy slice exterior to
{\alpha}
, i.e., region
O
in Fig.
20
.
From entanglement wedge reconstruction, the interior of the black hole is reconstructible from the boundary CFT for
t<t_{P}
, but not for
t>t_{P}
. From (
359
), we have

{\mathcal{X}}_{B}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}\lor\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}&t<t_{P}\cr\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}&t>t_{P}\end{cases},

(380)

where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{O}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}
denote the operator algebras of bulk fields on a Cauchy slice exterior and interior to the horizon, respectively. After the Page time (
t>t_{P}
), the black hole interior
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}
is no longer part of
B
. Since
B\cup R
is the full system, it must then belong to
R
.
53
53

53

As emphasized below (
378
), the bulk degrees of freedom are collectively encoded in the boundary. The statement that
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{I}
belongs to
R
should not be understood to mean that the degrees of freedom constituting
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{I}
form a subset of those of
R
.

Figure 20
:
An evaporating black hole in AdS. (a) The Penrose diagram of an evaporating black hole in AdS, with dashed vertical line denoting transparent boundary where the Hawking radiation can pass through. A Cauchy slice at time
t
is shown as a horizontal line, with two competing QES: one being the empty set (i.e., at
\rho=0
), and the other, denoted by
{\alpha}
, lying slightly inside the horizon. (b) Cartoon of the Cauchy slice shown in (a), with
B
denoting the boundary.
O
is the region between
{\alpha}
and the boundary, and
I
is the region interior to
{\alpha}
.

R
denotes the radiation system and dashed lines represent entanglement between the black hole interior and the radiation.

By using the entanglement wedge algebra
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}
and the causal wedge algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
defined for non-gravitational systems such as
R
in Sec.
VII.2.3
, the inclusion of the black hole interior within
R
can be expressed in a more precise mathematical language as

{\mathcal{X}}_{R}=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}&t<t_{P}\cr{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\lor\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}&t>t_{P}\end{cases},

(381)

where the causal wedge algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
denotes the algebra associated with the Hawking quanta in the low-energy effective field theory of the joint gravity-radiation system. Equation (
381
)—compared with (
380
)—provides a precise way to describe
the “transfer” of the black hole interior from
B
to
R
at
t_{P}

EngLiu23
.

From
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
, we can determine existence of an island from the radiation system alone, by considering

{{\mathcal{I}}}_{R}\equiv{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{X}}_{R}\ .

(382)

From (
381
),
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{R}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}
for
t>t_{P}
. If
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{R}
is non-empty, there is an island—that is, there exist operators independent of those in the low-energy effective description of the radiation. As in (
366
), the island algebra
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{R}
can be obtained from
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
using modular flows associated with
{\mathcal{X}}_{R}
.

We can apply the definition of an island (
382
) to any subsystem
Q
of an extended holographic system discussed around (
370
) as

{{\mathcal{I}}}_{Q}={\mathcal{Y}}_{Q}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{X}}_{Q},

(383)

where
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Q}
is the “causal wedge” algebra of
Q
, i.e., the algebra associated with the low-energy effective description of
Q
.
An island for a subsystem
Q
thus corresponds to the part which survives the semi-classical limit but does not belong to the low-energy description of
Q
itself.

Now consider the case where
Q
is taken to be a boundary subregion
A
. We can identify
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Q}
with
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
, the algebra generated by single-trace operators in
\hat{A}
. In this case, the island algebra
{{\mathcal{I}}}_{A}
consists of operators generated by modular flows from
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
that do not belong to
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
. In the bulk, these are operators that lie outside the causal wedge of
A
but within its entanglement wedge.
Geometrically,
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}={\mathfrak{c}}_{\hat{A}}\cup{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
, where
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\hat{A}}
and
{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
are illustrated in Fig.
21
, and thus

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\hat{A}}}\lor\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}}\quad{\Rightarrow}\quad{\mathcal{X}}_{A}={\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}\lor\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}},\quad{{\mathcal{I}}}_{A}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}},

(384)

which identified the region
{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
(or equivalently its algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}}
) as the island for
A
.

Figure 21
:
A constant-time bulk Cauchy slice with
\chi_{\hat{A}}
being the edge of the causal wedge, and the RT surface
\chi_{A}
being edge of the entanglement wedge.
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\hat{A}}
is the part between
\chi_{\hat{A}}
and
A
, and is a Cauchy slice for the causal wedge.

{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
is region between
\chi_{\hat{A}}
and
{{\gamma}}_{A}
. The regions
{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
and
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\hat{A}}
are highly entangled through short-distance entanglement near
\chi_{\hat{A}}

While identifying region
{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}
as the island for
A
may appear surprising at first sight, this situation is not fundamentally different from that of the radiation. In both cases, the island part is geometrically separated from the subsystem and is highly entangled with the low-energy part, except that for radiation, the entanglement between
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}
(as illustrated in Fig.
20
(b)) is non-local, while for a boundary region
A
, the entanglement between
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\hat{A}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}_{A}}
occurs through bulk short-distance entanglement near
\chi_{\hat{A}}
.

The transfer of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{I}
at
t_{P}
from (
380
) to (
381
) can now be interpreted as a transfer of an island between different subsystems. This is, in fact, a common phenomenon. An example is presented in Fig.
22
,
where the subalgebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{o}}
of the middle region
{\mathfrak{o}}
—now interpreted as an island—is transferred from
A
to
{\overline{A}}
when
A
becomes smaller than
{\overline{A}}
. In both the black hole evaporation example and that of Fig.
22
, the transfer occurs when the entropies of a system and its complement become equal, and there is an exchange of dominance of QES. Similar transfer of an island is expected whenever there is such an exchange of dominance
EngLiu23
.

Figure 22
:
Transfer of an island between
A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}
(consisting of two intervals) and
\bar{A}
in the vacuum state of a boundary CFT
2
, as the size of
A
is changed.
When
|A|=\pi+\epsilon
where
|A|
denotes the length of
A
, the RT surface is given by
{{\gamma}}_{1}
, giving rise to a connected entanglement wedge for
A
. In particular, the middle region labeled by
{\mathfrak{o}}
can be identified as an island of
A
from the discussion around (
384
).
For
|A|=\pi-\epsilon
, the RT surface is given by
{{\gamma}}_{2}
. Region
{\mathfrak{o}}
is now identified as an island of
\bar{A}
.
As we shrink the size of
A
from
\pi+\epsilon
to
\pi-\epsilon
, the island algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{o}}
is transferred from
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
to
{\mathcal{X}}_{\overline{A}}
.

Note that the semiclassical description of a (sub)system is expected to vary continuously under any continuous change of the system’s parameters; therefore, a sharp transition can only occur in the island part. Since the island part of an algebra
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}
can be generated from the causal wedge algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A}
by modular flows, the transfers of “islands” in an evaporating black hole at the Page time and in the example of Fig.
22
can be attributed to a transition in the properties of modular flows as the state of the system is varied with respect to some parameter.

VII.5
Boundary description of a bulk causal diamond and generalized causal wedge reconstruction

In this subsection we discuss the boundary subalgebras dual to bulk diamond-like regions which do not touch the boundary. We start with some simple examples and then give a general theorem which includes a large class of examples.

Consider the vacuum state
\lvert{\Omega}\rangle
, which is dual to empty global AdS as given in (
293
). Let
I_{w}
denote a time band on the boundary of width
w<\pi R
, i.e., the spacetime region with
t\in\left(-\frac{w}{2},\frac{w}{2}\right)
.
The “causal wedge” of this region
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}\equiv\tilde{J}^{+}(I_{w})\cap\tilde{J}^{-}(I_{w})

is a “spherical Rindler” region with “radius”
\rho_{w}
given by

\rho_{w}=R\tan\left({\pi\over 2}-{w\over 2R}\right),

(385)

as illustrated in Fig.
23
. Note that
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}={\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}^{\prime\prime}
, is a bulk domain of dependence.
Extending the causal wedge reconstruction to this case, we find the identification
BanBry16
;
LeuLiu22
:

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}\ .

(386)

That is, the bulk spherical Rindler region
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
is dual to a boundary time-band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
.

For
w\geq\pi R
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
covers an entire bulk Cauchy slice, and

{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}^{\prime\prime}
becomes the full global AdS spacetime. From (
304
),
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{B}
, the full boundary algebra. In this case, equation (
386
) is simply replaced by the last expression of (
298
).

Figure 23
:
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
and
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
are, respectively, the bulk duals of the time-band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
and its commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}
in the vacuum sector.

Taking commutant on both sides of (
386
) and using (
358
), we find

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}

(387)

where
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}={\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}^{\prime}
is the spherical diamond region in the center of global AdS, shown in Fig.
23
.
We then have the correspondence
LeuLiu22
:

{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}\quad{\leftrightarrow}\quad{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}\ .

(388)

We find that the boundary algebra describing the bulk diamond region
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
does not have a boundary geometric description: it is defined algebraically through the commutant structure of the boundary system. By considering increasing values of
w<\pi R
, we can use
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}
to describe progressively smaller diamonds in the bulk. As
w
approaches
\pi R
, the diamond can become arbitrarily small, and its local physics should approach that of flat spacetime.

This is rather intuitive: for a larger time band, there are more operators in the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
, and the commutant should become smaller. Physically, as we increase the size
w
of the time band, physical operations described by operators in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
can probe longer boundary time scales. The commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}
consists of physical operations that are “independent” of those in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
, and may heuristically be interpreted as operators probing even longer time scales than those in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
. It is such operations that correspond to those in the bulk diamond region
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
.
Such a relation is consistent with, and may be viewed as a precise operator-algebraic realization of, the usual IR/UV connection
SusWit98
;
PeePol98
.

Now consider
Y=I_{w}^{(R)}
, a time band of width
w
on the right boundary of the thermofield double state
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
at
T>T_{\rm HP}
, with the bulk metric given by (
309
).
We then have

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}}={\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}},\quad w=2\int_{\rho_{w}}^{\infty}{d\rho\over f(\rho)},\quad\forall w>0,

(389)

where
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
is the spherical wedge region in the
R
-region of the black hole, shown in Fig.
24
(a).

The existence of a horizon implies that for any choice of
w
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
covers only a proper subset of the
t=0
Cauchy slice of the
R
-region of the black hole, i.e., the
R
-region can never be “filled up” by spherical wedge regions, no matter how large
w
is.

Taking the commutant on both sides of (
389
), we find

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}}=({\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}})^{\prime},\qquad\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}}=({\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}})^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\ .

(390)

See Fig.
24
(a).
The geometric statement that
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
is a proper subset of the
R
-region for any
w
—equivalently, the existence of the horizon—translates to the algebraic condition

({\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}})^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}\neq\emptyset,\quad\forall w\ .

(391)

Thus, the existence of a horizon in the bulk can be diagnosed on the boundary through commutants of time band algebras.
See Fig.
24
(b) for the bulk region dual to the commutant of the algebra associated with the union of time-bands on
L
and
R
.

Figure 24
:
(a) In the plot,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
and
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
are, respectively, the bulk duals of the time-band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}}
in the thermofield double state and its relative commutant
({\mathcal{Y}}^{(R)}_{I_{w}})^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
.
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
is the bulk region to the left of the two thick blue solid lines, including the
L
region as well as parts of the
F
and
P
regions.
(b) The bulk region
{{\mathcal{D}}}
(enclosed by dashed blue lines) is dual to the commutant
({\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w_{1}}}^{(R)}\cup{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w_{2}}}^{(L)})^{\prime}
.

Now consider a semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
corresponding to a single-sided black hole geometry formed via a homogenous gravitational collapse (recall Fig.
11
(b)). On the boundary, the collapse process can be interpreted as the thermalization of
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, with the
black hole describing the macroscopic equilibrium state at late times.
54
54

54

In the Heisenberg picture, the state does not evolve, but correlation functions of single-trace operators approach those of a thermal state.

Consider the time band
I_{0}
on the boundary of Fig.
25
. Since the causal wedge of
I_{0}
covers an entire Cauchy slice in the bulk, the causal wedge reconstruction gives

{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{0}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk})

(392)

where
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm bulk}
denotes the bulk Hilbert space.
The bulk algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
for the region
R
in Fig.
25
long after the black hole has formed can be identified with the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}}
generated by single-trace operators localized in the semi-infinite time band
I_{1}
on the boundary. The bulk algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
in the region labeled by
L
can be identified with the commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}}^{\prime}
.
This gives a thermofield double structure for a single-sided black hole at times long after the black hole has formed.
55
55

55

{\mathcal{Y}}_{I}^{\prime}
provides a precise definition for the “mirror operators” envisioned in
PapRaj12
;
PapRaj13b
.

From (
392
),
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{0}}
is type I, while
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}}
is a proper subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{0}}
and is type III
1
. In fact we have

{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{0}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}\lor\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}\ .

(393)

At early times, the algebra of a sufficiently large time band is of type
I
. However, as the system approaches thermal equilibrium, the algebra associated with a time band becomes type III
1
, regardless of how large the band is (as long as its lower end is fixed). This change in the type of the algebra can be interpreted as a signature of horizon formation in the bulk and thermalization on the boundary (see Sec.
VIII.1
for further discussion).

Figure 25
:
Bulk duals of boundary time bands and their commutants in a single-sided collapsing black hole. For clarity, the collapsing matter is not shown.

We find that the commutants of time-band algebras provide a powerful framework for describing bulk causally complete regions, including those that do not intersect the boundary. Thus far, our discussion has been limited to uniform time bands—homogeneous in the boundary spatial directions—and to spherically symmetric bulk geometries.

Extending causal wedge reconstruction to a more general boundary region
Y
is, however, more intricate. Domains of dependence discussed in Sec.
VII.2
and uniform time bands in spherically symmetric spacetimes are special cases in which the causal wedge intersects the boundary exactly at the corresponding boundary region. This property does not hold in general, owing to the well-known focusing of null geodesics: a null congruence fired from the boundary into the bulk will typically not retrace a congruence fired from the bulk to the boundary, as caustics develop along the null generators.

On the boundary side, this is reflected in the fact that the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
generated by single-trace operators restricted to a boundary region
Y
is, in general, not a von Neumann algebra: taking the double commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}
can involve single-trace operators supported on a larger region. For instance, as illustrated in Fig.
18
(a), the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{Y}}_{A_{2}}
extends beyond
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}
depends on the chosen GNS sector. For example, in the thermofield double state, only at infinite temperature, does one find
{\mathcal{Y}}_{A_{1}}\lor{\mathcal{Y}}_{A_{2}}={\mathcal{Y}}_{A_{1}\cup A_{2}}

LeuLiu24
.

In
EngLiu25
, a general theorem is established: The algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
of single-trace operators associated with a boundary spacetime region
Y
admits a standard reconstruction of the causal wedge and is a von Neumann algebra if and only if the following condition is true: (1) it is causally convex; (2) denoting its generalized causal wedge as

{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}=\left(\tilde{J}^{+}[Y]\cap\tilde{J}^{-}[Y]\right)^{\prime\prime},

(394)

and the boundary manifold as
B
, we have

{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}\cap B=Y\ .

(395)

For such a
Y
, it can be shown that the causal wedge reconstruction holds

{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}}\ .

(396)

Taking commutant on both sides of (
396
) gives

{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}^{\prime}},

(397)

where in the second equality we have used (
358
). Equation (
397
) identifies the bulk causal complement of
{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}
with the commutant of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
.

For a boundary region
Y
not satisfying the above conditions, we have

Y\subset Y_{\rm max}\equiv{{\mathcal{C}}}_{Y}\cap B

(398)

and we expect
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{Y}}_{Y_{\rm max}}
.

The algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
in a generalized causal wedge reconstruction (
396
) and its commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime}
are emergent structures that arise in the large-
N
limit. At finite
N
, arbitrary products of single-trace operators can, in principle, generate the entire operator algebra. In particular, when
Y
covers a full boundary Cauchy slice, by the Heisenberg equations of motion, the resulting algebra on any Cauchy slice becomes equivalent to the full algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT})
. As a result, for any time band, the operator algebra becomes indistinguishable from
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT})
, whose commutant is trivial.

This emergent nature of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime}
for a time-band region
Y
is consistent with the expectation that sharply defined bulk subregions only exist in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit.

In the next subsection, we will argue that
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
may nevertheless admit a finite-
N
extension that remains strictly smaller than
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT})
and possesses a nontrivial commutant. Such finite-
N
extensions of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{Y}^{\prime}
could provide an algebraic framework for defining bulk subsystems in the quantum gravitational regime.

VII.6
Generalized entropy and subregion-subalgebra duality at finite
N

We discussed earlier in Sec.
VI.6
that, at any finite order in the
G_{N}
perturbation theory, the leading order type III
1
characterization of the algebras suffices.
The story should change significantly when
G_{N}
is treated non-perturbatively. For example, the operator algebra of CFT
R
in the thermofield double state should become type I and be state-independent.
Another example is the boundary algebra describing the bulk diamond region
{\mathfrak{b}}
in Fig.
23
; the argument for its existence breaks down at finite
N
and it is not clear whether an algebra can still be defined.

Treating
N
non-perturbatively in a precise manner is currently out of reach. However, the expected behavior of generalized gravitational entropy gives an indication that the
subregion-subalgebra duality may still be defined at finite
N

LeuLiu25
;
EngLiu25
, despite that geometrically a bulk subregion can no longer be sharply defined due to spacetime fluctuations. See also
BahBel22
;
JenRaj24
for other discussions of time band algebras at finite
N
.

Consider two copies of the boundary CFT in the TFD state, at a temperature above the Hawking-Page transition.
As discussed in Sec.
VI.3
, the algebra of observables for CFT
R
is given by
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
in the large
N
limit, and by
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
at finite
N
.
At finite
N
, the algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})

has a well-defined entropy
S_{R}
—the entanglement entropy between CFT
R
and CFT
L
.
In the large
N
limit,
S_{R}
can be identified with the generalized entropy of the dual black hole, defined as

S_{\rm gen}\equiv{A_{\rm hor}\over 4G_{N}(\epsilon)}+S_{\rm bulk}(\epsilon)\ .

(399)

Here,
A_{\rm hor}
denotes the area of the horizon,
S_{\rm bulk}(\epsilon)
is the bulk entanglement entropy of the
R
region, and
G_{N}(\epsilon)
is the bare Newton’s constant, with
\epsilon
representing a short-distance cutoff.

In the
G_{N}\to 0
limit,
56
56

56

Throughout the discussion below,
G_{N}
should be understood as the renormalized coupling, while the bare coupling is denoted by
G_{N}(\epsilon)
, with explicit dependence on the cutoff parameter
\epsilon
.
the bulk algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
for the
R
-region is type III
1
, for which
S_{\rm bulk}
cannot be defined. In practice, one introduces a bulk short-distance cutoff
\epsilon
to regularize the algebra, turning it into a type I algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\epsilon}
, whose entropy has leading divergence of the form

S_{\rm bulk}(\epsilon)=a{A_{\rm hor}\over\epsilon^{d-1}}+\cdots,\quad\epsilon\to 0,

(400)

where
a
is some constant and
\cdots
denotes less divergent and finite terms. The first term in (
399
) also exhibits bulk UV divergences through the bare coupling
G_{N}(\epsilon)
. Therefore, in the
G_{N}\to 0
limit, where the bulk is described by an effective field theory, neither of the terms on the right-hand side of (
399
) is individually well-defined.

However, there are indications that the UV divergences of the two terms in (
399
) cancel each other, and the sum is in fact UV finite
SusUgl94
, i.e,

S_{\rm gen}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left({A_{\rm hor}\over 4G_{N}(\epsilon)}+S_{\rm bulk}(\epsilon)\right)

(401)

has a well-defined limit.
The cancellation is an indication that the generalized entropy (
399
) can be extended to finite
G_{N}
, which is of course required if it is to be identified with the boundary entanglement entropy
S_{R}
.

Equations (
399
)–(
401
) also give a hint on how type III
1
algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
of the large
N
limit becomes a type I algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
at finite
N
, as follows. We first treat
G_{N}
perturbatively, with the short-distance cutoff
\epsilon
independent of the renormalized coupling
G_{N}
, which is implicit in the limit (
401
).
From the subregion-subalgebra duality, there should exist a type I boundary algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\epsilon}_{R}
,

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\epsilon}\ .

(402)

That is, there exists a regularization on the boundary in the
N\to\infty
limit that turns
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
into a type I algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\epsilon}
. While we do not currently know how to describe the regularization explicitly for a general boundary theory, the duality implies that it should exist.

Now consider a small but nonzero
G_{N}
(i.e., with
G_{N}
treated non-perturbatively). We can no longer take
\epsilon
all the way to
0
. For
\epsilon\sim\ell_{p}
, where
\ell_{p}
is the Planck length, quantum gravitational effects should become important and we should interpret (
399
) in the full quantum gravitational theory.
In this regime, it is no longer possible to have a clean separation between the
1/G_{N}
term in (
399
) and the term (
400
) in
S_{\rm bulk}(\epsilon)
as
{1\over G_{N}}\sim{1\over\ell_{p}^{d-1}}\sim{1\over\epsilon^{d-1}}
.
Therefore, now it is more sensible to interpret the total contribution
S_{\rm gen}
as the entropy for
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\epsilon}
. Now recall that at finite
N
,
S_{\rm gen}
is the entropy of
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
, which leads us to identify

\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\epsilon}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R}),\quad\epsilon\sim\ell_{p}\ .

(403)

In other words, as
\epsilon
is decreased all the way to the Planck length
\ell_{p}
, we expect that throughout the process, there exists a type I algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}^{\epsilon}
, which eventually becomes
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{R})
.

A similar discussion applies to the entanglement wedge
\hat{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
of a boundary region
A
. In this case, according to the RT formula
RyuTak06
;
HubRan07
;
FauLew13
, the generalized entropy of the bulk subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}_{A}
can be identified with the boundary entanglement entropy
S_{A}
57
57

57

A boundary short-distance cutoff is always assumed.
of
A
,

S_{\rm gen}({\mathfrak{b}}_{A})=S_{A},

(404)

and therefore should admit a finite
G_{N}
extension. The same argument as above suggests that a regularized
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}^{\epsilon}
should become

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{A}^{(N)}
when
\epsilon\sim\ell_{p}
.

The generalized entropy (
399
) can be extended to a general spacetime region. For example, consider
the spherical Rindler region
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
in Fig.
23
, which is dual to the time band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
on the boundary.
The generalized entropy for
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
is defined as

S_{\rm gen}[{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}]=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left({{\rm Area}({\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}})\over 4G_{N}(\epsilon)}+\widetilde{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}}(\epsilon)\right)\ .

(405)

Here
\widetilde{S}_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}}(\epsilon)
is the bulk entanglement entropy between
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
and its complement
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
. As in (
399
), while each individual term on the right hand side of (
405
) suffers from UV divergences, the sum is believed to be finite (see e.g., a review in
BouFis15a
).
By using the same argument as above, this can be used as an indication of:

(a)

S_{\rm gen}[{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}]
can be extended to finite
G_{N}
.

(b)

The algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
admits a finite-
N
extension to a type I subalgebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{I_{w}}
, whose entropy is given by the generalized entropy
S_{\rm gen}[{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}]
. Importantly,
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{I_{w}}
cannot coincide with the full boundary algebra
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\rm CFT})
, since the latter has zero entropy in the vacuum state.

Similarly, the commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}
, which is dual to the diamond region, should also possess a finite-
N
extension, denoted
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}}
.

Note that at finite
N
, the bulk regions
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho_{w}}
and
{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho_{w}}
can no longer be sharply defined due to spacetime fluctuations. Nevertheless, the above discussion suggests that there exist well-defined operator algebras that can be taken as the natural definitions of the corresponding subsystems in the quantum gravitational regime.
58
58

58

See also discussions in
ColDon23
from path integral perspectives.

VIII
Emergence of bulk geometric concepts

The subregion-subalgebra duality identifies an arbitrary bulk subregion
{\mathfrak{b}}
with a type III
1
boundary subalgebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
.
The subalgebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{b}}
in general does not have a geometric origin itself, and can be precisely defined only in the large
N
limit, which reflects that the bulk subregion can only be sharply defined in the perturbative
G_{N}
expansion.
An example is the commutant of the time band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I}
discussed in Sec.
VII.5
, describing a bulk diamond region that does not touch the boundary.

As discussed in Sec.
IV.4
, to describe a QFT in a curved spacetime, instead of using the action of the QFT with a background metric, we can use the collection of local subalgebras associated with open subregions.
In contrast to the conventional action-based formulation, the local algebraic description makes manifest local physics, the causal structure, and the entanglement properties of the system—all of which are hidden in the action approach.
In this section, we illustrate how the subregion-subalgebra duality can be used to understand the emergence of these aspects from properties of boundary algebras.

VIII.1
Algebraic characterization of bulk causal structure

In this subsection we discuss how the subregion-subalgebra duality can be used to characterize the bulk causal structure using boundary algebras, including an algebraic characterization of bulk horizons.

Boundary operators automatically commute when they are spacelike separated on the boundary.
However, time-like separated operators may also commute. Their commutation relations depend on the representations of boundary operators on
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Psi}^{(\rm GNS)}
, which are in turn specified by two-point functions of
boundary operators in
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
. The commutant structure of boundary subalgebras can be much richer than what is required of the boundary causal structure. In fact, from the subregion-subalgebra duality, it captures the
bulk causal structure
.

We first describe how the commutant structure of boundary algebras can be used to define a causal depth parameter, which quantifies the “depth” of the bulk interior
GesLiu24
.

Recall from the discussion around (
304
), in empty AdS, the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
associated with a time band
I_{w}
of width
w
becomes the full algebra when
w\geq\pi R
. Geometrically,
w=\pi R
is the minimal width of the band such that light rays from the band can cover a full bulk Cauchy slice. It can thus be used to characterize the “depth” of the radial direction probed in terms of the causal structure, see Fig.
26
(a). Motivated by this, a causal depth parameter can be defined in the boundary system as follows.

Definition VIII.1
.

The causal depth parameter
\mathcal{T}(t)
of a semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
at time
t
is the largest value of
w
for which the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}(t)}
for the time band
I_{w}(t)=(t-w/2,t+w/2)
has a nontrivial commutant.

For empty AdS, we have
{\mathcal{T}}(t)=\pi R
, being time-independent. For a general asymptotic AdS geometry,
{\mathcal{T}}(t)
is in general time-dependent, but if the bulk geometry does not contain a black hole, we expect
{\mathcal{T}}(t)
to be finite for all time.

For a single-sided black hole, from Fig.
26
(b),
{\mathcal{T}}(t)=\infty
for all time, which provides an intrinsic boundary characterization of the horizon. By considering time bands
I_{w}
with increasingly larger width
w
on the boundary, we can probe
progressively larger subregions of the exterior of the black hole using
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
; the full exterior region is accessible only in the limit
w\to\infty
.
59
59

59

In
NebQi23
, a quantum circuit model following the same line of thought to probe the bulk horizon was constructed.

For a single-sided black hole formed from collapse, from Fig.
25
and the discussion above (
393
),
{\mathcal{T}}(t)
is finite for any
t
, but increases monotonically to
\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
This divergence of
{\mathcal{T}}
as
t\to\infty
can be considered as a signature of the horizon formation.

Figure 26
:
(a) The depth parameter
\mathcal{T}(t)
can be seen as
the minimal width of a time band centered around time
t
such that the “causal wedge” of the time band can cover a full Cauchy slice in the bulk. For an asymptotic AdS geometry without a horizon, the
{\mathcal{T}}
is expected to be finite. See
Eng16
for development of this line of reasoning. (b) For a single-sided black hole,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
always has a nontrivial commutant for any finite value of
w
, and thus
{\mathcal{T}}(t)=\infty
for any
t
.

Definition
VIII.1
applies to a single-sided geometry. For a two-sided state, we can take the time band
I_{w}
to be on one of the boundaries, say the
R
-boundary. We can then define a depth parameter
{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(t)
for the causal wedge of the
R
-boundary by using the relative commutant
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
in the single-trace operator algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
for the
R
-boundary.

Definition VIII.2
.

The causal depth parameter
\mathcal{T}_{R}(t)
for the right causal wedge of a two-sided semi-classical state
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
at time
t
is defined as the largest value of
w
for which the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}(t)}
, associated with the time band
I_{w}(t)=(t-w/2,t+w/2)
on the right boundary, has a nontrivial relative commutant in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
.

Consider, for example, the thermofield double state. For
T<T_{\rm HP}
, with the bulk geometry given by two disconnected copies of AdS, with the causal wedge of the
R
-boundary given by the right AdS.

{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(t)
simply reduces to that of empty AdS, given by
\pi R
. For
T>T_{\rm HP}
, the bulk geometry is given by the eternal black hole. From Fig.
24
(a) and discussion around (
391
),
{\mathcal{T}}=\infty
for all time, which provides a boundary characterization of the bulk bifurcating horizon.

From the perspective of the boundary CFT, the depth parameter
{\mathcal{T}}
is the minimal width of a time interval
I_{w}
such that the knowledge of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
is enough to determine the full (single-trace) algebra of the system (or the
R
-system in the case of a two-sided state).
For example, in the case of empty AdS,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}={{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\Omega}^{(\rm GNS)})
for
w>\pi R
, i.e., we already have access to all the operations in the system given such an
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{w}}
. But in the case of the eternal black hole, it is not possible to have access to the full right algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
for any finite
w
.

For the theory at finite
N
, access to the operator algebra on a single Cauchy slice suffices
to determine the full algebra. In the large
N
limit, this property breaks down as there are no equations of motion.
Even though operators on different Cauchy slices cannot be expressed in terms of each other, there are still (state-dependent) relations among them (encoded in the structure of two-point functions that specifies the generalized free field theory). Such relations make it possible to recover the full algebra by having access to the operator algebra for a time band with a width
w>{\mathcal{T}}
.
For a black hole geometry,

{\mathcal{T}}=\infty
implies that the relations among operators at different Cauchy slices are so weak that the full algebra cannot be recovered by having access to operator algebras of arbitrary finite time bands.
60
60

60

In fact, it is not enough to have access to the algebra of a semi-infinite time band of the form
I=(t_{0},\infty)
.

The causal depth parameter
\mathcal{T}
can thus also be viewed as quantifying the level of determinism in the boundary theory. In a theory with equations of motion, there is full determinism and
{\mathcal{T}}=0
.
Whenever
{\mathcal{T}}
is nonzero, there is a loss of determinism, and when it is infinite, the determinism is lost completely.
Under this interpretation, the emergence of the radial direction is tied to loss of determinism on the boundary, and
a black hole represents a complete loss of determinism.
It should also be mentioned that
{\mathcal{T}}=0
by itself does not imply full determinism from a single time slice.

The causal depth parameter can be determined by the spectral function of the boundary theory, and it is equal to an invariant known in harmonic analysis as the
exponential type
. For simplicity, we will restrict to the time-translationally invariant case where
{\mathcal{T}}
is time-independent. Consider the commutator
61
61

61

In the discussion below, for notational simplicity we will suppress spatial dependence which does not play a significant role.

\rho(t-t^{\prime})\equiv{\left\langle{\Psi|[{{\mathcal{O}}}(t),{{\mathcal{O}}}(t^{\prime})]|\Psi}\right\rangle},

(406)

whose Fourier transform
\rho(\omega)
is called the spectral function.
Operators in a time-band algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I}
can be generated by

{{\mathcal{O}}}(f_{I})=\int dt\,{{\mathcal{O}}}(t)f_{I}(t),\quad{\rm supp}\,f_{I}\in I,

(407)

with
{\rm supp}\,f_{I}
denoting the support of function
f_{I}
.
The (relative) commutant of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{I}
can be shown to be generated by
{{\mathcal{O}}}(g)=\int dt\,{{\mathcal{O}}}(t)g(t)
, for
g
real-valued, satisfying

[{{\mathcal{O}}}(g),{{\mathcal{O}}}(f_{I})]=0\quad\to\quad(g,f_{I})\equiv\int dtdt^{\prime}\,g(t)\rho(t-t^{\prime})f_{I}(t^{\prime})=0\ .

(408)

Equation (
408
) can also be stated as that
g(t)
lies in the symplectic complement of the subspace spanned by
f_{I}
, since
(g,f)
, as defined by (
408
), is a symplectic product. Equation (
408
) can be written in frequency space as

\int{d\omega\over 2\pi}g^{*}(\omega)\rho(\omega)f_{I}(\omega)=0\ .

(409)

Finding
{\mathcal{T}}
, then becomes:

Exponential type problem:
Given a measure
\rho(\omega)
, what is the smallest value of
{\mathcal{T}}
for which the Fourier transforms of compactly supported distributions on
I=(-{\mathcal{T}}/2,{\mathcal{T}}/2)
become dense in
L^{2}(\rho)
?

The exponential type problem was first introduced in the work of Kolmogorov and others on chaos in classical dynamical systems (see for example
Kre45
), where they asked for how much time is necessary to observe a system in order to be able to predict its whole evolution. This is closely related to the loss of determinism in a large
N
system described above.

The value of the causal depth parameter
\mathcal{T}
can be obtained
from the largest value of
a
for which an
a
-uniform, or
a
-regular
, sequence can be embedded into the spectral function
\rho(\omega)

PolT13
. Roughly speaking, it is a sequence
\{\lambda_{n}\}

such that

\displaystyle\rho(\lambda_{n})\neq 0,\quad\lambda_{n}\approx\frac{n}{a}+c_{\pm},\quad n\to\pm\infty\ .

(410)

See Appendix C of
GesLiu24
for a precise description. We stress that only the behavior at large frequencies matters when deciding whether a sequence is
a
-uniform or not, and thus
{\mathcal{T}}
is solely encoded in the large-frequency behavior of the boundary spectral function. This may seem to be in tension with the IR/UV connection of the duality, as large-frequency behavior is typically associated with bulk physics near the boundary, whereas

{\mathcal{T}}
clearly serves as a parameter probing the interior (or the global structure) of the bulk spacetime.
We note that, it is not how
\rho(\omega)
grows or decays with a large
\omega
—usually characterized as the UV behavior—that is important here. Rather, it is the average spacing between the points in the support of
\rho
that is required to determine
\mathcal{T}
.

VIII.2
Emergence of Kruskal-like time and causal structure of an eternal black hole

In the last subsection, we introduced a causal depth parameter
{\mathcal{T}}
, which quantifies the depth of the bulk as probed by light rays. In particular,
{\mathcal{T}}=\infty
can be viewed as an indication of the presence of an event horizon. In this subsection, using the eternal black hole as an illustrative example, we show that a much finer understanding of bulk causal structure can be obtained. This includes the emergence of a Kruskal-like time, sharp boundary signatures of the horizon crossing, and probes of the black hole interior
LeuLiu21a
;
LeuLiu21b
.
In particular, we will provide a resolution of the meeting-behind-the-horizon puzzle mentioned in Sec.
VI.3
.

Consider the boundary system in the thermofield double state. In Sec.
VI.3
, we saw that in the large
N
limit, the boundary algebra for CFT
R
becomes type III
1
for
T>T_{\rm HP}
, while for
T<T_{\rm HP}
, the algebra is of type I.
These cases correspond, respectively, to the bulk eternal black hole and thermal AdS geometries. Recall Fig.
9
. From the bulk perspective, a key difference between the two is that the
L
and
R
boundaries are connected in the black hole geometry, but disconnected in thermal AdS.

This connection between the
L
and
R
regions in the black hole geometry has important implications for the causal structure: it should be possible for time evolution to take an operator from the
R
system across the horizon into the
F
region, where it becomes causally connected to operators in the
L
system and underlies the “meeting-behind-the-horizon puzzle” discussed in Sec.
VI.3
. In contrast, for two disconnected copies of AdS, such causal connection through time evolution is not possible.

Figure 27
:
Kruskal diagram for an eternal black hole, with the Kruskal time oriented upward and
U,V
labeling the Kruskal null coordinates. Transverse directions
x_{\perp}
are suppressed.
The dashed lines are event horizons (
U=0
or
V=0
), the solid red lines are the singularities (
UV=1
), and the solid black lines are the boundaries (
UV=-1
).
We also show the translation of a point near the past horizon (
V\ll 1
) or the future horizon (
|U|\ll 1
) along the
U
or
V
direction, respectively.

In the AdS Schwarzschild geometry, there are two distinct types of time, one is the Schwarzschild time, which maps the
R
(
L
) region to itself, and is an isometry. Another is the Kruskal time which can take a point in the
R
(or
L
) region to the
F
or
P
regions, see Fig.
27
. Approximating the near-horizon region by a locally flat Minkowski patch, the Kruskal time coincides with the Minkowski time, while the Schwarschild time coincides with the Rindler time. In particular, the Kruskal null coordinates
U,V
coincide with the light-cone coordinates
x^{-},x^{+}
of Fig.
6
.
The Kruskal time is not an isometry, and in the Hartle-Hawking state the black hole behaves as a time-dependent system in terms of its evolution. We will refer to a time evolution that takes the Cauchy slice at
t=0
to one across the horizon as a Kruskal-like evolution.

The Schwarzschild time reduces to the boundary time as the boundary is approached and thus can be naturally understood in terms of the boundary. In fact, they can be directly identified based on equivalence of bulk and boundary algebras.
The Schwarzschild time is the modular time of the bulk algebra
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
, while in the TFD state,
from (
307
), the boundary time coincides with the modular time for
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
.
62
62

62

Modular flows act in opposite directions on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}_{L}
and we take their boundary times to also go in opposite directions.
Given the identification of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
, the corresponding modular times are thus identified.

The boundary interpretation of a Kruskal-like time evolution is much more mysterious, as the boundary time approaches
\pm\infty
at the horizon and thus stops there. There appears to be no time evolution in the boundary that can be identified with it. We will now show that, in the large
N
limit, the emergent type III
1
structure of the boundary algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
gives rise to additional boundary times that can be identified in the bulk as Kruskal-like times.

More explicitly, in Sec.
IV.5
we discussed that given a von Neumann algebra
{{\mathcal{M}}}
with a cyclic and separating vector
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
, if there is a subalgebra
{{\mathcal{N}}}\subset{{\mathcal{M}}}
for which
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
is also cyclic, then there exists a positive operator,

G=K_{{\mathcal{M}}}-K_{{\mathcal{N}}}\geq 0,\quad G\lvert{\Psi}\rangle=0,

(411)

which can be used to generate new time flows that leave
\lvert{\Psi}\rangle
invariant.
Furthermore, if
{{\mathcal{N}}}
satisfies the half-sided modular inclusion condition (
167
) (or (
178
)), then
G,K_{{\mathcal{M}}},K_{{\mathcal{N}}}
form a closed algebra, and the flow generated by
G
has additional nice properties discussed in detail in Sec.
IV.5
.

Figure 28
:
The algebras
\mathcal{Y}_{+}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{-}
, which are supported on the semi-infinite time intervals
t>0
and
t<0
, are dual to the
regions
{\mathcal{W}}_{+}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{-}
in the bulk, respectively.

We can now construct Kruskal-like times from the boundary as follows. Take
{{\mathcal{M}}}
to be
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
with
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
being the cyclic and separating vector. We take
{{\mathcal{N}}}
to be the subalgebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{-}
associated with the semi-infinite time band
t<0
(i.e.,
generated by single-trace operators with
t<0
). From the discussion of Sec.
VII.5
,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{-}
is dual to the bulk subregion
{\mathcal{W}}_{-}
indicated in Fig.
28
. Thus it is also type III
1
, and
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is cyclic and separating with respect to it.

Since the modular flow of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
with respect to
\lvert{\Psi_{\beta}}\rangle
is simply time translation,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{-}
satisfies the half-sided modular inclusion condition (
167
). The corresponding operator defined by (
411
), which we denote as
G_{+}
, then generates a new time flow for
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
. Via the identification
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}={\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
, this also induces a new flow for bulk operators in the
R
-region.

Similarly, we can consider the subalgebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{+}
associated with the semi-infinite time band
t>0
(see Fig.
28
), with the corresponding operator defined by (
411
) denoted as
G_{-}
. The operator
G_{-}
generates an another time flow for
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{R}
.

How do these flows act on a bulk operator? We can in fact get a qualitative picture without doing any explicit calculation.
Since
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\pm}
are equivalent to the bulk algebras in the regions
{\mathcal{W}}_{\pm}
of Fig.
28
, we recover near the horizon—where the
R
-region of the black hole geometry reduces to Rindler spacetime—the situation discussed in Fig.
6
of Sec.
IV.5
. Therefore, near the horizon, the flows generated by
G_{\pm}
should correspond, respectively, to null shifts in
U
and
V
(see Fig.
27
), i.e.,

\displaystyle e^{iG_{+}s}\phi(X)e^{-iG_{+}s}=\phi(X_{s}),\quad X\equiv(U,V,x_{\perp}),\quad X_{s}=(U+s,V,x_{\perp}),\quad V\ll 1,

(412)

\displaystyle e^{iG_{-}s}\phi(X)e^{-iG_{-}s}=\phi(X_{s}),\quad X_{s}=(U,V+s,x_{\perp}),\quad U\ll 1\ .

(413)

This shows that flow generated by
G_{\pm}
can take an operator localized in the
R
region to the
F
and
P
regions.
We can also define

p=G_{-}-G_{+},\quad h=G_{+}+G_{-}

(414)

which generate, respectively, horizontal (spatial) and vertical (Kruskal-time) translations in the near-horizon region of Fig.
27
.

This discussion resolves the meeting-behind-the-horizon puzzle: although the system Hamiltonian
H=H_{R}+H_{L}
contains no interaction between the
R
and
L
CFTs, in the large-
N
limit the entanglement structure of the state gives rise to emergent Hamiltonians
G_{\pm}
. These couple the
R
and
L
systems and generate translations that move operators from the
R
and
L
regions behind the horizon.

Understanding how
G_{\pm}
act explicitly on a bulk local operator at general locations requires more elaborate technical machinery (see
LeuLiu21b
for details). Denoting
\Phi(X,s)\equiv e^{iG_{+}s}\phi(X)e^{-iG_{+}s}
, we
summarize here the explicit results for the BTZ black hole in AdS
3
:

1.

\Phi(X,s)
is not a bulk local operator, but may be understood as
\phi
smeared over a certain spacetime region.
With
X=(U_{0},V_{0},x_{\perp})
in terms of Kruskal null coordinates, we find

\Phi(X;s)
is supported only for
U<U_{0}+s
. In particular, for
s<s_{0}\equiv-U_{0}
,
\Phi(X;s)\in\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{R}
, while for
s>s_{0}
,
\Phi(X;s)
also involves operators in
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{L}
. This is exactly what one expects when
\Phi(X)
crosses the horizon along the
U
direction (see Fig.
29
(a)).

We stress that the existence of a finite
s_{0}
at which operators in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
“suddenly” appear in
\Phi(X,s)
is a highly nontrivial feature. In general, if
G_{+}
couples
R
and
L
operators in a nontrivial way, then the action of
e^{iG_{+}s}
will generate operators in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{M}}}_{L}
for
any nonzero

s
.

Figure 29
:
The boundary and horizon are suppressed in both Kruskal diagrams for clarity. (a) Translating
X=(U_{0},V_{0})
by
s>s_{0}\equiv-U_{0}
along the
U
direction moves the point into the interior of the horizon. There, the operator comes into causal contact with the
L
region, and consequently
\Phi(X;s)
must include operators in
\widetilde{{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{L}
. (b) Translation by
s>s_{12}\equiv-U_{1}+U_{2}
along the
U
direction moves
X_{1}=(U_{1},V_{1})
into the light cone of
X_{2}=(U_{2},V_{2})
, at which point the commutator (
415
) becomes nonzero.

2.

For general points
X_{1}\in R
and
X_{2}\in L
, we find that

[\Phi(X_{1},s),\phi(X_{2})]=0

(415)

for
s<s_{12}\equiv-U_{1}+U_{2}
, but the commutator becomes nonzero when
s>s_{12}
, precisely reproducing the casual structure expected from the black hole geometry. See Fig.
29
(b).

We see that while the evolution is nonlocal, it still respects the sharp causal structure.

3.

In the large
\Delta
limit, for operators that are smeared uniformly in the boundary spatial direction,
the transformation turns out to be local

\displaystyle\Phi(X,s)=\lambda_{X}(s)\phi(X_{s}),\quad X_{s}=(U_{s},V_{s}),

(416)

\displaystyle U_{s}=U_{0}+s,\quad V_{s}={V_{0}\over 1-sV_{0}},\quad\lambda_{X}=\sqrt{1-{2sV_{0}\over 1-U_{0}V_{0}}}\ .

(417)

The trajectories following from (
416
) are shown in
Fig.
30
. In particular, for
s\to s_{1}\equiv{1-U_{0}V_{0}\over 2V_{0}}
,
X_{s}
approaches the black hole singularity
63
63

63

More explicitly, at
s=s_{1}
we have
U_{s_{1}}={1+U_{0}V_{0}\over 2V_{0}}
,
V_{s_{1}}={2V_{0}\over 1+U_{0}V_{0}}

and
U_{s_{1}}V_{s_{1}}=1
.
, where
\lambda_{X}\to 0
.

Figure 30
:
The left plot gives trajectories of (
416
). The right plot gives constant
s
surfaces evolved from the
t=0
slice.
The orange dashed lines are the event horizons, black solid lines are the boundaries, while the red solid lines are the singularities.

Parallel statements can be made for evolution generated by
G_{-}
with the roles of
U
and
V
exchanged.
In the large
{{\Delta}}
limit, it can be shown that
G_{\pm}
and
K
form an
SL(2,R)
algebra
CuiLiu25
.
From the actions of
G_{\pm}
, we can also obtain the actions generated by (
414
).
For a black hole in AdS
2
, the algebra can be constructed explicitly for any operator and shown to be
SL(2,R)

LasLeu24
.

For
T<T_{\rm HP}
, the algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}_{R}
is type I, for which there does not exist the half-sided modular inclusion structure. This is consistent with the bulk picture that the two boundaries are disconnected.

VIII.3
Emergent spacetime connectivity: algebraic ER=EPR

In the thermofield double state, the bulk dual is given by the eternal black hole for
T>T_{\rm HP}
. For
T<T_{\rm HP}
, we find two disconnected spacetimes with entangled quantum fields. This has been elevated into a general ER=EPR principle
MalSus13
;
Van13
;
VerVer13a
: any
entangled quantum gravitational systems are connected by some kind of Einstein-Rosen (ER) bridge. However, there are
some significant weaknesses in the proposal:

•

When
T<T_{HP}
, with left and right systems have entanglement of order
{{\mathcal{O}}}(G_{N}^{0})
,
the ER “bridge” has to be some kind of quantum wormhole. However, no independent definition
of such a wormhole was given. Therefore, the proposal is more of a slogan than an equivalence in this case.

•

One may be tempted to refine the ER=EPR proposal by saying that there is an Einstein-Rosen (ER) bridge when there is

O(1/G_{N})
entanglement. However, a counterexample was identified in
EngFol22
in the context of an evaporating black hole, showing that the statement cannot hold in general. In particular, it was pointed out that at some time
t<t_{P}
, before the Page time, the black hole system is classically disconnected from the radiation, yet there exists
O(1/G_{N})
entanglement between them.

In Sec.
VI.3
, we saw that classical connectivity of two systems in the thermofield double state implies that the operator algebra of each system is a type III
1
von Neumann algebra in the large
N
limit. In other words, classical spacetime connectivity in this context requires a very specific entanglement structure (as characterized by type III
1
von Neumann algebras) in the corresponding boundary system. Furthermore, we discussed earlier in Sec.
VIII.2
that the type III
1
structure plays a crucial role for the emergence of an event horizon and associated causal structure.

Motivated by the weakness mentioned earlier and these observations, in
EngLiu23
, a more refined description of spacetime connectivity, called
algebraic ER=EPR
, was proposed, which associates bulk spacetime connectivity/disconnectivity with the operator algebraic structure of a quantum gravity system.
64
64

64

See
NogBan21
;
BanDor22
;
BanMor23
for a different algebraic perspective.

The operator algebraic structure not only includes information on the amount of entanglement, but also more importantly
the structure of entanglement
. The proposal gives a definition of quantum wormhole, which, as we will see momentarily, does not include the example of the TFD state at
T<T_{\rm HP}
.

More explicitly, the algebraic ER=EPR proposal for a two-sided state can be stated as follows
EngLiu23
.

Consider two entangled systems
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
in a pure semi-classical state
\lvert{\psi_{R_{1}R_{2}}}\rangle
